# Stage 1 detailed curriculum: 1.5 Probability

Blueprint work file, Stage 2 Phase 1. Draft 1, 6 October 2026; canon-gap P2 added on 7 October 2026 (Change log at the end).

**Source of truth.** `docs/curriculum.md` for stages, topics, hours and exit tests. This file expands item 1.5 Probability (250 h in `docs/curriculum.md`, "the priority"; 265 h with canon-gap P2, which the owner adopted on 7 October 2026) into courses, levels, lessons, homework, the topic exam and reading, and rebuilds the 1.5 exit test in the app.

> **Revision 2 of `docs/PLAN.md`, 7 October 2026: the Artifact publishing rule** (owner correction 5; added by the plan's second review round). These rows win over anything below that reads otherwise, the earlier review's rows included; content agents are briefed from them, and CI enforces them on the lesson files.
>

Every drill and every code item (K items, code screens, Labs, capstones, projects) runs in Pyodide 314.0.7 on the web app, or on CPython 3.12 on the learner's machine where this file says so; in the Artifact it shows its starter code, recorded output and the web-app link, and returns by result code (`docs/ASSESSMENT.md` 14.3). A lesson screen or quiz item whose widget runs learner code resolves as `read` in the Artifact and is finished in the web app (`docs/PLAN.md` 4.3, live-code screens). Lessons are otherwise unaffected: authored code runs at build time and ships as recorded output, and every number a widget shows comes from our own JavaScript models. **Built on.** `docs/design-research.md` sections 5, 8 and 9 (lesson anatomy, curriculum architecture, teaching style); `docs/blueprint/work/widgets-prob-stats-ts.md` (the probability widget catalogue, its working outline 1.5.1 to 1.5.9, which this file replaces as the lesson outline, its generators and its misconception register); `docs/blueprint/work/widgets-stage0.md` 15.1 (the id reconciliation); `docs/CONTENT-FORMAT.md` (ids, screen types, checks, verification, and section 10, the reference lesson `lsn.cond.shrinking-sample-space`); `docs/ASSESSMENT.md` (assessment ladder, the `finals` paper kind, exit parts, the weekly probability problems); `docs/AI-TUTOR.md` (Bertie's availability); `docs/blueprint/work/canon-gap.md` (the 1.5 resource canon, and proposal P2 on stochastic processes in continuous time, section G3, which levels Z4b and Z4c teach); the sibling files `docs/curriculum/stage0-precalc-computing.md` (0.3 course C, Counting, Chance and Data), `docs/curriculum/stage1-python-tools.md` (1.1 level A3, simulation) and `docs/curriculum/stage1-calculus.md` (1.3, whose densities, Gaussian integral, Jacobian and joint densities this file uses and does not repeat); the approved prototype's 1.5 course map (`prototype/js/data.js`); the owner's lesson-creator, maths-teaching, code-craft and project-forge skills.

**Tags.** [our choice] marks a design decision. [check] marks a resource detail (a chapter, section, lecture, problem or set number or title, a licence, an exam rule) that I recall but could not confirm from this environment; confirm it before the content cites it. [recalled] marks a fact from memory that I am confident of but did not verify here. [mode to add] marks a widget mode the catalogue does not yet list.

**Answers.** Every numeric answer in sections 2 (hooks that state a number), 3, 4 and 7 was recomputed on 6 October 2026 with Python 3.11, SymPy 1.14, NumPy 2.4 and SciPy 1.17: exact enumeration with `itertools` and `fractions` for every counting and discrete answer, exact linear solves for first-step and Markov chain answers, SymPy integration for densities and regions, SciPy for normal, $t$ and binomial tails, and brute-force path enumeration for the random-walk counts.

Continuous-time paper (`exm.prob.continuous`, canon-gap P2): 90 min, 8 questions, 50 marks; pass 70% |

1.5 exit (`1.5.exit`): the finals-style paper gates it in the app; two real Stat 110 past finals, each timed and self-marked at 70%, confirm it (section 4.3). The canon-gap P2 part, `1.5.continuous`: the continuous-time paper at 70%, with 70% on each of its three named problems (sections 4.3 and 4.4).

## 0. Scope, decisions and conventions

### 0.1 Decisions

| # | Decision | Chosen | Rejected | Reason |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| 1 | Unit of lesson time | **15 minutes per lesson, quiz included**, the unit of every other curriculum file | 10 or 12 minutes | One unit across the path keeps hours tables comparable; design research 5.8 allows 10 to 15 minutes of screens, and the quiz is part of the sitting |
| 2 | Reading spine | **Blitzstein and Hwang, Introduction to Probability, 2nd edition** (free online) with the **Harvard Stat 110 lectures** and **Stat 110 Strategic Practice** sets with solutions, as the curriculum says. Evans and Rosenthal (the curriculum's named alternative) and MIT RES.6-012 with Bertsekas and Tsitsiklis are the second-explanation column in section 5 | Ross, A First Course in Probability; Bertsekas and Tsitsiklis as the spine; Grimmett and Stirzaker as the spine | The curriculum names Blitzstein and Hwang, and the exit is two Stat 110 finals; the book, lectures, practice sets and exams are one family written by one teacher. Grimmett and Stirzaker is too steep as a first book; it is canon-gap P2's reading for Brownian motion (level Z4c), and Durrett's *Essentials of Stochastic Processes* (3rd edition) is P2's spine (section 5.5) |
| 3 | Course structure | **Five courses following the approved prototype's 1.5 course map**: Counting and Chance (N), Conditional Probability and Bayes (H), Random Variables and Their Distributions (V), Joint Distributions, Conditional Expectation and Limit Theorems (J), Markov Chains, Random Walks and Interview Problems (Z). Continuous distributions move from the prototype's fourth course into V, so each course is 22 to 46 lessons; course Z's 46, one above design research 8.2's guide of 45 (a guide, not a schema rule: `docs/CURRICULUM.md` 1, Shape), comes from canon-gap P2's two levels, which belong beside the chains and martingales they extend | Nine courses, one per section of the widget catalogue's working outline (1.5.1 to 1.5.9); one course per book chapter | The brief caps an item at five courses, and the prototype's five were approved in Stage 1. V and J follow the book's chapter order (3 to 6, then 7 to 10), so depth-first order holds |
| 4 | Boundary with Stage 0, 1.1 and 1.3 | **1.5 does not re-teach** 0.3 course C (permutations, combinations, four sampling cases, inclusion-exclusion counts, derangements, Pascal, GCSE probability with two dice, trees, the birthday 23), 1.1 level A3 (seeded Monte Carlo with standard errors, simulated gambler's ruin and price paths), or 1.3's densities, Gaussian integral, Jacobian and joint densities. It proves what those observed and asks the questions they could not (section 0.2) | Restarting probability from "a coin has two sides" | A learner arriving from 0.3 already counts and shrinks sample spaces; the level check (ASSESSMENT.md 2.7) lets one who tests out of a level skip it |
| 5 | Topics beyond the cover list | **Covered**: moment generating and probability generating functions, transformations and convolutions, order statistics, Beta and Gamma with the Beta-Gamma connections, Poisson processes, the multinomial and the multivariate normal, Markov, Chebyshev, Chernoff, Jensen and Cauchy-Schwarz inequalities, sampling distributions and the delta method. **Added for a top programme**: martingales and optional stopping (level Z4, 5 lessons), Metropolis sampling (one lesson), geometric probability (level Z5), optimal stopping and a Kelly preview (level Z6), the inspection paradox, covariance-matrix validity, streaks; and, from canon-gap P2 (adopted 7 October 2026), continuous-time Markov chains (level Z4b, 5 lessons) and Brownian motion with its martingales, exits and running maximum (level Z4c, 7 lessons) | Teaching the cover list only | The exit is two Stat 110 finals, which examine the first group routinely [inferred from the book's chapter list and the widget catalogue's finding 0.2]. The second group is what quant interviews and ML labs ask about that the cover list omits (canon-gap G3) |
| 6 | Rigour | **Stat 110 level**: every rule derived, by story, by indicators, by conditioning or by calculus; long-form proofs of inclusion-exclusion, Bayes' coherence, linearity, memorylessness, universality, the normal MGF, Eve's law, Jensen, the weak law, detailed balance, the ballot theorem, optional stopping for ruin, time shares from the jump chain and the reflection principle for Brownian motion. **No measure theory**: countable additivity is stated, sigma-algebras are named once and left to canon-gap P16; the existence of Brownian motion and its strong Markov property are stated, not proved | A measure-theoretic course (Grimmett and Stirzaker, Williams) | Stat 110 finals are not measure-theoretic; interviews ask "prove it" at this level. P16 holds the rest |
| 7 | Conventions | **Blitzstein and Hwang's**: $\mathrm{Geom}(p)$ counts failures before the first success (support $0, 1, 2, \dots$), $\mathrm{FS}(p)$ counts trials including the success, $\mathrm{NBin}(r, p)$ counts failures, $\mathrm{Expo}(\lambda)$ and $\mathrm{Gamma}(a, \lambda)$ use rates. The convention label is always shown on screen | Switching between books silently | The exit papers use these conventions; the green book (3.3) uses others, so the label stays visible (widget catalogue decision 13) |
| 8 | Exactness and simulation | **Every discrete answer is an exact fraction; simulations are never graded on their estimate**, only on a prediction made before running, or as a check that sits beside an exact answer | Grading a simulated proportion within a tolerance | Widget catalogue decisions 3 and 4: a tolerance on a random estimate fails honest learners on unlucky seeds or is too wide to teach |
| 9 | Quant and ML transfer | **Every level has at least one transfer screen**, and 34 lessons are built around a quant or ML use: coherence and the Dutch book (17), regime-weighted hit rates (26), fraud and classifier precision (27), skill or luck (32), naive Bayes (33), correlated defaults (37), Simpson across regimes (38), ruin (43), Fisher's test and capture-recapture (49), pricing by expectation (68), calibration by the probability integral transform (71), fat tails (72), order arrivals (74), the inspection paradox (75), skewness of option selling (77), Beta-binomial win rates (84), lognormal prices (86), portfolio variance (96, 97, 98), correlated normals (105), regression to the mean (104), the best predictor (111), Chernoff and learning bounds (113), Jensen and convexity (114), sample sizes for a backtest (116), the delta method for a Sharpe ratio (122), credit migration (132), Metropolis sampling (134), Kelly (154), streaks (155), the queue at a price level (160), stop-loss and target exits for a P&L (164, 166) | Pure probability, with applications left to 1.6 to 3.3 | The owner's goal is quant and ML-lab thinking; these are the places where probability is the job. Each is still taught from a problem the learner can attempt cold |
| 10 | Code | **One code drill in every homework set** (29), standard library only (`math`, `fractions`, `itertools`, and `zqtest`'s seeded generator in place of `random`), written to be `micropython-ok` and tagged by CI; NumPy, SciPy and matplotlib only in the Probability Lab project (web app) | NumPy drills throughout | The drills check probability, not library use; pure-Python drills run in the Artifact if WebAssembly is allowed there (open item 5) |
| 11 | Calculators | Lesson screens mark calculator use per screen; homework allows the in-app scientific calculator except on items marked "exact"; **the finals-style paper allows none** and accepts exact forms such as $\binom{10}{4}2^{-10}$, $1 - e^{-2}$ or $\Phi(1.2)$ | A calculator everywhere | Stat 110 answers are routinely left in closed form [recalled]; exact forms are what the exit tests |
| 12 | Topic exam | **The finals-style paper is the topic exam**, in the shape of a Stat 110 final: 180 minutes, 10 multi-part questions, 100 marks, 40 of them long-form, with a one-page formula card (the book's table of distributions) in place of the note sheets Stat 110 allows (the Fall 2011 final allowed four standard pages of notes written on both sides and no calculator; check the rules printed on the papers chosen) | No formula card; two shorter papers | One three-hour sitting matches ASSESSMENT.md's `finals` kind; the card keeps parity with the real paper's notes without letting the learner bring a page of worked answers |
| 13 | Held-back resources | **Stat 110 final exams are not used for practice** (two unseen ones confirm the exit and real papers are single-use); **Zhou's chapters 4 and 5 are not used** before 3.3 (its exit is "solved cold"); levels Z4b and Z4c reach chapter 5's difficulty with original problems only | Using past finals as homework | ASSESSMENT.md decision 1: real papers are finite. Stat 110 practice midterms, Strategic Practice and homework sets are free to use [check that practice midterms with solutions are public] |
| 14 | The reference lesson | **`lsn.cond.shrinking-sample-space` opens course H** (lesson 23), specified in full in CONTENT-FORMAT.md section 10 | Level 1, lesson 3, as in the prototype course map | 0.3 lesson 116 already taught conditioning on two dice; in 1.5 the reference lesson is the formal entry to conditioning, so it comes first. Ids do not depend on position |
| 15 | Sample lesson | **Lesson 1, "Which outcomes are equally likely?"** (`lsn.count.equally-likely-outcomes`), in full in section 7 | The reference lesson (already in full in CONTENT-FORMAT.md); linearity of expectation (lesson 52) | Every later answer depends on choosing a sample space whose outcomes are equally likely, and that is where the commonest wrong answers in probability start (Galileo's gamblers, the Bose-Einstein count, Bertrand's box, the two-children puzzle) |
| 16 | Display codes | **N, H, V, J, Z** (couNting, Hypotheses and evidence, random Variables, Joint behaviour, the zig-zag of a random walk) | Two-letter codes; the first letter of each course name | None clashes with Stage 0's letters or with 1.3's D, U, I, X, M; 1.1's file uses D and T too, so all Stage 1 letters are provisional (open item 1). Ids do not depend on them |
| 17 | Weekly probability problems | **Outside the 265 hours**: the curriculum calls them a habit "not on the map", and ASSESSMENT.md 8.3 takes 0.75 h a week from Main for them, starting the Monday after lesson 1 | Counting them inside 1.5 | They run to the end of the path; charging them to 1.5 would starve its lessons. Their Stage 1 bank is Mosteller and original problems (ASSESSMENT.md 10.3), and they draw only on patterns already taught |
| 18 | Exit hours | **Charged to the exams block** (22 h): two practice papers, the finals-style paper, two real Stat 110 finals and marking (20 h, `1.5.exit`); the continuous-time paper and its review (2 h, `1.5.continuous`) | A separate block | 1.5's 265 hours (the curriculum's 250 and P2's 15) must hold everything |

### 0.2 What 1.5 inherits, and does not repeat

| Topic | Taught in | 1.5 does |
|---|---|---|
| Arrangements, combinations, repeats, circular seating, restrictions, the four sampling cases with stars and bars | 0.3 level C1 (lessons 98 to 102) | Uses them at speed from lesson 3; lesson 4 adds the probability question 0.3 left out: which scheme's outcomes are equally likely (the Bose-Einstein error) |
| Inclusion-exclusion counts, pigeonhole, lattice paths, derangements | 0.3 level C2 (lessons 103 to 106) | Lesson 14 moves inclusion-exclusion to probabilities with its proof; lesson 15 redoes matching with intersections of events; lattice paths return in level Z3 as random walks |
| Pascal's triangle, the binomial theorem, combinatorial identities | 0.3 level C3 | Lessons 7 and 8 make story proofs the standard tool; lesson 48 turns the triangle into the binomial distribution |
| Equally likely outcomes with two dice, relative frequency, Venn diagrams, trees, conditioning by shrinking, the birthday 23 | 0.3 level C4 (lessons 112 to 117) | Lesson 1 lifts the question to three dice and to the choice of sample space; lesson 10 adds the birthmate count and the exponential approximation; lesson 23 formalises conditioning |
| Histograms with frequency density | 0.3 lesson 121 | Lesson 69 turns them into densities |
| Seeds, Monte Carlo with standard errors, vectorised simulation, simulated gambler's ruin, lognormal price paths, an expected payoff, SciPy as a checker | 1.1 level A3 (lessons 69 to 75) | Proves what A3 observed: the ruin formula (43), $\frac{\sigma}{\sqrt n}$ (97, 116), the lognormal mean (86); every simulation screen in 1.5 sits beside an exact answer |
| Densities, CDFs and expectations as integrals; geometric and Poisson series; Stirling; absolute convergence; double integrals; the Gaussian integral; the Jacobian; joint densities and the meeting problem | 1.3 lessons 72, 73, 92, 97, 98, 129, 133, 134, 135 | Uses them without re-deriving; lesson 92 sets a new region problem (expected distance) because 1.3 lesson 135 did the meeting problem |
| Matrices, eigenvalues, positive definiteness | 1.4 (before 1.5 in the curriculum's order) | Lessons 98, 124, 126 and 131 use them; Markov chains are linear algebra on probability vectors |
| Logarithms against underflow | 0.3 lesson 59 | Lesson 33 multiplies likelihood ratios as sums of logs |
| Python functions, loops, `fractions`, `itertools`; Git and pytest | 1.1, 1.2 | Every code drill; the Probability Lab lives in the learner's repository with tests (curriculum 1.2 exit) |

### 0.3 Conventions for every lesson

**Ids** follow CONTENT-FORMAT.md section 4.1. Courses: `crs.count`, `crs.cond`, `crs.rv`, `crs.joint`, `crs.markov`. Levels `lvl.<course>.<slug>`, lessons `lsn.<course>.<slug>`, homework `set.<course>.<level-slug>`, topic exam `exm.prob.finals`. Libraries use the topic namespace `prob`: `skl.prob.*`, `mc.prob.*`, `gen.prob.*`, `drl.prob.*`. The `#` column is the lesson's number within 1.5; display numbers are computed from position and never stored.

**Every lesson** runs the design research 8.3 sequence: a hook on a canvas, free manipulation, the name for what the learner just did, a ladder of 2 to 4 screens adding one constraint each, the trap, the rule stated as a description of the learner's own steps with at least one derived line the learner completes, a transfer screen where a true quant, statistics or ML context exists, the summary card, then a quiz of 3 to 5 items. From the owner's skills, every lesson also carries the "why this exists" frame, one common trap, at least one edge case, a worked example with a decision point where the naive route fails, and a motivated leap wherever a creative step appears (`derive` screens carry `leap: naive, fails, pointsTo`). Probability adds one rule [our choice]: **every lesson names its sample space or its equally likely units on screen** before any number is computed. Bertie, the tutor, is available on lesson screens after the first Check, after each quiz item is graded, on homework after submission, and never during the finals-style paper (AI-TUTOR.md 2.2 and 2.12). His probability guidance: never state an answer; the `secret()` block carries exact answers and any simulation's true parameters under the never-reveal rule (widget catalogue decision 9); prefer "which outcomes are equally likely here?", "what would you condition on?" and "can you write it as a sum of indicators?" to statements.

**Widgets** use the ids of widgets-prob-stats-ts.md section 12 (`prob-square`, `icon-array`, `contingency-table`, `odds-ledger`, `rv-mapper`, `dist-explorer`, `galton-board`, `convolver`, `indicator-board`, `trial-strip`, `generating-function`, `order-statistics`, `sampling-lab`, `joint-density`, `covariance-lab`, `gaussian-2d`, `cond-expectation`, `geo-prob`, `game-lab`, `first-step-solver`, `markov-chain`, `lattice-walk`, the canon-gap P2 widgets `ctmc-clock` and `brownian-path` (canon-gap 5.1; `docs/PLAN.md` 12), and the primitives `mass-editor`, `node-graph`, `chance-object`), the Stage 0 composites it extends (`outcome-grid`, `tree-diagram`, `trial-simulator`, `counting-slots`, `venn-diagram`, `pascal-triangle`, `binomial-expander`, `graph-explorer`, `iteration-cobweb`, `stat-chart`, `card-sort`), the calculus and linear algebra composites it extends (`function-mapping`, `accumulator`, `region-bounds`, `convexity-chord`, `taylor-builder`, `quadratic-form`, `transform-grid`, `filtration-tree`, `linear-dynamics`, `eigen-finder`, `matrix-multiply`, `path-simulator`, `least-squares-fit`, `series-explorer`), and the shared inputs under the names widgets-stage0.md 15.1 recommends (`choice`, `numeric-input`, `expression-input`, `derivation-steps`, `value-table`, `region-select`, `token-tray`, `sketch-pad`, `slider`, `matrix`, `code-cell`, `sim-runner`, `free-response`). Every lesson also uses the general inputs; they are listed only where they carry the idea. [mode to add] marks a mode the catalogue does not list; open item 4 collects them.

**Quiz item types** (CONTENT-FORMAT.md 3.4), the same letters as the other curriculum files: N numeric (exact fractions accepted), X expression, C single choice, M multi-select, O order the steps, S sort or match, I interval, P point or curve on a graph, R region selection, A matrix or vector entry, K code with tests. Long-form items appear in homework and exams, never in quizzes. Single and multiple choice stay at or below 40% of a course's quiz items (design research 10); section 0.4 gives the counts.

**Level review.** Every level ends with a Level review of 8 items at difficulty 2 to 4, interleaved, with up to 30% drawn from earlier levels of the same course (ASSESSMENT.md 2.4). The homework set follows (section 3).

**Columns.** Objective is one measurable sentence. Hook is the puzzle the lesson opens with, with its answer in brackets where it is a number. Trap is the misconception the trap screen is built around; Edge is the fringe case the lesson must show.

**Notation** (design research 9.4, extended for probability; the book's notation): $S$ for the sample space; $P(A \mid B)$; $A^c$ for complements; $I_A$ for the indicator of $A$; $X \sim \mathrm{Bin}(n, p)$ and the family names above, with $\mathcal N(\mu, \sigma^2)$ taking the variance second; $q = 1 - p$; $E$, $\mathrm{Var}$, $\mathrm{Cov}$, $\mathrm{Corr}$; $\Phi$ and $\varphi$ for the standard normal CDF and density; "independent and identically distributed" written out on first use in each course, then i.i.d.; transition matrices with rows as "from" and columns as "to"; distributions as row vectors.

## 1. Item overview

### 1.1 Purpose in a quant or ML career

- **It is the interview.** Trader and researcher interviews test probability harder than anything else (curriculum 1.5): expected values by indicators, conditioning puzzles, waiting times, random walks, optimal stopping and betting games, asked live, with the reasoning marked as it happens. The weekly problems from lesson 1 onwards and the 3.3 exit build on this item.
- **Every model is a probability model.** A regression assumes a conditional distribution; a classifier outputs one; a language model is a sequence of conditional distributions. Maximum likelihood, Bayesian updating, cross-entropy, calibration and uncertainty estimates are probability before they are statistics or machine learning (1.6, 2.1).
- **Prices are expectations.** An option's value is a discounted expectation; a market maker's quote is an expectation plus a spread for variance and adverse selection; a fair price process is a martingale (1.7, 3.2). Binary contracts that do not add up to 1 are an arbitrage (lesson 17).
- **Risk is a distribution, not a number.** Volatility is a standard deviation, value at risk a quantile, diversification a covariance calculation, a default model a dependence model. The failures that matter come from tails and from dependence assumed away (lessons 37, 72, 98, 105).
- **Markets run in continuous time.** Orders arrive and the queue at a price level grows and drains as a continuous-time chain; every pricing model starts from Brownian motion; and the chance that a drifting P&L reaches its target before its stop-loss is an optional-stopping calculation (levels Z4b and Z4c; Avellaneda and Stoikov in 3.1; 3.2).
- **Honest research needs it.** Whether a track record is skill or luck (32), whether a streak means anything (155), how many observations a backtest really has (97), and why the best of many strategies looks good by chance are probability questions first (3.1).
- **ML labs use the advanced end.** Concentration inequalities bound generalisation error (113), Markov chain Monte Carlo samples posteriors (134), Jensen's inequality gives the evidence lower bound, and the multivariate normal, conditional expectation as projection and the central limit theorem run through every model and every experiment.

### 1.2 Learning outcomes

At the end of 1.5 the learner can:

| # | Outcome | Measured by |
|---|---|---|
| PR-O1 | Build a sample space whose outcomes are equally likely, decide when the naive definition applies, and count with the multiplication rule, the sampling table, overcounting corrections and multinomial coefficients | 85% on fresh difficulty-3 items; exam Q1 |
| PR-O2 | Prove counting identities by story proofs, and replace counts by symmetry arguments | Long-form at 7 of 10 (homework N2) |
| PR-O3 | Derive consequences of the axioms (complements, the Fréchet bounds, inclusion-exclusion) and apply them to matching, occupancy and countably infinite sample spaces; detect and exploit incoherent prices | 85% on difficulty-3 items; long-form at 7 of 10 (homework N3) |
| PR-O4 | Compute conditional probabilities and apply the multiplication rule, total probability and Bayes' rule in probability, natural-frequency and odds forms, including sequential evidence and extra conditioning | 85% on difficulty-3 items; exam Q2 at 7 of 10 |
| PR-O5 | Decide independence, pairwise and mutual independence and conditional independence, and explain Simpson's paradox, explaining away and correlated defaults | 85% on difficulty-3 items; long-form at 7 of 10 (homework H3) |
| PR-O6 | Solve the classic conditioning puzzles (Monty Hall and its variants, two children, Bertrand's box, the prosecutor's fallacy) and recursive problems by first-step analysis | 85% on difficulty-3 items |
| PR-O7 | Treat random variables as functions, compute PMFs and CDFs, and recognise the stories of the Bernoulli, binomial, hypergeometric, discrete uniform, geometric, first-success, negative binomial and Poisson distributions | 90% on story-recognition items; exam Q3 |
| PR-O8 | Compute expectations by definition, linearity, indicators, LOTUS and tail sums, and variances of counts of dependent events | 85% on difficulty-3 items; exam Q4 at 7 of 10 |
| PR-O9 | Solve waiting-time problems (geometric waits, patterns, coupon collecting, records) and approximate rare-event counts by the Poisson | 85% on difficulty-3 items |
| PR-O10 | Use PDFs, CDFs and quantiles, universality of the uniform, the normal and the exponential, and Poisson processes with merging, thinning and the inspection paradox | 85% on difficulty-3 items; exam Q5 |
| PR-O11 | Use moments, skewness and kurtosis, and moment and probability generating functions for moments, sums, branching processes and dice | 85% on difficulty-3 items; exam Q6 |
| PR-O12 | Use the Gamma and Beta families, conjugate Beta-binomial updating, Pólya's urn, the lognormal, $\chi^2$, Student $t$ and Cauchy | 85% on difficulty-3 items |
| PR-O13 | Work with joint, marginal and conditional distributions, discrete and continuous, independence of random variables, 2D LOTUS and the multinomial | 85% on difficulty-3 items; exam Q7 |
| PR-O14 | Compute covariance and correlation, variances of sums and portfolios, and test covariance matrices for validity | 85% on difficulty-3 items |
| PR-O15 | Transform random variables in one and two dimensions, compute convolutions and order statistics, use the Beta-Gamma connections and the bivariate normal | 85% on difficulty-3 items; long-form at 7 of 10 (homework J3) |
| PR-O16 | Compute conditional expectations given events and random variables, use Adam's and Eve's laws, random sums and conditioning for waiting times, and explain $E[Y \mid X]$ as the best predictor | Exam Q8 at 7 of 10 |
| PR-O17 | Prove and apply the Markov, Chebyshev, Chernoff, Jensen and Cauchy-Schwarz inequalities | Long-form at 7 of 10 (homework J5) |
| PR-O18 | Prove the weak law of large numbers, apply the central limit theorem with continuity corrections, the sampling distributions of the mean and variance, and the delta method, and say when the laws fail | Exam Q9 at 7 of 10 |
| PR-O19 | Analyse Markov chains: transition matrices, classification, stationary distributions, convergence and periodicity, reversibility, absorbing chains, walks on graphs, PageRank and Metropolis sampling | 85% on difficulty-3 items; exam Q10 |
| PR-O20 | Solve random-walk problems with reflection, the ballot theorem and Catalan numbers, and explain recurrence and the scaling limit | 85% on difficulty-3 items; long-form at 7 of 10 (homework Z3) |
| PR-O21 | Use martingales and optional stopping with their conditions, Wald's identity and the pattern martingale; solve geometric probability and optimal stopping problems; compute and interpret Kelly growth | 85% on difficulty-3 items; long-form at 7 of 10 (homeworks Z4 to Z6) |
| PR-O22 | Pass the 1.5 exit | `exm.prob.finals` at 70%; two real Stat 110 finals at 70%, each self-marked (section 4.3); for `1.5.continuous`, `exm.prob.continuous` at 70% with 70% on each of Q1 to Q3 (section 4.4) |
| PR-O23 | Model continuous-time Markov chains by holding rates and a jump chain or by a generator; compute two-state transition probabilities, stationary distributions by flow balance, birth-death and queue distributions, and expected hitting times | 85% on difficulty-3 items; `exm.prob.continuous` Q1 at 70% |
| PR-O24 | Use Brownian motion's definition, covariance and martingales; solve exit problems with and without drift by optional stopping, with its conditions; and find the running maximum and first-passage laws by reflection | Long-form at 7 of 10 (homework Z4c); `exm.prob.continuous` Q2 and Q3 at 70% |

### 1.3 Prerequisites

- **0.3 Precalculus complete**, especially course C (levels C1 to C4: counting, the binomial theorem, GCSE probability), level G5 (geometric series), and levels R1 and R2 (proof by contradiction and induction).
- **1.1 Python** for the code drills (functions, loops, `fractions`, `itertools`, seeded simulation from level A3); **1.2 Tools** for the Probability Lab repository and its tests.
- **1.3 Calculus**: integration and improper integrals (level I4: lessons 72 and 73 on densities), series (X1, X2: geometric and exponential series, absolute convergence), multiple integrals and the Jacobian (M5, M6: lessons 129 to 135), and first-order linear differential equations by an integrating factor (level I6), which lesson 158 uses for the two-state chain. canon-gap P8 (differential equations) was not adopted, so 1.5 needs nothing beyond I6: larger chains get $e^{tQ}$ from 1.4's diagonalisation.
- **1.4 Linear algebra**: matrix products, inverses, eigenvalues and positive definite matrices (Markov chains, covariance matrices, the multivariate normal), and diagonalisation (lesson 158's $e^{tQ}$).
- **Alongside:** 1.7 Finance basics runs beside 1.3 to 1.6 (curriculum); the finance transfer screens are self-contained. The Daily 10 continues, and the weekly probability problems begin (decision 17).

**Feeds.** 1.6 (likelihood, sampling distributions, Bayesian updating, the delta method, multiple testing), 1.7 (expectations as prices, lognormal prices, binomial and trinomial trees, no-arbitrage), 2.1 (classifiers, calibration, concentration, Bayesian ML, MCMC), 2.2 (stationarity, autocorrelation as covariance, Markov models), 2.3 (backtest statistics, position sizing, simulation), 3.1 (skill against luck, false discoveries), 3.2 (random walks to Brownian motion, martingales; level BR3 opens with recaps of Z4c once its file applies P2, open item 8), 3.3 (every interview book on the list; level PZ6 likewise opens with recaps of Z4c, and Zhou's chapter 5 is worked cold there).

### 1.4 Hours budget (265 h)

The curriculum's 250 hours plus canon-gap P2's 15 (adopted 7 October 2026: levels Z4b and Z4c, their homework, the continuous-time paper and their reading).

| Block | What | Hours | Share |
|---|---|---|---|
| In-app lessons | 168 lessons at 15 minutes, quiz included (12 of them from P2) | 42.0 | 15.8% |
| In-app practice and redo | 29 level reviews at about 15 minutes (7.25 h); generated practice (10.0 h); the Redo queue at 7 and 30 days (11.5 h). P2's share: 2 reviews (0.5 h), practice 0.5 h, Redo 0.5 h | 28.75 | 10.8% |
| Homework | Estimated by difficulty (d3 10 minutes, d4 20, d5 40, long-form 30): 9 sets of 8 items at about 3 hours (27.0 h); 20 sets of 10 items at about 3 hours 30 minutes (70.0 h). P2's share: Z4b (8 items, 3.0 h) and Z4c (10 items, 3.5 h) | 97.0 | 36.6% |
| Exams | Two practice papers of 3 hours (a Stat 110 rehearsal and the finals shape, 6.0 h); the finals-style paper (3.0 h); two real Stat 110 finals, timed (6.0 h; the Fall 2011 final was 3 hours, check the papers chosen); marking and review of all five (5.0 h); the continuous-time paper (1.5 h) and its review (0.5 h) | 22.0 | 8.3% |
| External reading and lectures | Videos and reading 25.5 h: Stat 110 lectures pull-based, watched when a level review falls below 70% (about 6.0 h expected of the 22.0 h for all 33) [check lengths]; targeted reading in Blitzstein and Hwang (15.0 h); six 3Blue1Brown probability videos (1.75 h); other targeted reading: Grinstead and Snell, MacKay, short papers (1.5 h); P2 reading: Durrett chapters 4 and 5 (0.75 h) and Grimmett and Stirzaker chapter 13 (0.5 h). External problems 30.25 h: Stat 110 Strategic Practice sets (13.0 h), Blitzstein and Hwang exercises with online solutions (14.5 h), Grinstead and Snell Markov chain and random walk exercises (2.0 h), MIT 6.262's problem set 10 on Markov processes, which has posted solutions (two of its four exercises, 0.75 h). Mosteller and the Jane Street guide are 3.3's, worked cold | 55.75 | 21.0% |
| Projects or drills | The Probability Lab project in four parts (16.0 h, section 3.2); 7 extra code drills of 30 minutes beyond homework (3.5 h) | 19.5 | 7.4% |
| **Total** | | **265.0** | 100% |

Solving time (everything except the 25.5 hours of videos and reading, and the non-task screens of lessons) is about 232 hours, 88%, above the curriculum's 60% floor (rule 2). Lectures marked optional in section 5 are outside the budget, and so are the MIT 6.262 and 18.S096 lectures offered to Z4b and Z4c as pull-based alternatives and the weekly probability problems (decision 17: about 0.75 h a week from Main).

## 2. Courses

Five courses, 29 levels, 168 lessons. Each level is 4 to 7 lessons, then a Level review (8 items) and a homework set (section 3).

### Course N: Counting and Chance (`crs.count`, 4 levels, 22 lessons)

0.3 taught the counting tools. This course asks the question 0.3 could not: which outcomes are equally likely, and what to do when none are. It builds sample spaces from labelled outcomes, turns counting into proof (story proofs, symmetry), replaces "favourable over total" by the axioms when outcomes are unequal or infinitely many, and ends with the first interview patterns: complements, the problem of points, occupancy, permutation cycles, and the habit of checking an answer three ways. Blitzstein and Hwang chapter 1; Stat 110 lectures 1 to 3 [check 2 and 3].

**Level N1: Outcomes and the naive definition** (`lvl.count.naive`, 6 lessons)

| # | Lesson | Objective | Hook | Widgets | Trap and edge case | Quiz |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| 1 | `lsn.count.equally-likely-outcomes` Which outcomes are equally likely? | Build a sample space of labelled outcomes for several dice, compute probabilities with the naive definition $P(A) = \frac{\lvert A \rvert}{\lvert S \rvert}$, and state when it applies (finitely many, equally likely outcomes) | Gamblers noticed that three dice total 10 more often than 9, although each total can be made from six combinations of faces. Who is right, the gamblers or the count? ($\frac{27}{216}$ against $\frac{25}{216}$) | `token-tray`, `counting-slots` (three coloured slots), `value-table`, `outcome-grid` (three-dice layers), `trial-simulator`, `tree-diagram` | Trap: the six combinations treated as equally likely outcomes. Edge: dice that look identical behave as labelled; a loaded die or an unlimited number of tosses breaks the naive definition | N, N, C, M, N (section 7) |
| 2 | `lsn.count.events-as-sets` Events as sets | Translate English descriptions of events into unions, intersections and complements of subsets of $S$, use De Morgan's laws, and count the outcomes in each | Three loans each default or not. Write "at most one defaults" and "not all the same" as sets of the 8 outcomes. How many outcomes are in both? (3: exactly one default) | `outcome-grid` (list layout, event chips), `venn-diagram`, `region-select` | Trap: "at most one" read as "exactly one"; $(A \cup B)^c$ taken as $A^c \cup B^c$. Edge: the empty set and $S$ are events; an outcome $s$ and the event $\{s\}$ are different objects | S, R, M, C |
| 3 | `lsn.count.multiplication-rule` The multiplication rule, with the stages chosen well | Count by sequential choices, ordering the stages so that the number of options at each stage does not depend on earlier choices, and split into cases and add when it does | Ten runners: how many podiums (gold, silver, bronze) are there in which Ann does not take silver? (648) | `counting-slots`, `tree-diagram` (count mode) | Trap: choosing gold first, where the number of silver options depends on whether Ann took gold, then multiplying a guess such as $10 \times 8 \times 8$. Edge: the options may differ from branch to branch as long as their number does not; silver first gives $9 \times 9 \times 8$ | N, N, O, C |
| 4 | `lsn.count.sampling-schemes` Sampling schemes, and which outcomes they make equally likely | Count samples of size $k$ from $n$ in the four schemes ($n^k$, $\frac{n!}{(n - k)!}$, $\binom nk$, $\binom{n + k - 1}k$) and decide which scheme's outcomes are equally likely when the sample is drawn at random | Draw 2 numbers from $\{1, 2, 3, 4\}$ with replacement and record the pair without order. There are 10 possible records. Is each $\frac1{10}$? (No: a double is $\frac1{16}$, a mixed pair $\frac2{16}$) | `card-sort` (four bins), `counting-slots` (enumerate, probability toggle) | Trap: unordered samples with replacement treated as equally likely (the Bose-Einstein error); $\frac{n^k}{k!}$ offered as their count. Edge: unordered samples without replacement are equally likely, which is why $\binom nk$ works in card problems | S, N, N, C |
| 5 | `lsn.count.overcounting` Adjusting for overcounting | Count by an ordered procedure and divide by the number of times each unordered result was counted, after checking that every result is overcounted equally | Ten players split into two teams of five. Is the number of splits $\binom{10}5 = 252$? (126) | `counting-slots` (collapse mode, circular) | Trap: unlabelled groups counted as labelled, and the reverse. Edge: dividing works only when every result is overcounted equally: 12 people into three unlabelled groups of four is $\frac{12!}{4!^3\,3!} = 5{,}775$, but groups of sizes 3, 3 and 6 divide by $2!$ only | N, N, C, N |
| 6 | `lsn.count.card-hands` Counting card hands | Compute poker-hand probabilities by building each hand as a sequence of choices that produces every hand exactly once | Five cards from a shuffled deck: chance of a full house? Of two pair? ($\frac{3{,}744}{2{,}598{,}960} \approx 0.0014$; $\frac{123{,}552}{2{,}598{,}960} \approx 0.0475$) | `counting-slots`, `chance-object` (cards) | Trap: two pair counted as "rank of the first pair, then rank of the second", which counts each hand twice (247,104). Edge: ordered hands on top and ordered hands below give the same probability, provided top and bottom agree | N, N, C, N |

Transfer screens: lesson 1's labelled outcomes are the paths of a three-step trinomial tree (1.7); lesson 3 counts sequences of fills in an order book; lesson 6's "every object exactly once" check is the test behind every exact enumerator in the Probability Lab.

Then the Level review and homework `set.count.naive`.

**Level N2: Stories and symmetry** (`lvl.count.stories`, 5 lessons)

| # | Lesson | Objective | Hook | Widgets | Trap and edge case | Quiz |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| 7 | `lsn.count.story-proofs` Story proofs | Prove binomial identities by counting one set in two ways, including $\binom nk = \binom n{n - k}$ and $k\binom nk = n\binom{n - 1}{k - 1}$ | A club of $n$ chooses a committee of $k$ with a chair. Count by choosing the committee first, then by choosing the chair first. What identity falls out? | `counting-slots` (procedure comparison), `derivation-steps` (justify mode) | Trap: checking a few rows of Pascal's triangle offered as proof. Edge: the identity holds for $k > n$ (both sides 0) and the story still works there | O, X, M, C |
| 8 | `lsn.count.vandermonde` Vandermonde's identity and splitting by cases | Prove $\binom{m + n}k = \sum_j\binom mj\binom n{k - j}$ by splitting on a feature of the chosen set, and derive $\sum_k\binom nk^2 = \binom{2n}n$ | A committee of 5 is chosen from 6 women and 4 men. Count by the number of women, then compare with $\binom{10}5$ (both 252) | `pascal-triangle`, `counting-slots`, `derivation-steps` | Trap: cases that overlap or that miss some sets. Edge: terms with $j > m$ are 0, so the sum can run over every $j$ | N, X, O, C |
| 9 | `lsn.count.multinomial` Multinomial coefficients | Count assignments of $n$ labelled objects into labelled groups of sizes $n_1, \dots, n_r$ as $\frac{n!}{n_1!\cdots n_r!}$, and use it for card deals and words with repeated letters | A bridge deal gives 13 cards to each of four players. Chance that each player gets exactly one ace? ($\frac{2{,}197}{20{,}825} \approx 0.105$) | `counting-slots` (groups), `chance-object` (cards) | Trap: dividing by $4!$ although the players are labelled (North, East, South, West). Edge: MISSISSIPPI is a multinomial count (34,650); there are about $5.4 \times 10^{28}$ bridge deals | N, X, N, C |
| 10 | `lsn.count.birthday` The birthday problem and its approximation | Compute $P(\text{shared birthday}) = 1 - \prod_{j=0}^{k-1}\frac{365 - j}{365}$, approximate it by $1 - e^{-k(k - 1)/730}$ from the number of pairs, and tell "any pair" from "a pair involving me" | 0.3 showed 23 people give better than even odds of a shared birthday. How many people do you need for an even chance that someone shares your birthday? (253) | `graph-explorer` (integer $k$), `trial-simulator` (birthday), `indicator-board` (pairs) | Trap: linear reasoning ($\frac k{365}$, or "half of 365, so 183"). Edge: a hash table with $N$ slots sees its first collision near $\sqrt N$ entries, the same mathematics | N, N, C, N |
| 11 | `lsn.count.symmetry` Symmetry arguments | Replace a count by symmetry: positions, orders or labels the problem cannot tell apart have the same probability | Shuffle a deck and deal it face up. Chance that the tenth card is the ace of spades? ($\frac1{52}$) | `indicator-board` (card positions), `choice` | Trap: conditioning on the first nine cards and drowning in cases. Edge: the card straight after the first ace is as likely to be the ace of spades as the two of clubs, $\frac1{52}$ each (homework N2) | N, C, N, M |

Transfer screens: lesson 10's pair count sizes identifiers and hash keys (1.8); lesson 11's symmetry says every ordering of exchangeable data is equally likely under a null hypothesis, the basis of permutation tests (1.6).

Then the Level review and homework `set.count.stories`.

**Level N3: The axioms of probability** (`lvl.count.axioms`, 6 lessons)

| # | Lesson | Objective | Hook | Widgets | Trap and edge case | Quiz |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| 12 | `lsn.count.axioms` Probability without equally likely outcomes | State the axioms ($P(\emptyset) = 0$, $P(S) = 1$, countable additivity), check whether an assignment of probabilities is valid, and compute event probabilities by adding outcome masses | A die is loaded so that a six is twice as likely as each other face. Chance of an even number? ($\frac47$) | `mass-editor`, `venn-diagram` (probability mode) | Trap: forgetting to rescale (giving the six $\frac26$). Edge: no probability can choose a positive whole number "uniformly at random": countable additivity would force every mass to 0 | N, C, N, M |
| 13 | `lsn.count.consequences` What the axioms imply | Derive $P(A^c) = 1 - P(A)$, monotonicity, $P(A \cup B) = P(A) + P(B) - P(A \cap B)$ and Boole's inequality from the axioms, and find the range of $P(A \cap B)$ allowed by $P(A)$ and $P(B)$ | $P(A) = 0.7$ and $P(B) = 0.5$. How small and how large can $P(A \cap B)$ be? ($[0.2, 0.5]$) | `prob-square` (events), `venn-diagram`, `derivation-steps` | Trap: $P(A \cap B) = 0.35$, which assumes independence nobody stated. Edge: both ends are attained; the lower end is $\max(0, P(A) + P(B) - 1)$ | I, N, O, C |
| 14 | `lsn.count.inclusion-exclusion` Inclusion-exclusion for probabilities | Apply inclusion-exclusion to $n$ events, justify it by counting how often each outcome is included, and turn "every face appears" into "no face is missing" | Roll a die 10 times. Chance that all six faces appear? ($\frac{38{,}045}{139{,}968} \approx 0.272$) | `venn-diagram` (three sets), `derivation-steps`, `indicator-board` | Trap: treating the events "face $i$ is missing" as disjoint and adding them. Edge: an outcome in $j$ of the events is counted $\sum_{i \ge 1}(-1)^{i+1}\binom ji = 1$ time; stopping the alternating sum early gives upper and lower bounds (Bonferroni) | N, X, O, C |
| 15 | `lsn.count.matching` De Montmort's matching problem | Compute $P(\text{at least one match})$ for a random permutation by inclusion-exclusion with $P(A_{i_1} \cap \dots \cap A_{i_k}) = \frac{(n - k)!}{n!}$, and see the limit $1 - \frac1e$ | Cards numbered 1 to 52 are shuffled. Chance that at least one card lands in the position of its number? (about 0.632) | `trial-simulator` (permutation), `indicator-board` (fixed points), `derivation-steps` | Trap: "52 chances, so almost certain"; or $1 - \left(\frac{51}{52}\right)^{52} \approx 0.636$ from assumed independence, close by luck and wrong by reasoning. Edge: the answer barely changes once $n \ge 6$ | N, X, C, N |
| 16 | `lsn.count.infinite-spaces` Countably infinite sample spaces | Compute probabilities on countably infinite spaces by summing geometric series and by a first-step symmetry argument | Ann and Bob take turns rolling a die, Ann first; the first to roll a six wins. Ann's chance? ($\frac6{11}$) | `trial-strip`, `series-explorer`, `first-step-solver` | Trap: $\frac12$, "they take turns, so it is fair". Edge: "nobody ever rolls a six" has probability 0 yet is a possible outcome: probability 0 does not mean impossible | N, N, C, N |
| 17 | `lsn.count.coherence` Prices as probabilities: coherence and the Dutch book | Read the prices of binary contracts as probabilities, check them against the axioms, and build the trade that profits for certain when they fail | A contract paying £1 if the central bank raises rates trades at 62p; one paying £1 if it does not trades at 41p. What do you trade, and what do you lock in? (Sell both: take £1.03, pay out exactly £1, keep 3p) | `mass-editor`, `odds-ledger`, `choice` | Trap: reading 62p as the market's probability without removing the 3p overround. Edge: a contract on "a rise of at least 0.5%" priced above one on "a rise of at least 0.25%" breaks monotonicity, another free profit | N, C, M, N |

Transfer screens: lesson 17 is the no-arbitrage idea behind every derivative price (1.7) and every bookmaker's margin (3.3); lesson 14 counts the chance that a set of stress scenarios covers every state.

Then the Level review and homework `set.count.axioms`.

**Level N4: First interview patterns** (`lvl.count.patterns`, 5 lessons)

| # | Lesson | Objective | Hook | Widgets | Trap and edge case | Quiz |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| 18 | `lsn.count.complements` At least one: complements, de Méré and Newton-Pepys | Compute "at least one" and "at least $m$" probabilities through complements and counts of sequences, and explain why multiplying one chance by the number of tries fails | Samuel Pepys asked Isaac Newton which is most likely: at least one six with 6 dice, at least two with 12, or at least three with 18. (0.665, 0.619, 0.597: the first) | `trial-simulator`, `counting-slots`, `value-table` | Trap: $4 \times \frac16 = \frac23$ for "at least one six in four rolls", adding chances of events that can happen together; "more dice, same ratio, same chance". Edge: de Méré's two bets: one six in 4 rolls is 0.518, a double six in 24 rolls of two dice is 0.491 | N, N, C, N |
| 19 | `lsn.count.problem-of-points` The problem of points | Divide the stake of an interrupted game by each player's chance of winning, imagining the remaining rounds all played so that every sequence is equally likely | A first-to-5 coin-tossing match stops with Ann on 3 wins and Bob on 2. How should the £100 pot be split? (£68.75 to Ann) | `tree-diagram`, `counting-slots`, `trial-simulator` | Trap: splitting by the score (3 to 2, £60), or 50-50. Edge: playing on after the winner is settled changes no one's chance but makes the outcomes equally likely: the creative step of Pascal and Fermat | N, X, C, N |
| 20 | `lsn.count.occupancy` Balls in boxes | Compute occupancy probabilities (empty boxes, every box filled, exactly $j$ filled) for balls dropped at random, counting labelled placements | Drop 4 balls at random into 4 boxes. Chance that exactly one box stays empty? ($\frac9{16}$) | `counting-slots`, `trial-simulator` (urn), `indicator-board` | Trap: the 35 stars-and-bars configurations treated as equally likely (lesson 4 again). Edge: the expected number of empty boxes is $n\left(1 - \frac1n\right)^n \approx \frac ne$, a preview of indicators (lesson 53) | N, N, C, N |
| 21 | `lsn.count.permutation-cycles` Cycles of a random permutation: the 100 prisoners | Count permutations with a cycle longer than $\frac n2$ ($\frac{n!}k$ for each length $k > \frac n2$) and use cycles to explain why a coordinated strategy beats independent guessing | 100 prisoners must each find their own number among 100 boxes, opening 50 each. Guessing, all succeed with chance $2^{-100}$. Following the numbers succeeds about 31% of the time. How? ($1 - \sum_{k=51}^{100}\frac1k \approx 0.312$) | `trial-simulator` (permutation), `node-graph` (cycle view), `derivation-steps` | Trap: each prisoner succeeds with chance $\frac12$, so all do with chance $2^{-100}$: independence assumed where the strategy correlates the events. Edge: a permutation has at most one cycle longer than $\frac n2$, which is why the counts add | N, X, C, N |
| 22 | `lsn.count.three-checks` Checking an answer three ways | Verify a probability by exact enumeration of small cases, by a seeded simulation with a standard error, and by a formula, and say which of the three is proof | Chance that a random 4-digit PIN has a repeated digit? ($\frac{62}{125} = 0.496$) | `code-cell`, `sim-runner`, `trial-simulator` | Trap: trusting a simulation that cannot tell two candidate answers apart: 0.496 and 0.5 need about 60,000 trials before two standard errors separate them. Edge: enumeration proves only the cases enumerated; the formula generalises; the simulation catches mistakes in both | K, N, C, N |

Transfer screens: lesson 21 shows why decisions driven by one shared model fail together; lesson 22 is the working quant's habit: code checks the maths and the maths checks the code.

Then the Level review and homework `set.count.patterns`.

### Course H: Conditional Probability and Bayes (`crs.cond`, 4 levels, 22 lessons)

How a probability changes when you learn something, and how to avoid the mistakes that experts make with it. The course opens with the reference lesson (CONTENT-FORMAT.md section 10), builds the multiplication rule, total probability and Bayes' rule from the probability square, turns Bayes into odds and sequential evidence, separates independence from its look-alikes, and ends with the puzzles that fool experts and first-step analysis. Blitzstein and Hwang chapter 2; Stat 110 lectures 4 to 7 [check].

**Level H1: Updating on evidence** (`lvl.cond.updating`, 5 lessons)

| # | Lesson | Objective | Hook | Widgets | Trap and edge case | Quiz |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| 23 | `lsn.cond.shrinking-sample-space` Shrinking the sample space | Compute $P(A \mid B)$ by restricting to the outcomes in $B$, state $P(A \mid B) = \frac{P(A \cap B)}{P(B)}$, and say why it is undefined when $P(B) = 0$ | Roll two dice. Given that at least one shows a 5, chance the total is 10? ($\frac1{11}$) | `outcome-grid` (condition, shrink), `trial-simulator`, `derivation-steps` | Trap: "at least one 5" read as "the first die is a 5" ($\frac16$). Edge: the double (5, 5) meets the condition through both dice but is one outcome | C, N, N, N (as in CONTENT-FORMAT.md section 10) |
| 24 | `lsn.cond.area-ratio` Conditioning as rescaling | Read $P(A \mid B)$ as the share of $B$'s area that $A$ covers, and show that $P(\cdot \mid B)$ obeys the axioms, so complements and unions work inside the condition | In the unit square $A$ covers 30%, $B$ 40% and their overlap 20%. After learning $B$, how much of the new whole is $A$? (0.5) | `prob-square` (condition) | Trap: 0.2, the overlap's share of the old whole. Edge: with an overlap of 12% the answer is 0.3, unchanged: knowing $B$ tells you nothing (independence, lesson 34) | N, N, M, N |
| 25 | `lsn.cond.multiplication-trees` The multiplication rule and trees | Compute probabilities of sequences with $P(A_1 \cap \dots \cap A_n) = P(A_1)P(A_2 \mid A_1)\cdots$, matching each leaf of a tree to a rectangle of the square | Deal cards one at a time. Chance that the first ace is the fifth card? ($\frac{48}{52}\cdot\frac{47}{51}\cdot\frac{46}{50}\cdot\frac{45}{49}\cdot\frac4{48} \approx 0.060$) | `tree-diagram`, `prob-square` (events) | Trap: $\frac4{52}$ as the last factor, ignoring the cards already gone; adding along a branch. Edge: any order of conditioning gives the same joint probability, so choose the order that makes each factor easy | N, N, O, C |
| 26 | `lsn.cond.total-probability` The law of total probability | Compute $P(B) = \sum_iP(B \mid A_i)P(A_i)$ over a partition as a weighted average of conditional probabilities | A signal is right 70% of the time in calm markets and 45% in volatile ones; markets are volatile 30% of the time. Overall hit rate? (62.5%) | `prob-square` (partition), `tree-diagram` | Trap: the plain average, 57.5%. Edge: the pieces must cover everything without overlapping; the overall rate always lies between the smallest and largest conditional rate | N, N, X, M |
| 27 | `lsn.cond.bayes-natural-frequencies` Bayes' rule with natural frequencies | Derive $P(A \mid B) = \frac{P(B \mid A)P(A)}{P(B)}$ from the definition used twice, and compute it with counts out of 1,000 or 10,000 cases | One trade in 200 is fraudulent. A model flags 95% of frauds and 3% of honest trades. A trade is flagged: chance it is fraud? (about 13.7%) | `icon-array` (Bayes), `prob-square` (Bayes) | Trap: 95%, the share of frauds flagged, read as the share of flags that are frauds. Edge: if half of all trades were frauds the same flag would mean 96.9%: the posterior depends on the base rate | N, N, C, N |

Transfer screens: lesson 26 is regime-weighted performance; lesson 27 is the precision of a classifier (2.1) and the false-alarm problem of every surveillance system.

Then the Level review and homework `set.cond.updating`.

**Level H2: Bayes' rule** (`lvl.cond.bayes`, 6 lessons)

| # | Lesson | Objective | Hook | Widgets | Trap and edge case | Quiz |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| 28 | `lsn.cond.flip-the-tree` Turning the condition around | Distinguish $P(A \mid B)$ from $P(B \mid A)$, rebuild a tree split by the evidence first, and show the two are equal only when $P(A) = P(B)$ | 90% of funds that made money were levered (borrowed to invest). Does being levered make a fund likely to make money? With 30% of funds profitable and 85% of the rest also levered, $P(\text{profitable} \mid \text{levered})$ is 31.2% | `tree-diagram` (flip), `contingency-table` | Trap: reading 90% as $P(\text{profit} \mid \text{levered})$. Edge: evidence that nearly everyone shows, winners or not, barely moves the probability | N, S, N, M |
| 29 | `lsn.cond.second-test` Updating twice | Use the posterior after one piece of evidence as the prior for the next, under conditional independence given the hypothesis | A disease affects 1%; a test catches 99% of cases and falsely flags 5% of healthy people. After one positive the chance is $\frac16$. A second, independent test is also positive. Now? (about 79.8%) | `prob-square` (evidence sequence), `icon-array` | Trap: $0.99^2$ as the answer; or the tests treated as independent outright rather than given the disease. Edge: if both tests run in the same lab and share false alarms, the second positive adds much less (homework H2) | N, N, O, M |
| 30 | `lsn.cond.odds-form` The odds form | Update by posterior odds $=$ prior odds $\times$ likelihood ratio, and add log-odds for independent evidence | One coin in a hundred is two-headed. You pick one at random and toss 5 heads. Chance it is two-headed? ($\frac{32}{131} \approx 0.244$) | `odds-ledger` | Trap: adding evidence to the probability ("each head adds 10%"); reading the likelihood ratio 32 as the answer. Edge: one tail gives likelihood ratio 0: a single observation can settle a hypothesis for good | N, N, C, O |
| 31 | `lsn.cond.extra-conditioning` Conditional probabilities are probabilities | Apply Bayes' rule and total probability with an extra condition throughout, and predict the next observation by averaging over the hypotheses | A coin is fair, or lands heads with chance $\frac34$, each equally likely. The first toss is heads. Chance the second is heads? ($\frac{13}{20}$, not $\frac58$) | `prob-square` (nested), `tree-diagram` | Trap: tosses treated as independent, giving $\frac58$, the chance of heads before anything was seen; they are independent only given the coin. Edge: the tosses are dependent yet exchangeable | N, N, C, N |
| 32 | `lsn.cond.skill-or-luck` Skill or luck: Bayes on a trading record | Compute the posterior probability of skill from a track record, with likelihoods from sequences and a sceptical prior | A manager beat the market in 8 of 10 months. Suppose 10% of managers are skilled and win 60% of months, and the rest win 50%. Chance this one is skilled? (about 23%) | `odds-ledger`, `prob-square` (Bayes, three to five hypotheses) | Trap: "80% wins, so surely skilled", ignoring the prior and how often luck alone gives 8 of 10. Edge: the binomial coefficient cancels in the likelihood ratio; only the win and loss counts matter | N, N, X, M |
| 33 | `lsn.cond.naive-bayes` Many pieces of evidence: naive Bayes | Combine several pieces of evidence by multiplying likelihood ratios under conditional independence, adding their logarithms, and recognise when the assumption fails | 20% of email is spam. "Free" appears in 50% of spam and 5% of other mail; "meeting" in 2% of spam and 20% of other mail. An email contains both. Chance it is spam? (20%: the ratios 10 and $\frac1{10}$ cancel) | `odds-ledger`, `contingency-table` | Trap: counting only the suspicious word. Edge: a word never seen in genuine mail gives an infinite ratio unless counts are smoothed; the classifier ranks well even though its independence assumption is false (2.1) | N, C, N, M |

Transfer screens: lesson 32 is the first question about any backtest or track record (3.1); lesson 33 is a classifier still used in production text filters.

Then the Level review and homework `set.cond.bayes`.

**Level H3: Independence** (`lvl.cond.independence`, 5 lessons)

| # | Lesson | Objective | Hook | Widgets | Trap and edge case | Quiz |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| 34 | `lsn.cond.independence` When knowing tells you nothing | Test independence by $P(A \cap B) = P(A)P(B)$, show it is equivalent to $P(A \mid B) = P(A)$ when $P(B) > 0$, and that it passes to complements | Draw a card. Are "ace" and "spade" independent? Are "ace" and "picture card"? (Yes; no, they are disjoint) | `prob-square` (events), `outcome-grid` (row proportions) | Trap: disjoint taken as independent. Edge: an event of probability 0 or 1 is independent of every event, itself included | C, S, N, N |
| 35 | `lsn.cond.pairwise-mutual` Pairwise is not enough | Check mutual independence of three or more events on every sub-collection, and build events that are pairwise but not mutually independent | Toss two fair coins. A: the first is heads. B: the second is heads. C: the coins match. Check each pair, then all three. (Each pair independent; $P(A \cap B \cap C) = \frac14 \ne \frac18$) | `contingency-table`, `outcome-grid` (event chips) | Trap: pairwise checks taken as enough. Edge: $P(A \cap B \cap C) = P(A)P(B)P(C)$ can hold without pairwise independence; both kinds of condition are needed | M, N, C, S |
| 36 | `lsn.cond.conditional-independence` Conditional independence and explaining away | Distinguish independence from conditional independence in both directions, including the dependence created by conditioning on a common effect | Two tests are independent given whether you are ill. Are they independent overall? And: a fund database keeps only funds that beat their benchmark through skill or luck. Among the survivors, are skill and luck related? | `prob-square` (nested), `icon-array` | Trap: conditional independence assumed to carry over to independence, or the reverse. Edge: conditioning on a common effect (survivorship) creates dependence between causes that were independent (Berkson's paradox) | C, N, M, N |
| 37 | `lsn.cond.correlated-defaults` Correlated defaults | Compute joint default probabilities under a common factor and compare them with the independent answer | Two loans each default with chance 10%. In a bad year (chance 20%) each defaults with chance 40%, otherwise 2.5%, independently given the year. Chance both default? (3.25%, against 1% if independent) | `prob-square` (nested), `tree-diagram`, `contingency-table` | Trap: multiplying the marginal chances because the loans are "independent", when they are only conditionally independent. Edge: for five such loans, all default with chance 0.2%, 200 times the independent $10^{-5}$: tails are where dependence bites | N, N, X, M |
| 38 | `lsn.cond.simpson` Simpson's paradox | Recognise and explain a reversal between strata and the aggregate, and decide which comparison answers the question asked | In a kidney-stone study (Charig and others, 1986 [recalled]) treatment A cured more patients than B with small stones (93% against 87%) and with large stones (73% against 69%), yet fewer overall (78% against 83%). How? | `contingency-table` (strata), `icon-array` (Simpson) | Trap: trusting the aggregate. Edge: whether to stratify depends on what caused what: stone size drove the choice of treatment, so compare within sizes; a variable caused by the treatment should not be stratified on | N, N, M, C |

Transfer screens: lesson 37 is the error behind the 2007 to 2008 losses on pooled mortgage securities; lesson 38 appears whenever a strategy is compared across regimes or desks of different sizes.

Then the Level review and homework `set.cond.independence`.

**Level H4: Puzzles that fool experts** (`lvl.cond.puzzles`, 6 lessons)

| # | Lesson | Objective | Hook | Widgets | Trap and edge case | Quiz |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| 39 | `lsn.cond.monty-hall` Monty Hall | Solve Monty Hall by conditioning on the host's behaviour, and show how the answer changes for an ignorant or a biased host | You pick door 1 of 3; the host, who knows where the car is, opens door 3 to show a goat. Switch? (Switching wins $\frac23$) | `game-lab` (Monty Hall), `tree-diagram` | Trap: "two doors left, so 50-50". Edge: a host who opens a door at random and happens to show a goat makes it $\frac12$; a host who opens door 3 with probability $q$ when he has the choice makes it $\frac1{1 + q}$ (homework H4) | N, C, N, M |
| 40 | `lsn.cond.two-children` Two children, one boy | Compute conditional probabilities when the information is "at least one", "a specific one" or a rare detail, and say how the way information arrived matters | A family has two children and at least one is a boy. Chance both are boys? Now: at least one is a boy born on a Tuesday? ($\frac13$; $\frac{13}{27}$) | `outcome-grid` (labels), `contingency-table` | Trap: $\frac12$ every time. Edge: if you met one child at random and he is a boy, the answer is $\frac12$: what you learn depends on how you learned it | N, N, S, M |
| 41 | `lsn.cond.bertrand-box` Bertrand's box: count faces, not cards | Identify the equally likely units when conditioning on what is seen | Three cards: red on both sides, white on both, red and white. One is drawn at random and shows red. Chance the other side is red? ($\frac23$) | `chance-object` (two-sided cards), `outcome-grid` | Trap: $\frac12$, "it is one of two cards". Edge: the same structure as lesson 1: the right sample space is the one whose outcomes are equally likely, here the six faces | N, C, N, M |
| 42 | `lsn.cond.prosecutors-fallacy` The prosecutor's fallacy | Separate $P(\text{evidence} \mid \text{innocent})$ from $P(\text{innocent} \mid \text{evidence})$ and compute the second from a population count | A DNA profile matches one person in a million. In a city of 5 million a suspect matches, and there is no other evidence. Chance the suspect is the source? (about $\frac16$) | `icon-array` (fallacy), `odds-ledger` | Trap: "one in a million, so guilty beyond doubt". Edge: the defence's mirror error: "few men who beat their wives go on to murder them" ignores that this wife was murdered; condition on everything known | N, C, N, M |
| 43 | `lsn.cond.gamblers-ruin` First-step analysis: gambler's ruin | Set up and solve $p_i = p\,p_{i+1} + q\,p_{i-1}$ with its boundary values by conditioning on the first step | You have £3 and bet £1 at a time, winning each bet with chance 0.4, until you reach £5 or £0. Chance of reaching £5? ($\frac{76}{211} \approx 0.360$) | `first-step-solver`, `path-simulator` (barrier) | Trap: leaving out a boundary value; expecting the "fair share" $\frac35$. Edge: in a fair game $p_i = \frac iN$; against a bank with unlimited money, ruin is certain whenever $p \le \frac12$ | N, X, C, N |
| 44 | `lsn.cond.first-step-games` First-step analysis for games: deuce | Solve games that can return to an earlier state by writing one equation per state | At deuce a player who wins each point with chance 0.6 must get two points ahead. Chance she wins the game? ($\frac{0.36}{0.52} \approx 0.692$) | `first-step-solver`, `tree-diagram` | Trap: drawing an endless tree instead of noticing that the state repeats. Edge: $x = p^2 + 2pq\,x$ has one solution because $2pq < 1$; from 0-0 her chance of the game is 0.736 (homework H4) | N, X, N, C |

Transfer screens: lesson 43 is the risk of ruin of a trading account with a small edge; lesson 39 is the habit of asking how a piece of information was selected before using it.

Then the Level review and homework `set.cond.puzzles`.

### Course V: Random Variables and Their Distributions (`crs.rv`, 7 levels, 43 lessons)

The longest course, because it carries the vocabulary of everything after it. A random variable is a function on the sample space; its distribution is what probability statements about it need. The course builds the named discrete distributions from their stories, makes expectation the main tool (linearity and indicators above all), meets waiting times and rare events, moves to densities, the normal, the exponential and the Poisson process, then generating functions and the Gamma and Beta families. Blitzstein and Hwang chapters 3 to 6, sections 8.3, 8.4 and 10.4, and chapter 13; Stat 110 lectures 7 to 18, 20, 23, 24 and 30 [check].

**Level V1: Random variables and distributions** (`lvl.rv.distributions`, 6 lessons)

| # | Lesson | Objective | Hook | Widgets | Trap and edge case | Quiz |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| 45 | `lsn.rv.function` A random variable is a function | Define a random variable as a function $X: S \to \mathbb R$, compute the event $\{X = x\}$ as a set of outcomes, and tell a random variable from its distribution | Toss a coin three times. $X$ counts heads; $Y$ is the length of the longest run. Which outcomes give $X = 2$? $Y = 2$? (HHT, HTH, THH; HHT, HTT, THH, TTH) | `rv-mapper` | Trap: $X$ thought of as an outcome or as a single number. Edge: $X$ and $3 - X$ (heads and tails) have the same distribution but are never equal on HHH | S, N, X, M |
| 46 | `lsn.rv.pmf` Probability mass functions | Compute a PMF by adding the probabilities of the outcomes that map to each value, and check validity (non-negative, summing to 1) | Roll two dice and let $X$ be the larger number. Find $P(X = k)$. ($\frac{2k - 1}{36}$) | `rv-mapper`, `mass-editor` | Trap: $\frac16$ for each value, counting values instead of outcomes. Edge: a PMF can have infinite support and still sum to 1 ($2^{-k}$ for $k \ge 1$) | X, N, C, M |
| 47 | `lsn.rv.cdf` Cumulative distribution functions | Compute and sketch a discrete CDF as a right-continuous step function, and use $P(a < X \le b) = F(b) - F(a)$ with the endpoints handled exactly | For the larger of two dice $F(k) = \frac{k^2}{36}$. Find $P(2 < X \le 5)$ and $P(2 \le X \le 5)$. ($\frac7{12}$; $\frac23$) | `dist-explorer`, `sketch-pad` (step features) | Trap: treating $<$ and $\le$ alike for a discrete variable. Edge: the maximum's CDF is the product of the CDFs, $\left(\frac k6\right)^2$, the shortest route to its distribution | N, P, N, C |
| 48 | `lsn.rv.binomial` Bernoulli and binomial: the story | Derive the $\mathrm{Bin}(n, p)$ PMF from its story (successes in $n$ independent trials) by counting the sequences with $k$ successes | A ball falls through 10 rows of pins, going left or right with equal chance at each. Which bin is most likely, and how likely? (The middle one: $\frac{252}{1024} \approx 0.246$) | `galton-board`, `pascal-triangle`, `binomial-expander` (probability mode) | Trap: every bin equally likely, or one path per bin. Edge: if $X \sim \mathrm{Bin}(n, p)$ then $n - X \sim \mathrm{Bin}(n, 1 - p)$; for $p \ne \frac12$ the distribution is skewed | N, X, C, N |
| 49 | `lsn.rv.hypergeometric` Hypergeometric against binomial | Use the hypergeometric distribution for sampling without replacement, compare it with the binomial, and estimate a population size by capture-recapture | 50 fish are tagged and released. Later 40 are caught and 7 are tagged. Which lake size $N$ makes this most likely? (285) | `trial-simulator` (urn), `dist-explorer` (compare) | Trap: the binomial for a small population sampled without replacement. Edge: when the population is large next to the sample the two agree; the best $N$ comes from the ratio of consecutive likelihoods, not a derivative | N, N, C, M |
| 50 | `lsn.rv.functions-of-rv` Functions of a random variable | Find the PMF of $g(X)$ by pushing mass through $g$ and merging values that land together | $X$ is uniform on $\{-2, -1, 0, 1, 2\}$ and $Y = X^2$. PMF of $Y$? ($P(Y = 0) = \frac15$, $P(Y = 1) = P(Y = 4) = \frac25$) | `rv-mapper` (g stage) | Trap: keeping five equally likely values for $Y$. Edge: $g(X)$ is a random variable on the same sample space; when $g$ is one to one no mass merges | X, N, C, S |

Transfer screens: lesson 49 is Fisher's exact test (1.6) and the estimate of a hidden population, such as the size of an iceberg order from the fills you see; lesson 48 counts the paths to each node of a binomial pricing tree (1.7).

Then the Level review and homework `set.rv.distributions`.

**Level V2: Expectation** (`lvl.rv.expectation`, 6 lessons)

| # | Lesson | Objective | Hook | Widgets | Trap and edge case | Quiz |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| 51 | `lsn.rv.expectation` Expectation as a balance point | Compute $E[X] = \sum_xx\,P(X = x)$, read it as a balance point and a long-run average, and know it need not be a possible value | Guess where the PMF of the larger of two dice balances, then compute it. ($\frac{161}{36} \approx 4.47$) | `dist-explorer` (fulcrum), `mass-editor` | Trap: the most likely value (6) or the middle value (3.5) offered as the mean. Edge: an expectation can be infinite or fail to exist (lesson 64) | N, N, C, M |
| 52 | `lsn.rv.linearity` Linearity of expectation | Use $E[X + Y] = E[X] + E[Y]$ and $E[cX] = cE[X]$ with or without independence | Shuffle $n$ letters into their $n$ envelopes. Expected number in the right envelope, for $n = 3$, 10 and 52? (1 each time) | `indicator-board` (fixed points) | Trap: "the events depend on each other, so the expectations cannot add". Edge: $E[XY] = E[X]E[Y]$ needs more than linearity: independence is enough, but not necessary | N, N, X, M |
| 53 | `lsn.rv.indicators` Indicators and the fundamental bridge | Write a count as a sum of indicators, use $E[I_A] = P(A)$, and derive the binomial and hypergeometric means in one line | Toss a coin 10 times. Expected number of times HH appears, overlaps allowed? ($\frac94$) | `indicator-board`, `derivation-steps` | Trap: finding the distribution of the count first. Edge: an expected count need not be a whole number; the expected number of tagged fish, $\frac{nw}{w + b}$, needs no hypergeometric PMF | N, X, N, C |
| 54 | `lsn.rv.indicators-interview` Indicators for interview problems | Solve expected-count problems on seatings, runs, birthdays and graphs with indicators and symmetry | Ten couples sit at random in 20 seats round a table. Expected number of couples sitting together? ($\frac{20}{19}$) | `indicator-board` | Trap: computing $P(\text{at least one couple together})$ instead. Edge: linearity gives expectations, never probabilities; the expected number of distinct birthdays among $k$ people is $365\left(1 - \left(\frac{364}{365}\right)^k\right)$ | N, N, X, C |
| 55 | `lsn.rv.lotus` LOTUS | Compute $E[g(X)] = \sum_xg(x)P(X = x)$ without finding the distribution of $g(X)$, and compare $E[X^2]$ with $(E[X])^2$ | A game pays the square of a die roll in pounds and costs $3.5^2 = £12.25$. Fair? (No: $E[X^2] = \frac{91}6 \approx £15.17$) | `rv-mapper` (LOTUS), `dist-explorer` | Trap: $E[g(X)] = g(E[X])$. Edge: for convex $g$ the gap always has the same sign (Jensen, lesson 114) | N, N, C, X |
| 56 | `lsn.rv.variance` Variance and standard deviation | Compute $\mathrm{Var}(X) = E[(X - \mu)^2] = E[X^2] - \mu^2$, use $\mathrm{Var}(aX + b) = a^2\mathrm{Var}(X)$, and read the standard deviation in the units of $X$ | Three bets each have mean £1: £1 for sure; £0 or £2 on a coin; £$-8$ or £10 on a coin. How much more spread has the third than the second? (Variance 81 against 1; standard deviation 9 against 1) | `dist-explorer` (deviation squares), `mass-editor` | Trap: $\mathrm{Var}(aX + b) = a\,\mathrm{Var}(X) + b$; variance and standard deviation swapped. Edge: $\mathrm{Var}(X) = 0$ exactly when $X$ is constant with probability 1 | N, N, C, M |

Transfer screens: lesson 56 is volatility, the standard deviation of returns (1.1, 1.7); lesson 54's method computes the expected overlap between two portfolios or two watch lists.

Then the Level review and homework `set.rv.expectation`.

**Level V3: Waiting times and rare events** (`lvl.rv.waiting`, 6 lessons)

| # | Lesson | Objective | Hook | Widgets | Trap and edge case | Quiz |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| 57 | `lsn.rv.geometric` Geometric and first-success distributions | Derive the PMF and mean of $\mathrm{Geom}(p)$ (failures before the first success, mean $\frac qp$) and of $\mathrm{FS}(p)$ (trials including it, mean $\frac1p$), by series and by first-step analysis | Roll a die until the first six. Expected number of rolls? Of non-six rolls before it? (6; 5) | `trial-strip`, `dist-explorer` | Trap: mixing the two conventions (the label is always on screen). Edge: for small $p$ the standard deviation is nearly as large as the mean, so waits vary wildly | N, N, C, X |
| 58 | `lsn.rv.memoryless` Memorylessness | Prove $P(X \ge n + k \mid X \ge n) = P(X \ge k)$ for the geometric, and that it is the only memoryless distribution on $\{0, 1, 2, \dots\}$ | You have rolled 10 times without a six. Expected further rolls until a six? (Still 6) | `trial-strip` (condition) | Trap: "a six is due". Edge: lifetimes of things that wear out are not memoryless: for them a long wait so far means a shorter wait to come | N, O, N, M |
| 59 | `lsn.rv.negative-binomial` Negative binomial: waiting for the $r$th success | Derive the $\mathrm{NBin}(r, p)$ PMF, write it as a sum of $r$ geometric waits for its mean, and solve best-of-$n$ series | A team wins each game with chance 0.55. Chance it wins a best-of-seven series? (0.608) | `trial-strip`, `dist-explorer` | Trap: $\binom74p^4q^3$ alone, the chance of exactly four wins in seven games. Edge: imagining all seven games played gives the same answer (lesson 19's leap again); the expected number of games actually played is 5.78 | N, X, C, N |
| 60 | `lsn.rv.pattern-waiting` Waiting for patterns: HH against HT | Compute expected waiting times for short patterns by first-step analysis on how much of the pattern has been matched | Toss a fair coin. Which takes longer on average to appear, HH or HT? (6 against 4) | `trial-strip` (pattern race), `first-step-solver` (pattern automaton), `game-lab` (Penney) | Trap: "both are two particular tosses, so both take 4". Edge: Penney's game: every pattern of length 3 is beaten by some other, so the second player always has the advantage (THH beats HHT three times in four) | N, N, C, M |
| 61 | `lsn.rv.poisson` The Poisson distribution | Derive $\mathrm{Pois}(\lambda)$ as the limit of $\mathrm{Bin}\left(n, \frac\lambda n\right)$, and use its PMF, mean and variance $\lambda$ | A desk averages 2 "fat-finger" errors a month among thousands of trades. Chance of none this month? ($e^{-2} \approx 0.135$) | `dist-explorer` (compare), `trial-strip` (discretised) | Trap: $\lambda$ read as a probability; the Poisson used for counts with a hard cap or strong clustering. Edge: mean equals variance, so a sample variance far above the mean signals a mixture (overdispersion) | N, N, C, M |
| 62 | `lsn.rv.poisson-approx` Poisson approximation in practice | Approximate counts of many weakly dependent rare events by a Poisson with $\lambda$ equal to the expected count | In a class of 30, approximate the chance of a shared birthday by counting pairs. ($\lambda = \frac{435}{365} \approx 1.19$, so about 0.696; exact 0.706) | `indicator-board` (pairs), `dist-explorer` | Trap: $\lambda = \frac{30}{365}$, people instead of pairs. Edge: the approximation needs rare, weakly dependent events; triple birthdays need their own count | N, N, C, N |

Transfer screens: lesson 61 models order arrivals and defaults in a loan book; lesson 60 shows why "signals" defined by patterns in noise fire at different rates.

Then the Level review and homework `set.rv.waiting`.
