**Level V4: Expectation puzzles** (`lvl.rv.puzzles`, 6 lessons)

| # | Lesson | Objective | Hook | Widgets | Trap and edge case | Quiz |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| 63 | `lsn.rv.coupon-collector` Coupon collector | Write the collection time as a sum of geometric waits and derive $E = nH_n$ | Roll a die until every face has appeared. Expected number of rolls? (14.7) | `indicator-board` (coupons), `trial-strip`, `derivation-steps` | Trap: 6 or 36. Edge: $nH_n \approx n\ln n$; the last face alone takes 6 rolls on average, more than the first three together | N, X, C, N |
| 64 | `lsn.rv.st-petersburg` St Petersburg and infinite expectations | Show the St Petersburg game has infinite expectation, and value it against a finite bank or with a utility | A coin is tossed until the first head; if it comes on toss $k$ you win $£2^k$. What would you pay? ($E = \infty$; against a bank holding $£2^{30}$, exactly £31) | `game-lab` (St Petersburg) | Trap: "the expected value is the fair price" when it is infinite and the running average never settles. Edge: capping the payout at $2^{30}$ gives $30 + 1 = 31$, and the cap, not the coin, decides the price | N, C, N, M |
| 65 | `lsn.rv.tail-sum` The tail-sum formula | Prove and use $E[X] = \sum_{n \ge 0}P(X > n)$ for non-negative whole-number $X$ | Expected value of the largest of three dice? ($\frac{119}{24} \approx 4.96$) | `dist-explorer`, `derivation-steps` | Trap: summing $P(X \ge n)$ from $n = 0$, one term too many. Edge: the continuous version $E[X] = \int_0^\infty P(X > x)\,dx$ (lesson 70) | N, O, N, C |
| 66 | `lsn.rv.variance-of-counts` The variance of a count | Compute the variance of a sum of indicators from $E\left[\left(\sum I_j\right)^2\right] = \sum P(A_j) + \sum_{i \ne j}P(A_i \cap A_j)$, without assuming independence | The number of letters in the right envelope has mean 1. Its variance? (1, for every $n \ge 2$) | `indicator-board` (pairs), `derivation-steps` | Trap: indicators assumed independent, giving $1 - \frac1n$. Edge: the binomial variance $npq$ and the hypergeometric variance with its factor $\frac{N - n}{N - 1}$ come from the same identity | N, X, C, N |
| 67 | `lsn.rv.records` Records in a random order | Use the symmetry of random orderings to show $P(\text{the } j\text{th value is a record}) = \frac1j$, and add indicators to get $H_n$ expected records | 100 distinct daily closing prices arrive in a random order. Expected number of days that set a new high? ($H_{100} \approx 5.19$) | `indicator-board` (records), `trial-simulator` (permutation) | Trap: records "stop coming"; they keep arriving, ever more slowly. Edge: the record indicators are independent, a surprise; real prices are not in random order, which is why trends produce more highs | N, X, C, N |
| 68 | `lsn.rv.pricing-by-expectation` Pricing a bet: expectation and spread | Price a payoff at its expectation, quote a two-sided market with a width tied to its standard deviation, and decide which side of a given quote to take | Make a market on the total of three dice. Where is your mid, and what sets your width? (Mid 10.5; standard deviation about 2.96) | `dist-explorer`, `convolver` (discrete), `numeric-input` | Trap: quoting the most common total or a familiar number. Edge: expectation is the right price for small bets repeated many times; for one large bet the spread and your bankroll matter (lesson 154) | N, N, C, M |

Transfer screens: lesson 68 is the first market-making game (3.3); lesson 64 is why bankroll limits and log utility enter trading; lesson 67 counts how often a random sequence sets new highs.

Then the Level review and homework `set.rv.puzzles`.

**Level V5: Continuous random variables** (`lvl.rv.continuous`, 7 lessons)

| # | Lesson | Objective | Hook | Widgets | Trap and edge case | Quiz |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| 69 | `lsn.rv.densities` From histograms to densities | Use a PDF to compute probabilities as areas, check validity, and explain why $P(X = x) = 0$ and why a density can exceed 1 | $X$ has density $f(x) = 2x$ on $[0, 1]$. Is $f(0.9) = 1.8$ a probability? What is $P(X > 0.9)$? (No; 0.19) | `sampling-lab` (population view), `stat-chart` (frequency density), `dist-explorer`, `accumulator` | Trap: a density value read as a probability. Edge: a density can be unbounded ($\frac1{2\sqrt x}$ on $(0, 1]$); probability 0 does not mean impossible | N, C, N, P |
| 70 | `lsn.rv.cdf-quantiles` CDFs, quantiles and the median | Move between PDF and CDF ($F' = f$), compute quantiles and the median, and use $E[X] = \int_0^\infty P(X > x)\,dx$ for non-negative $X$ | A wait is exponential with mean 10 minutes. Median wait? ($10\ln 2 \approx 6.9$ minutes) | `dist-explorer`, `accumulator` | Trap: the median taken equal to the mean for a skewed distribution. Edge: an insurance claim with a point mass at 0 has a CDF with a jump, neither discrete nor continuous | N, X, C, P |
| 71 | `lsn.rv.universality` Universality of the uniform | Prove $F^{-1}(U) \sim F$ and $F(X) \sim \mathrm{Unif}(0, 1)$ for continuous $F$, and use them to simulate and to test calibration | You have only a uniform random number generator. Simulate an exponential wait with mean 10. ($-10\ln(1 - U)$) | `dist-explorer` (inverse transform, PIT), `code-cell` | Trap: applying $F$ instead of $F^{-1}$. Edge: if a model's forecast distributions are right, the values $F(\text{observed})$ are uniform: the calibration test of a risk model or a probabilistic forecaster (2.1, 3.1) | X, N, C, K |
| 72 | `lsn.rv.normal` The normal distribution | Standardise $X \sim \mathcal N(\mu, \sigma^2)$ to $Z$, compute probabilities with $\Phi$ and its symmetry, and use the 68-95-99.7 rule | Daily returns are modelled as $\mathcal N(0.05\%, (1\%)^2)$. Chance of a loss worse than 2%? ($\Phi(-2.05) \approx 0.020$) | `dist-explorer` (standardise) | Trap: dividing by the variance instead of the standard deviation. Edge: real returns have fatter tails; a five-standard-deviation fall has normal probability about $3 \times 10^{-7}$ a day, yet markets show several a decade | N, N, C, N |
| 73 | `lsn.rv.exponential` The exponential distribution | Use $\mathrm{Expo}(\lambda)$, prove its memorylessness, and derive that the minimum of independent exponentials is exponential with $P(X_1 < X_2) = \frac{\lambda_1}{\lambda_1 + \lambda_2}$ | Buses come at random, on average every 10 minutes. You have waited 8 minutes. Expected further wait? (10 minutes) | `dist-explorer`, `trial-strip` (Poisson) | Trap: "about 2 more minutes". Edge: $\min(X_1, X_2) \sim \mathrm{Expo}(\lambda_1 + \lambda_2)$; the exponential is the only memoryless continuous distribution | N, N, C, X |
| 74 | `lsn.rv.poisson-process` Poisson processes | Use the counts ($\mathrm{Pois}(\lambda t)$ in a window of length $t$, independent across disjoint windows) and gaps ($\mathrm{Expo}(\lambda)$) of a Poisson process, and merge and thin processes | Buy orders arrive at 3 a minute and sell orders at 2 a minute, independently. Chance the next order is a buy? Distribution of the number of orders in 2 minutes? ($\frac35$; $\mathrm{Pois}(10)$) | `trial-strip` (Poisson) | Trap: counts in separate windows treated as dependent, or rates not added when streams merge. Edge: if each order is large with chance 0.1, large orders form their own Poisson process of rate 0.5 a minute, independent of the small ones; given $n$ arrivals in $[0, t]$ their times are uniform | N, N, C, M |
| 75 | `lsn.rv.inspection-paradox` The inspection paradox | Show that the gap you arrive in is longer than a typical gap (size-biased sampling), and compute the expected wait for a Poisson process | Buses arrive as a Poisson process, on average every 10 minutes. You turn up at a random time. Expected wait? Expected length of the gap you land in? (10 minutes; 20 minutes) | `trial-strip` (Poisson, arrival marker) [mode to add], `dist-explorer` | Trap: "half a gap, so 5 minutes". Edge: the same bias makes the average class a student sits in larger than the average class, and positions sampled at a random date look longer-held than typical ones | N, N, C, M |

Transfer screens: lesson 74 is the order-arrival model of Avellaneda and Stoikov (3.1); lesson 71 is how every Monte Carlo engine turns uniforms into the distributions it needs (1.7); lesson 72's tails are why risk models are judged on their exceptions.

Then the Level review and homework `set.rv.continuous`.

**Level V6: Moments and generating functions** (`lvl.rv.moments`, 6 lessons)

| # | Lesson | Objective | Hook | Widgets | Trap and edge case | Quiz |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| 76 | `lsn.rv.loss-minimisers` Mean, median and mode as best guesses | Show that $E[(X - c)^2]$ is minimised at the mean and $E\lvert X - c \rvert$ at any median | You must guess a die roll and pay the squared error; then you pay the absolute error. Best guesses? (3.5; any number in $[3, 4]$) | `dist-explorer` (loss) | Trap: assuming the mean minimises every kind of error. Edge: a median need not be unique; for skewed returns the typical outcome (median) sits below the mean | N, I, C, M |
| 77 | `lsn.rv.skew-kurtosis` Moments, skewness and kurtosis | Compute standardised third and fourth moments, and read skewness and excess kurtosis as statements about tails | Two strategies have the same mean and standard deviation; one sells far out-of-the-money options. Which number tells them apart? (Skewness: strongly negative for the option seller) | `dist-explorer`, `sampling-lab` | Trap: kurtosis read as "peakedness". Edge: a Student $t$ with $\nu$ degrees of freedom has a finite kurtosis only for $\nu > 4$; the Cauchy has no moments at all | N, C, M, S |
| 78 | `lsn.rv.mgf` Moment generating functions | Compute the MGFs of the main distributions, get moments from derivatives at 0, and read $M(t)$ as an expectation | $M(t) = \left(\frac12 + \frac12e^t\right)^{10}$. What is $E[X]$, without finding the distribution? (5) | `generating-function`, `taylor-builder` | Trap: $M(t)$ read as a probability. Edge: the MGF can fail to exist near 0 (lognormal, Cauchy) although the distribution is fine | X, N, C, N |
| 79 | `lsn.rv.mgf-sums` MGFs of sums, and uniqueness | Use $M_{X + Y} = M_XM_Y$ for independent $X$, $Y$ and the uniqueness of MGFs to identify the distribution of a sum | $X \sim \mathrm{Pois}(2)$ and $Y \sim \mathrm{Pois}(3)$ are independent. Distribution of $X + Y$? ($\mathrm{Pois}(5)$) | `generating-function` (product, match) | Trap: MGFs added for a sum. Edge: $X + X = 2X$ has MGF $M(2t)$, not $M(t)^2$: independence is the whole point | S, X, C, N |
| 80 | `lsn.rv.pgf-branching` PGFs and branching processes | Use $G(s) = E\left[s^X\right]$, compose PGFs across generations, and find the extinction probability as the smallest fixed point in $[0, 1]$ | Everyone who hears a rumour tells 0, 1 or 2 people with chances $\frac14$, $\frac14$, $\frac12$. Chance the rumour dies out, starting from one person? ($\frac12$) | `generating-function` (PGF, cobweb), `iteration-cobweb` | Trap: the root 1, the largest fixed point. Edge: if the mean number told is at most 1, extinction is certain (unless everyone tells exactly one) | N, X, C, M |
| 81 | `lsn.rv.pgf-dice` Generating functions for dice | Find the distribution of a sum of dice from the product of PGFs, and factorise to find other dice with the same distribution of totals | Can two dice with positive whole-number faces, other than ordinary dice, give exactly the usual distribution of totals? (Yes: $\{1, 2, 2, 3, 3, 4\}$ and $\{1, 3, 4, 5, 6, 8\}$, Sicherman's dice) | `generating-function`, `convolver` (discrete grid) | Trap: "the totals pin the faces down". Edge: factorising $(x + \dots + x^6)^2 = x^2(1 + x)^2(1 + x + x^2)^2(1 - x + x^2)^2$ and sharing the factors differently is the creative step | N, X, C, M |

Transfer screens: lesson 77 reads a strategy's return distribution beyond its Sharpe ratio (2.3); lesson 80 models default contagion and viral spread; lesson 78's MGF reappears in the Chernoff bound (lesson 113).

Then the Level review and homework `set.rv.moments`.

**Level V7: Gamma, Beta and their relatives** (`lvl.rv.families`, 6 lessons)

| # | Lesson | Objective | Hook | Widgets | Trap and edge case | Quiz |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| 82 | `lsn.rv.gamma` Gamma: waiting for the $k$th arrival | Identify $\mathrm{Gamma}(k, \lambda)$ as a sum of $k$ independent $\mathrm{Expo}(\lambda)$ waits, compute its mean $\frac k\lambda$, and use $P(T_k > t) = P(N(t) < k)$ | Orders arrive at 2 a minute. Expected time until the fifth order? Chance it takes more than 3 minutes? (2.5 minutes; $P(\mathrm{Pois}(6) \le 4) = 115e^{-6} \approx 0.285$) | `convolver` (repeat), `dist-explorer`, `trial-strip` | Trap: rate and scale confused (mean $k\lambda$). Edge: the duality between waiting times and counts turns a Gamma tail into a Poisson sum; $\Gamma\left(\frac12\right) = \sqrt\pi$ (1.3) | N, N, C, X |
| 83 | `lsn.rv.beta` Beta: Bayes' billiards | Derive $\int_0^1\binom nkp^k(1 - p)^{n - k}\,dp = \frac1{n + 1}$ by a story, and use $\mathrm{Beta}(a, b)$ with mean $\frac a{a + b}$ | Throw a ball at random onto a table and mark its position $p$. Throw $n$ more; $k$ land left of the first. Chance of each $k$? ($\frac1{n + 1}$ for every $k$) | `order-statistics`, `dist-explorer` | Trap: $k \sim \mathrm{Bin}\left(n, \frac12\right)$. Edge: throwing all $n + 1$ balls first and choosing which is "first" afterwards gives the answer with no integral: the creative step | N, X, C, M |
| 84 | `lsn.rv.beta-binomial` Learning a probability: Beta-binomial updating | Update a $\mathrm{Beta}(a, b)$ prior to $\mathrm{Beta}(a + k, b + n - k)$ after $k$ successes in $n$ trials, and compute the predictive chance $\frac{a + k}{a + b + n}$ | A new signal won 7 of its first 10 trades. With a uniform prior on its win rate, chance it wins the next? ($\frac8{12} = \frac23$) | `dist-explorer` (Beta update) [mode to add], `trial-strip` | Trap: $\frac7{10}$, the observed rate, as the predictive chance. Edge: Laplace's rule of succession $\frac{k + 1}{n + 2}$: even after 10 wins in 10 the next is not certain ($\frac{11}{12}$) | N, X, C, N |
| 85 | `lsn.rv.polya-urn` Pólya's urn | Compute sequence probabilities in Pólya's urn, show they depend only on the colour counts (exchangeability), and connect the urn to Beta-binomial updating | An urn holds one red and one black ball. Draw one, return it with another of the same colour, repeat. Chance the second draw is red? Distribution of the number of reds in four draws? ($\frac12$; uniform on $\{0, 1, 2, 3, 4\}$) | `trial-simulator` (Pólya) | Trap: "the urn favours whatever came first, so the second draw is more likely red", which holds only given the first. Edge: the urn behaves exactly like Bayes' billiards: a uniform $p$ chosen once, then independent $p$-coin tosses | N, N, C, M |
| 86 | `lsn.rv.lognormal` Lognormal prices | Derive $E\left[e^X\right] = e^{\mu + \sigma^2/2}$ for $X \sim \mathcal N(\mu, \sigma^2)$ from the normal MGF, and compare the mean and median of a lognormal price | A year's log return is $\mathcal N(0, 0.2^2)$. Is the expected price in a year above today's? (Yes, by the factor $e^{0.02} \approx 1.020$, while the median is today's price) | `dist-explorer`, `function-mapping` (density mode) | Trap: $E\left[e^X\right] = e^{E[X]}$. Edge: the lognormal has every moment but no MGF; most paths end below the mean (observed in 1.1 lesson 73, proved here) | N, X, C, N |
| 87 | `lsn.rv.chi-t-cauchy` Chi-square, Student $t$ and Cauchy | Build $\chi^2_n$, $t_n$ and the Cauchy from normals, and recognise distributions with heavy tails or no mean | The ratio of two independent standard normals: what is its mean? (It has none: the ratio is Cauchy) | `dist-explorer`, `sampling-lab` (Cauchy) | Trap: "symmetric about 0, so the mean is 0". Edge: $t_\nu \to \mathcal N(0, 1)$ as $\nu \to \infty$, and $t_1$ is the Cauchy | C, N, M, C |

Transfer screens: lesson 84 estimates a new strategy's win rate without overreacting to its first trades (2.3); lesson 86 is the price model of Black-Scholes (1.7); lesson 87's $t$ is 1.6's small-sample distribution and a model of fat-tailed returns.

Then the Level review and homework `set.rv.families`.

### Course J: Joint Distributions, Conditional Expectation and Limit Theorems (`crs.joint`, 6 levels, 35 lessons)

Several random variables at once: how they move together, how one predicts another, how to transform them, and what happens to their averages. The course builds joint, marginal and conditional distributions, covariance and the covariance matrix, transformations with the Jacobian, the multivariate normal, conditional expectation with Adam's and Eve's laws, the inequalities, and the laws of large numbers and the central limit theorem. Blitzstein and Hwang chapters 7 to 10; Stat 110 lectures 19 to 30 [check].

**Level J1: Joint, marginal and conditional** (`lvl.joint.joint`, 6 lessons)

| # | Lesson | Objective | Hook | Widgets | Trap and edge case | Quiz |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| 88 | `lsn.joint.tables` Joint, marginal and conditional distributions | Compute marginal and conditional PMFs from a joint table, and check independence cell by cell | Roll two dice; $X$ is the smaller number and $Y$ the larger. Find the joint PMF and $P(Y = 6 \mid X = 2)$. ($\frac29$) | `contingency-table` (joint), `joint-density` (table) | Trap: a joint cell read as a conditional probability. Edge: different joint tables share the same marginals; the marginals do not fix the dependence | N, N, X, M |
| 89 | `lsn.joint.joint-pdf` Joint densities | Normalise a joint density over a region that is not a rectangle, and find marginals by integrating out with the right limits | $(X, Y)$ is uniform on the triangle $0 < x < y < 1$. Find the constant and the density of $X$. ($c = 2$; $f_X(x) = 2(1 - x)$) | `joint-density`, `region-bounds` | Trap: integrating over the whole square. Edge: a constant joint density on a triangle is not independent: the support alone creates dependence | N, X, R, M |
| 90 | `lsn.joint.conditional-densities` Conditional densities and continuous Bayes | Compute $f_{Y \mid X}(y \mid x) = \frac{f(x, y)}{f_X(x)}$ as a renormalised slice, and use continuous versions of total probability and Bayes' rule | On the triangle $0 < x < y < 1$, given $X = 0.6$, what is the distribution of $Y$? ($\mathrm{Unif}(0.6, 1)$) | `joint-density` | Trap: using the slice without dividing by its area. Edge: conditioning on an event of probability 0 is defined through slices, and different ways of approaching the event can disagree (Borel's paradox) | X, N, N, M |
| 91 | `lsn.joint.independence-rv` Independence of random variables | Decide independence by factorising the joint PMF or PDF on a support shaped like a rectangle, and use that functions of independent variables are independent | Are $X$ and $Y$ independent when $f(x, y) = 6e^{-2x - 3y}$ on $x, y > 0$? When $f(x, y) = 8xy$ on $0 < x < y < 1$? (Yes; no) | `joint-density` | Trap: a formula that factorises on a support that does not. Edge: independence makes a double integral factorise, the picture of 1.3 lesson 129 | C, M, N, X |
| 92 | `lsn.joint.two-d-lotus` Probabilities over regions and 2D LOTUS | Compute $P((X, Y) \in R)$ and $E[g(X, Y)]$ by double integrals over the right region, using symmetry where it halves the work | Two points are dropped uniformly on a stick of length 1. Expected distance between them? ($\frac13$) | `joint-density`, `region-bounds`, `geo-prob` | Trap: covering only the case $X < Y$. Edge: by symmetry $E\lvert X - Y \rvert = 2E\left[(Y - X)I_{\{X < Y\}}\right]$ | N, N, R, X |
| 93 | `lsn.joint.multinomial` The multinomial distribution | Use the multinomial PMF, its binomial marginals and its conditional distributions, and lump categories | Roll a die 12 times. Chance each face appears exactly twice? ($\frac{12!}{2^6 \cdot 6^{12}} \approx 0.0034$) | `counting-slots`, `trial-simulator` (urn) | Trap: multiplying six binomial probabilities as if the counts were independent. Edge: the counts are negatively correlated: $\mathrm{Cov}(N_i, N_j) = -np_ip_j$ (homework J1) | N, X, C, N |

Transfer screens: lesson 88 is a confusion matrix (2.1); lesson 93 models the split of a day's volume across trading venues.

Then the Level review and homework `set.joint.joint`.

**Level J2: Covariance and correlation** (`lvl.joint.covariance`, 5 lessons)

| # | Lesson | Objective | Hook | Widgets | Trap and edge case | Quiz |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| 94 | `lsn.joint.covariance` Covariance | Compute $\mathrm{Cov}(X, Y) = E[XY] - E[X]E[Y]$, read it as an average signed rectangle, and use bilinearity | $X$ is the first die and $S$ the total of two dice. What is $\mathrm{Cov}(X, S)$? ($\frac{35}{12}$, by bilinearity) | `covariance-lab` | Trap: covariance read as the slope or the strength of a relationship. Edge: $\mathrm{Cov}(X, X) = \mathrm{Var}(X)$; covariance carries the units of both variables | N, X, N, M |
| 95 | `lsn.joint.correlation` Correlation; uncorrelated but dependent | Compute correlation, read it as covariance of standardised variables, and show zero correlation does not imply independence | $X \sim \mathrm{Unif}(-1, 1)$ and $Y = X^2$. $Y$ is a function of $X$. Their correlation? (0) | `covariance-lab`, `derivation-steps` | Trap: zero correlation read as independence. Edge: correlation is unchanged by positive rescaling; it is $\pm1$ exactly when $Y$ is a linear function of $X$ (proved in lesson 115) | N, C, M, O |
| 96 | `lsn.joint.variance-of-sums` Variance of sums and the two-asset portfolio | Use $\mathrm{Var}(aX + bY) = a^2\mathrm{Var}(X) + b^2\mathrm{Var}(Y) + 2ab\,\mathrm{Cov}(X, Y)$ and find the minimum-variance mix | Two assets each have 20% volatility and correlation 0.3. Volatility of a half-and-half portfolio? (about 16.1%) | `gaussian-2d` (combination, portfolio), `covariance-lab` (sum) | Trap: adding standard deviations (20%), or dropping the covariance term. Edge: with correlation $-1$ a riskless mix exists; the minimum-variance weight is $w^* = \frac{\sigma_2^2 - \rho\sigma_1\sigma_2}{\sigma_1^2 + \sigma_2^2 - 2\rho\sigma_1\sigma_2}$ | N, X, C, N |
| 97 | `lsn.joint.sample-mean` The variance of an average | Prove $\mathrm{Var}(\bar X) = \frac{\sigma^2}n$ for independent draws, and $\sigma^2\left(\rho + \frac{1 - \rho}n\right)$ when every pair has correlation $\rho$ | Average 100 returns, each with standard deviation 2%. Standard deviation of the average if independent? If every pair has correlation 0.2? (0.2%; about 0.91%) | `sampling-lab`, `covariance-lab` | Trap: $\frac\sigma n$; or the independent formula used for correlated data. Edge: correlation puts a floor $\rho\sigma^2$ under diversification; the effective sample size is $\frac n{1 + (n - 1)\rho}$ | N, N, C, X |
| 98 | `lsn.joint.covariance-matrix` The covariance matrix | Build a covariance matrix, compute $\mathrm{Var}(\mathbf w^\top\mathbf X) = \mathbf w^\top\Sigma\mathbf w$, and use positive semidefiniteness to rule out impossible correlations | Can three assets have pairwise correlations 0.9, 0.9 and $-0.9$? (No: the matrix has a negative eigenvalue, $-0.8$, so some portfolio would have negative variance) | `matrix`, `quadratic-form`, `eigen-finder` | Trap: any symmetric matrix with ones on the diagonal and entries in $[-1, 1]$ taken as a correlation matrix. Edge: $\mathrm{Cov}(A\mathbf X) = A\Sigma A^\top$, the rule behind every factor model and PCA (1.4) | A, C, N, M |

Transfer screens: lesson 96 is portfolio construction (1.7, canon-gap P6); lesson 97 explains why a backtest on correlated assets has fewer effective observations than rows; lesson 98 is a risk system's check on a correlation matrix estimated from patchy data.

Then the Level review and homework `set.joint.covariance`.

**Level J3: Transformations and the multivariate normal** (`lvl.joint.transform`, 7 lessons)

| # | Lesson | Objective | Hook | Widgets | Trap and edge case | Quiz |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| 99 | `lsn.joint.change-of-variables` Change of variables | Find the density of $Y = g(X)$ with $f_Y(y) = f_X(x)\left\lvert\frac{dx}{dy}\right\rvert$ for monotone $g$, and by summing over branches otherwise | $U \sim \mathrm{Unif}(0, 1)$. Density of $U^2$? ($\frac1{2\sqrt y}$ on $(0, 1)$) | `function-mapping` (density mode) | Trap: substituting $x = \sqrt y$ into $f_X$ and stopping, with no stretch factor. Edge: $Z^2$ for $Z \sim \mathcal N(0, 1)$ needs both branches $\pm\sqrt y$, and gives $\chi^2_1$ | X, N, C, X |
| 100 | `lsn.joint.convolution` Convolutions | Compute the distribution of a sum of independent variables by convolution, discrete and continuous | Add two independent $\mathrm{Unif}(0, 1)$ variables. Density of the sum at 0.5? At 1? (0.5; 1) | `convolver` (flip and slide) | Trap: adding the two densities; expecting the sum to be uniform. Edge: a sum of a few uniforms already looks normal (lesson 119); a sum of independent normals is normal | N, P, C, X |
| 101 | `lsn.joint.order-statistics` Order statistics | Derive the CDFs of the maximum and minimum and the distribution of the $k$th smallest of $n$ uniforms, $\mathrm{Beta}(k, n - k + 1)$ | Five sealed bids are independent and uniform on $[0, 1]$. Expected highest bid? Expected second highest? ($\frac56$; $\frac46$) | `order-statistics` | Trap: the maximum of uniforms expected to be 1; sorted values treated as independent. Edge: the $n + 1$ gaps between sorted uniforms are exchangeable, each with mean $\frac1{n + 1}$ | N, N, X, X |
| 102 | `lsn.joint.box-muller` Two-dimensional transformations: Box-Muller | Transform joint densities with the Jacobian determinant, and derive the Box-Muller method for making normals from uniforms | From two independent uniforms, make two independent standard normals. ($R = \sqrt{-2\ln U_1}$, $\Theta = 2\pi U_2$) | `transform-grid`, `joint-density` | Trap: forgetting $\lvert\det J\rvert$, or using the Jacobian of the wrong direction. Edge: the method works because a pair of independent standard normals looks the same after any rotation, the reason for the $\sqrt{2\pi}$ (1.3 lesson 133) | X, N, C, M |
| 103 | `lsn.joint.bank-post-office` The bank and the post office | Show that for independent $X \sim \mathrm{Gamma}(a, \lambda)$ and $Y \sim \mathrm{Gamma}(b, \lambda)$, the total $X + Y \sim \mathrm{Gamma}(a + b, \lambda)$ and the fraction $\frac X{X + Y} \sim \mathrm{Beta}(a, b)$ are independent | You wait at the bank for an exponential time, then at the post office for another, independent one with the same rate. What share of your total time was the bank, and does it depend on the total? ($\mathrm{Unif}(0, 1)$; no) | `transform-grid`, `joint-density`, `trial-strip` | Trap: "a long total means the bank took longer", assumed dependence. Edge: this independence characterises the Gamma (Lukacs's theorem); it gives the Beta mean $\frac a{a + b}$ in one line | X, N, C, M |
| 104 | `lsn.joint.bivariate-normal` The bivariate normal and regression to the mean | Use $E[Y \mid X = x] = \mu_Y + \rho\frac{\sigma_Y}{\sigma_X}(x - \mu_X)$ and $\mathrm{Var}(Y \mid X) = \sigma_Y^2(1 - \rho^2)$, and explain regression to the mean | Fathers' and sons' heights both have mean 175 cm and standard deviation 7 cm, with correlation 0.5. A father is 189 cm. Predict his son's height. (182 cm) | `gaussian-2d` | Trap: the prediction line taken as the long axis of the ellipse. Edge: within the multivariate normal, uncorrelated means independent, and every linear combination is normal | N, N, X, M |
| 105 | `lsn.joint.correlated-normals` Building correlated normals | Construct correlated normals as $Y = \rho X + \sqrt{1 - \rho^2}\,Z$ (the $2 \times 2$ Cholesky factor), and show that normal marginals with zero correlation need not be independent | Make two standard normals with correlation 0.6 from independent ones. Then: $X \sim \mathcal N(0, 1)$ and $Y = SX$ with an independent random sign $S$. Both are normal with correlation 0. Independent? (No: $\lvert Y \rvert = \lvert X \rvert$) | `gaussian-2d` (Cholesky, show-why), `transform-grid` | Trap: normal marginals plus zero correlation taken as independence. Edge: joint normality is a property of the pair, not of each part; this is the gap the Gaussian copula papered over | X, C, N, M |

Transfer screens: lesson 105 is how a Monte Carlo risk engine simulates correlated assets; lesson 101 is the theory of auctions and the winner's curse; lesson 104 is why last year's top fund is expected to do less well this year.

Then the Level review and homework `set.joint.transform`.

**Level J4: Conditional expectation** (`lvl.joint.condexp`, 6 lessons)

| # | Lesson | Objective | Hook | Widgets | Trap and edge case | Quiz |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| 106 | `lsn.joint.given-event` Conditional expectation given an event | Compute $E[Y \mid A]$ as an average over the outcomes in $A$, and as the best constant guess for $Y$ inside $A$ | Roll two dice. Given that the larger is 4, what is the expected smaller? ($\frac{16}7$) | `cond-expectation` | Trap: the unconditional mean of the smaller die, $\frac{91}{36}$. Edge: $E[Y \mid A]$ for an event of probability 0 needs a density or a limit | N, X, C, N |
| 107 | `lsn.joint.given-rv` $E[Y \mid X]$ is a random variable | Compute $E[Y \mid X]$ as a function of $X$, and use "taking out what is known" and independence | Choose $X$ uniform on $(0, 1)$, then $Y$ uniform on $(0, X)$. What are $E[Y \mid X]$ and $E[Y]$? ($\frac X2$; $\frac14$) | `cond-expectation` (collapse) | Trap: $E[Y \mid X]$ thought of as a number. Edge: $E[Y \mid X] = E[Y]$ holds when $X$ and $Y$ are independent but does not imply independence | X, N, C, M |
| 108 | `lsn.joint.adams-law` Adam's law and random sums | Use $E[Y] = E[E[Y \mid X]]$, including $E\left[\sum_{i=1}^NX_i\right] = E[N]E[X]$ when $N$ is independent of the $X_i$ | A trader makes $N \sim \mathrm{Pois}(20)$ trades a day, each with expected P&L £50, independent of $N$. Expected daily P&L? (£1,000) | `cond-expectation`, `filtration-tree` (tower), `trial-simulator` | Trap: averaging the slice means without weighting by slice size. Edge: if the number of trades depends on their outcomes (stop after a loss), $E[N]E[X]$ can fail; lesson 144 says when it still holds | N, N, C, X |
| 109 | `lsn.joint.eves-law` Eve's law | Use $\mathrm{Var}(Y) = E[\mathrm{Var}(Y \mid X)] + \mathrm{Var}(E[Y \mid X])$ to split variance into within and between parts | In lesson 108 each trade's P&L has standard deviation £200. Standard deviation of daily P&L? (about £922) | `cond-expectation` (Eve bar) | Trap: keeping only the variance of the slice means, or only the variance inside slices. Edge: for a compound Poisson sum, $\mathrm{Var} = \lambda E\left[X^2\right]$ | N, X, N, M |
| 110 | `lsn.joint.conditioning-tool` Conditioning as a problem-solving tool | Compute expected waiting times and probabilities by conditioning on the first steps and solving for the unknown expectation | Expected number of rolls of a die until two sixes in a row? (42) | `first-step-solver` | Trap: 36, that is $\frac1{p^2}$; restarting from zero after a partial match when part of it survives. Edge: for $k$ in a row with success chance $p$ the answer is $\frac{1 - p^k}{qp^k}$ | N, X, C, N |
| 111 | `lsn.joint.best-predictor` The best predictor | Show $E[Y \mid X]$ minimises $E\left[(Y - g(X))^2\right]$ and its error is uncorrelated with every function of $X$, and compare it with the best straight line | $Y = X^2 + \varepsilon$ with $X$ symmetric about 0. Best straight-line prediction against best prediction? (A flat line at $E\left[X^2\right]$ against the curve $X^2$) | `cond-expectation` (projection view, MSE), `least-squares-fit`, `gaussian-2d` (regression line) | Trap: linear regression assumed to estimate $E[Y \mid X]$ whatever the relationship. Edge: for the bivariate normal the two coincide; the residual is uncorrelated with $X$ but need not be independent of it | C, N, M, X |

Transfer screens: lesson 111 is what every regression and every neural network trained on squared error estimates (1.6, 2.1); lesson 109 splits a strategy's risk into within-regime and between-regime parts.

Then the Level review and homework `set.joint.condexp`.

**Level J5: Inequalities** (`lvl.joint.inequalities`, 4 lessons)

| # | Lesson | Objective | Hook | Widgets | Trap and edge case | Quiz |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| 112 | `lsn.joint.markov-chebyshev` Markov's and Chebyshev's inequalities | Prove Markov's inequality from an indicator and Chebyshev's from Markov's, and find the distributions that make them tight | Average pay at a firm is £30,000. At most what share of staff can earn over £150,000? ($\frac15$) | `dist-explorer` (bounds), `mass-editor` | Trap: the bound read as an estimate of the tail. Edge: a two-point distribution attains Markov's bound, so it cannot be improved without more information | N, N, C, O |
| 113 | `lsn.joint.chernoff` Chernoff bounds | Bound tails by $P(X \ge a) \le \min_{t > 0}e^{-ta}M(t)$ and compare with Chebyshev and the exact tail | 100 fair coin tosses. Chance of at least 75 heads: exact, Chebyshev, Chernoff? (about $2.8 \times 10^{-7}$; at most 0.04; at most $2.1 \times 10^{-6}$) | `dist-explorer` (bounds) | Trap: thinking Chebyshev is close because it is a theorem. Edge: choosing $t$ to minimise the bound is the creative step; Hoeffding's version gives the generalisation bounds of learning theory (2.1) | N, N, C, M |
| 114 | `lsn.joint.jensen` Jensen's inequality | Prove $E[g(X)] \ge g(E[X])$ for convex $g$ from a supporting line, and apply it to options, logarithms and reciprocals | Two shares have the same expected price next month, but one is more uncertain. A call option pays $\max(S - K, 0)$. On which share is the call worth more in expectation? (The more uncertain one) | `convexity-chord` (random-variable mode), `rv-mapper` | Trap: $E[g(X)] = g(E[X])$. Edge: equality only when $g$ is linear on the support or $X$ is constant; for concave $g$ (log utility) the inequality flips | C, N, M, N |
| 115 | `lsn.joint.cauchy-schwarz` The Cauchy-Schwarz inequality | Prove $\lvert E[XY] \rvert \le \sqrt{E\left[X^2\right]E\left[Y^2\right]}$ from $E\left[(X - tY)^2\right] \ge 0$, and deduce $\lvert\rho\rvert \le 1$ | Can a sample correlation exceed 1? Drag points and try. (No) | `covariance-lab`, `derivation-steps` | Trap: an example offered as proof. Edge: equality exactly when one variable is a multiple of the other | O, C, N, M |

Transfer screens: lesson 114 is volatility drag and the value of optionality (1.7); lesson 113 sizes the number of trades needed before a hit rate can be trusted.

Then the Level review and homework `set.joint.inequalities`.

**Level J6: Laws of large numbers and the central limit theorem** (`lvl.joint.limits`, 7 lessons)

| # | Lesson | Objective | Hook | Widgets | Trap and edge case | Quiz |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| 116 | `lsn.joint.wlln` The weak law of large numbers | Prove the weak law from Chebyshev's inequality and use it to size samples, distinguishing convergence in probability from certainty | How many tosses guarantee, by Chebyshev, at least a 95% chance that the share of heads is within 0.01 of $\frac12$? What does the normal approximation say? (50,000; about 9,604) | `trial-simulator` (funnel), `sampling-lab` (LLN), `derivation-steps` | Trap: expecting small samples to match proportions (the law of small numbers). Edge: the strong law says more (averages converge along almost every path), stated here and proved in canon-gap P16 | N, O, C, N |
| 117 | `lsn.joint.counts-diverge` Proportions converge; counts do not | Show that $\frac{S_n}n \to \frac12$ while $\left\lvert S_n - \frac n2\right\rvert$ typically grows like $\sqrt n$, and refute the gambler's fallacy | After 1,000 tosses you are 20 heads short of half. Will the shortfall shrink? (Its expected value stays 20; only the proportion recovers) | `trial-simulator` (count-deviation trace) | Trap: "the law of averages will even it out". Edge: $E\left\lvert S_n - \frac n2\right\rvert \approx \sqrt{\frac n{2\pi}} \approx 12.6$ for $n = 1{,}000$, and it keeps growing | N, C, M, N |
| 118 | `lsn.joint.lln-fails` When the law fails | Recognise distributions whose averages do not settle (no mean) or settle very slowly (infinite variance) | Plot the running mean of a million Cauchy draws. Where does it settle? (Nowhere: the mean of $n$ Cauchy draws is again Cauchy) | `sampling-lab` (LLN, Cauchy) | Trap: the law applied without a finite mean. Edge: with a finite mean and infinite variance (a Pareto tail with index between 1 and 2) the average converges, slowly and without a normal limit | C, M, N, N |
| 119 | `lsn.joint.clt` The central limit theorem | State the central limit theorem, sketch its proof through MGFs, and predict how fast sums of skewed variables look normal | Predict the shape of the average of 2, 5 and 30 exponential draws. Then watch | `sampling-lab` (CLT), `galton-board`, `convolver` (repeat) | Trap: the theorem read as "the data become normal". Edge: the speed depends on skewness (Berry-Esseen); averages of Cauchy draws never become normal | C, N, M, X |
| 120 | `lsn.joint.normal-approx` Normal approximation and the continuity correction | Approximate binomial and Poisson probabilities by the normal with a continuity correction, and say when not to | A roulette player makes 1,000 even-money £1 bets, each won with chance $\frac{18}{38}$. Chance the player is ahead? (about 0.045) | `dist-explorer` (compare, continuity) | Trap: no continuity correction (0.042 instead of 0.045). Edge: the approximation is poor far in the tails and when $np$ is small (use the Poisson) | N, N, C, N |
| 121 | `lsn.joint.sampling-distributions` Sampling distributions: $\chi^2$ and $t$ | Use that for normal data $\bar X$ and $S^2$ are independent, $\frac{(n - 1)S^2}{\sigma^2} \sim \chi^2_{n-1}$, and $\frac{\bar X - \mu}{S/\sqrt n} \sim t_{n-1}$ | Standardise means of 5 normal draws with the sample standard deviation. Do 95% land within $\pm1.96$? (No: about 87.8%) | `sampling-lab` (t statistic), `dist-explorer` | Trap: the normal used where $\sigma$ is estimated from few points. Edge: the bridge to 1.6's intervals and tests | N, C, M, N |
| 122 | `lsn.joint.delta-method` The delta method | Approximate the standard deviation of $g(\bar X)$ by $\lvert g'(\mu)\rvert\frac\sigma{\sqrt n}$, and know when the approximation fails | A win rate is estimated as 0.2 from 400 trades (standard error 0.02). Standard error of the estimated log-odds? (0.125) | `taylor-builder` (delta method), `sampling-lab` | Trap: pushing the standard error through $g$ without the derivative. Edge: if $g'(\mu) = 0$ the first-order answer is 0 and the second-order term takes over | N, X, C, N |

Transfer screens: lesson 120 is the casino's edge and a market maker's P&L over many trades; lesson 116 sizes a backtest; lesson 122 gives the standard error of a Sharpe ratio (1.6, 2.3).

Then the Level review and homework `set.joint.limits`.

### Course Z: Markov Chains, Random Walks and Interview Problems (`crs.markov`, 8 levels, 46 lessons)

Probability in motion, and the problems that interviews love. Markov chains as linear algebra on probability vectors, with stationary distributions, absorption, walks on graphs, PageRank and a first Markov chain Monte Carlo sampler; random walks counted by reflection; fair games, stopping times and the optional stopping theorem; geometric probability; optimal stopping, a preview of Kelly betting, and streaks. Blitzstein and Hwang chapter 11 and section 12.1; Stat 110 lectures 31 to 33 [check 32]; beyond the book, Grinstead and Snell chapters 11 and 12, Mosteller, and the martingale chapters named in section 5.

Levels Z4b and Z4c, added by canon-gap P2 (adopted 7 October 2026), take chains and walks into continuous time: holding times and generators, flow balance, queues and hitting times; then Brownian motion, its martingales, exits from an interval with and without drift, reflection and the running maximum. Durrett, *Essentials of Stochastic Processes* (3rd edition), chapters 4 and 5, and Grimmett and Stirzaker, *Probability and Random Processes*, chapter 13 (section 5.5).

**Order and numbers.** The learner meets the levels in the order Z1, Z2, Z3, Z4, Z4b, Z4c, Z5, Z6: the new levels need Z1's chains, Z3's reflection and Brownian preview and Z4's optional stopping, and nothing in Z5 or Z6 needs them. Their lessons carry the reference numbers 157 to 168, not 146 onwards, because other files cite lessons 145 to 155 and level Z6 by number (`WIDGETS.md`, `stage1-finance-projects.md`, `stage1-dsa-cpp.md`, `stage2-ml-papers.md`, `stage3-stochastic-interview.md`, `canon-gap.md`). The `#` column is a reference label (section 0.3): the app numbers lessons by position, so on screen these are lessons 146 to 157 and Z5 and Z6 follow them.

**Level Z1: Markov chains** (`lvl.markov.chains`, 6 lessons)

| # | Lesson | Objective | Hook | Widgets | Trap and edge case | Quiz |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| 123 | `lsn.markov.property` The Markov property | State the Markov property, write a transition matrix with rows as "from", and compute multi-step probabilities by summing over paths | A market regime stays bull with chance 0.9 (else turns bear) and stays bear with chance 0.7. Today is bull. Chance of bull in two days? (0.84) | `markov-chain` (diagram, matrix, walker), `node-graph` | Trap: rows and columns swapped. Edge: the Markov property is conditional independence of future and past given the present, not independence of the days | N, A, C, M |
| 124 | `lsn.markov.n-step` $n$-step transitions | Compute $n$-step probabilities as entries of $P^n$ and distributions as row vectors times $P^n$ | Same chain, starting bull: chance of bull in 10 days? ($\frac34 + \frac14(0.6)^{10} \approx 0.752$) | `markov-chain` (population), `matrix-multiply` | Trap: squaring the entries instead of the matrix. Edge: the second eigenvalue, 0.6, sets how fast the starting state is forgotten | N, A, X, C |
| 125 | `lsn.markov.classification` Classifying states | Find communicating classes, classify states as recurrent or transient, and recognise irreducible chains | A five-state chain diagram: which states will the walker eventually leave for ever? | `markov-chain` (classes) | Trap: "a state with a loop to itself is recurrent". Edge: in a finite chain a class is recurrent exactly when no arrow leaves it | S, M, N, M |
| 126 | `lsn.markov.stationary` Stationary distributions | Solve $\pi P = \pi$ with $\sum_i\pi_i = 1$, read $\pi$ as long-run shares of time, and use the expected return time $\frac1{\pi_i}$ | In the bull-bear chain, what share of days are bull in the long run? Starting from a bear day, expected days until the next bear day? ($\frac34$; 4) | `markov-chain`, `eigen-finder`, `linear-dynamics` (Markov mode) | Trap: "stationary" read as "the walker stops moving". Edge: a chain with two closed classes has many stationary distributions | N, X, C, N |
| 127 | `lsn.markov.periodicity` Convergence and periodicity | State the convergence theorem for irreducible aperiodic finite chains, and recognise periodic chains that never converge | A walker on the corners of a square moves to a random neighbour each step, starting at corner 1. Where is it after 1,000 steps? (Corner 1 or 3, each with chance $\frac12$; never 2 or 4) | `markov-chain` | Trap: assuming every chain settles. Edge: a lazy step (stay put with chance $\frac12$) removes the period and leaves $\pi$ unchanged | C, N, M, N |
| 128 | `lsn.markov.reversibility` Reversibility and detailed balance | Find stationary distributions by detailed balance $\pi_ip_{ij} = \pi_jp_{ji}$, and recognise reversible chains | Ehrenfest's urns: 4 balls are split between two urns, and each step a random ball changes urn. Long-run chance that all 4 are in the left urn? ($\frac1{16}$) | `markov-chain` (flows) | Trap: the stationary distribution assumed uniform over the five states. Edge: detailed balance is sufficient, not necessary; every birth-death chain is reversible | N, X, A, M |

Transfer screens: lesson 123 is a regime-switching model; lesson 126 is long-run market share; lesson 128 is the property Markov chain Monte Carlo is built on (lesson 134).

Then the Level review and homework `set.markov.chains`.

**Level Z2: Markov chains at work** (`lvl.markov.applied`, 6 lessons)

| # | Lesson | Objective | Hook | Widgets | Trap and edge case | Quiz |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| 129 | `lsn.markov.graph-walks` Random walks on graphs | Show $\pi_i \propto \deg(i)$ for a random walk on an undirected graph, and use it for return times | A knight moves at random on an empty chessboard. Starting in a corner, expected number of moves to return to it? (168) | `markov-chain` (graph walk), `node-graph` | Trap: a uniform stationary distribution, giving 64. Edge: a corner has degree 2 and the degrees sum to 336, so $\pi_{\text{corner}} = \frac2{336}$ | N, N, X, M |
| 130 | `lsn.markov.pagerank` PageRank | Compute PageRank as the stationary distribution of a walk with teleporting, and explain why the damping makes it well defined | Four pages: A links to B and C, B to C, C to A, D to C. A and B each have one incoming link. Why does A rank nearly twice as high as B? (A's link comes from the top page, C; with damping 0.85 the ranks are 0.37, 0.20, 0.39, 0.04) | `markov-chain` (PageRank) | Trap: ranking by counting incoming links. Edge: without teleporting, a page with no outgoing links or a closed group traps the walker; damping fixes both | S, N, A, M |
| 131 | `lsn.markov.absorbing` Absorbing chains | Compute absorption probabilities and expected times to absorption with $N = (I - Q)^{-1}$, $B = NR$ and $N\mathbf 1$ | Each month a current loan stays current (90%), falls late (5%) or repays (5%); a late loan cures (30%), stays late (40%) or defaults (30%). Chance a current loan eventually defaults? Expected months until it repays or defaults? ($\frac13$; $\frac{130}9 \approx 14.4$) | `markov-chain` (absorbing), `first-step-solver`, `matrix` | Trap: leaving out an absorbing state's boundary value. Edge: the entries of $N$ are expected visits to each transient state | N, A, N, C |
| 132 | `lsn.markov.credit-migration` Credit-rating migration | Compute multi-year default probabilities from an annual rating matrix, and compare them with multiples of the one-year rate | Each year a B-rated bond defaults with chance 10% and moves up to A with chance 10%; an A bond defaults with chance 1% and falls to B with chance 9%. Chance a B bond defaults within 5 years? (0.350, not $5 \times 10\% = 0.5$) | `markov-chain` (ratings preset), `matrix-multiply` | Trap: multiplying the one-year rate by the number of years. Edge: for the A bond the naive rule underestimates (0.108 against 0.05), because downgrades come first; real ratings show momentum, which breaks the Markov assumption | N, A, N, M |
| 133 | `lsn.markov.patterns-as-chains` Patterns as chains | Build the chain of partial matches for a pattern, compute expected waiting times, and solve races between patterns | Expected tosses until HTH? Until HHT? Which appears first more often? (10; 8; HHT, with chance $\frac23$) | `first-step-solver` (pattern automaton), `trial-strip` | Trap: resetting to the start when the last tosses still begin the pattern (after HHH you still have HH). Edge: a race is not decided by comparing expected times; it needs the joint chain | N, N, A, C |
| 134 | `lsn.markov.metropolis` Sampling with a Markov chain: Metropolis | Build a chain with a given stationary distribution by proposing moves and accepting with chance $\min\left(1, \frac{\pi_j}{\pi_i}\right)$, and check it by detailed balance | You can compute weights proportional to 1, 2, 3, 4, 5 for five states, but cannot sample them directly. Design a walk whose long-run visits come in those proportions | `markov-chain` (Metropolis) [priority P2 to P0] | Trap: accepting every proposal, so the walk follows the proposal, not the target. Edge: only ratios of the target are needed, never its normalising constant, which is why the method fits Bayesian models (canon-gap P15); successive samples are correlated | X, C, N, M |

Transfer screens: lesson 132 is a bank's credit-risk model; lesson 130 is an eigenvector computation on a web-scale graph; lesson 134 is how posterior distributions in Bayesian machine learning are sampled.

Then the Level review and homework `set.markov.applied`.

**Level Z3: Random walks** (`lvl.markov.walks`, 6 lessons)

| # | Lesson | Objective | Hook | Widgets | Trap and edge case | Quiz |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| 135 | `lsn.markov.walk-paths` Simple random walk: paths and counts | Compute $P(S_n = k) = \binom n{(n + k)/2}2^{-n}$ for a symmetric walk, with its parity condition | After 10 fair $\pm1$ steps, chance the walk is back at 0? ($\frac{252}{1024} \approx 0.246$) | `lattice-walk` (build, count), `pascal-triangle` | Trap: every endpoint taken as equally likely; a return at an odd time. Edge: $P(S_{2n} = 0) \approx \frac1{\sqrt{\pi n}}$ (1.3 lesson 98): returns grow rarer but never stop | N, X, C, N |
| 136 | `lsn.markov.reflection` The reflection principle | Count paths that touch a level by reflecting the part before the first touch, and derive the distribution of the running maximum | From height 2, how many 10-step paths end at height 4 and touch 0 on the way? (45 of the 210 paths from 2 to 4) | `lattice-walk` (reflect) | Trap: reflecting the part after the touch. Edge: $P(M_n \ge a) = P(S_n \ge a) + P(S_n > a)$; for 10 steps $P(M_{10} \ge 3) = \frac{11}{32}$ | N, N, C, O |
| 137 | `lsn.markov.ballot` The ballot theorem | Prove that if A gets $a$ votes and B gets $b < a$, counted in random order, A is strictly ahead throughout with chance $\frac{a - b}{a + b}$ | A gets 6 votes and B gets 4. Chance A is strictly ahead throughout the count? ($\frac15$) | `lattice-walk` (ballot) | Trap: answering with $P(\text{A wins}) = 1$, or $\frac a{a + b}$. Edge: the bad paths that start with a vote for A pair off one to one with the paths that start with a vote for B: the reflection trick again | N, X, C, N |
| 138 | `lsn.markov.catalan` Catalan numbers | Count paths that never go below zero as $\frac1{n + 1}\binom{2n}n$ by reflection | 5 buy orders and 5 sell orders of one lot each arrive in some order. In how many orders does inventory never go negative? (42) | `lattice-walk` (Catalan) | Trap: $\binom{10}5 = 252$. Edge: the same numbers count valid bracket sequences and binary trees; reflection gives $\binom{2n}n - \binom{2n}{n + 1}$ | N, X, C, M |
| 139 | `lsn.markov.recurrence` Returning home: recurrence and first passage | Show the symmetric walk on a line returns to 0 with probability 1 yet has infinite expected return time, and compare dimensions | A fair walk starts at 0. Will it come back? How long do you expect to wait? (Yes, with probability 1; infinitely long on average) | `lattice-walk` (first passage), `path-simulator` | Trap: "it surely returns, so the expected wait is finite". Edge: Pólya's theorem: the walk returns surely in one and two dimensions, with chance about 0.34 in three | C, N, M, N |
| 140 | `lsn.markov.brownian-preview` From random walks to Brownian motion | Scale a walk by $\frac1{\sqrt n}$ in space and $\frac1n$ in time, connect the limit to the central limit theorem, and meet the arcsine law | In a fair game of 1,000 rounds, which is more likely: that you lead about half the time, or almost all or almost none of the time? (The extremes: leading less than 10% or more than 90% of the time has chance about 0.41, against about 0.13 for 40% to 60%) | `path-simulator` (scaled walk), `lattice-walk` | Trap: expecting the lead to change hands often. Edge: scaling space by $\frac1n$ instead of $\frac1{\sqrt n}$ gives a flat line; Brownian paths are continuous and differentiable nowhere (3.2) | C, N, M, N |

Transfer screens: lesson 136 prices barrier features on a binomial tree (1.7); lesson 138 counts the inventory paths of a market maker; lesson 140 is the step from 1.5 to stochastic calculus (3.2).

Then the Level review and homework `set.markov.walks`.

**Level Z4: Fair games and stopping times** (`lvl.markov.martingales`, 5 lessons)

| # | Lesson | Objective | Hook | Widgets | Trap and edge case | Quiz |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| 141 | `lsn.markov.martingale` Fair games: martingales | Check the martingale property $E[M_{n+1} \mid M_0, \dots, M_n] = M_n$, and recognise martingales built from walks, urns and Bayesian updating | Which of these are fair games: a fair walk $S_n$; $S_n^2$; $S_n^2 - n$; the share of red in Pólya's urn; your posterior probability of a hypothesis as evidence arrives? (All but $S_n^2$) | `filtration-tree`, `path-simulator` | Trap: constant expectation taken as enough to be a martingale. Edge: a posterior probability is a martingale: you cannot expect your future belief to differ from your present one | M, C, X, N |
| 142 | `lsn.markov.optional-stopping` Stopping rules and the doubling strategy | State the optional stopping theorem's conditions, and show where the doubling strategy breaks them | Double your stake after every loss on a fair coin until the first win: you always end £1 up. With £1,023 you can afford to lose, what is your expected profit? (0: you win £1 with chance $\frac{1023}{1024}$ and lose £1,023 otherwise) | `path-simulator`, `game-lab` | Trap: "the game stops for sure, so the strategy wins for sure". Edge: optional stopping holds for bounded stopping times, or for bounded steps with a finite expected stopping time; doubling needs unlimited credit | N, C, M, N |
| 143 | `lsn.markov.ruin-by-martingale` Gambler's ruin by martingales | Derive ruin probabilities and expected durations from $S_n$, $S_n^2 - n$ and $\left(\frac qp\right)^{S_n}$ with optional stopping | Start at 0 with fair $\pm1$ steps; stop at $+3$ or $-7$. Chance of $+3$ first? Expected number of steps? ($\frac7{10}$; 21) | `path-simulator` (barrier), `first-step-solver` | Trap: an expected duration of $a + b = 10$. Edge: for a biased walk, $S_n - (p - q)n$ gives the expected duration (homework Z4) | N, N, X, C |
| 144 | `lsn.markov.wald` Wald's identity | Use $E[S_T] = E[T]E[X]$ for a stopping time $T$ with $E[T] < \infty$, and spot stopping rules that peek ahead | Roll a die until the first six, adding every roll including the six. Expected total? (21) | `trial-strip`, `cond-expectation` (random sum) | Trap: $5 \times 3.5 + 6 = 23.5$; the rolls before the six average 3, not 3.5. Edge: Wald fails for a rule that looks at the next roll before deciding to stop | N, C, N, M |
| 145 | `lsn.markov.abracadabra` Pattern waiting times by fair betting | Compute expected waiting times for patterns with the gambling-team martingale: the sum of $m^k$ over every $k$ for which the pattern's first $k$ letters equal its last $k$ | A monkey types capital letters at random. Expected keystrokes until ABRACADABRA? ($26^{11} + 26^4 + 26$) | `trial-strip` (pattern race), `first-step-solver`, `derivation-steps` | Trap: $26^{11}$ alone. Edge: the same rule gives HTH 10 and HHT 8 in two lines, where lesson 133 needed a chain | N, X, C, N |

Transfer screens: lesson 142 is why no betting system beats a fair game, and why a fair price process is a martingale (1.7, 3.2); lesson 143 sizes the risk of ruin.

Then the Level review and homework `set.markov.martingales`.

**Level Z4b: Continuous-time chains** (`lvl.markov.continuous-chains`, 5 lessons; canon-gap P2)

| # | Lesson | Objective | Hook | Widgets | Trap and edge case | Quiz |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| 157 | `lsn.markov.ctmc-hold-jump` Continuous-time chains: hold, then jump | Describe a continuous-time Markov chain by its rates: it holds in state $i$ for an $\mathrm{Expo}(q_i)$ time, $q_i = \sum_{j \ne i}q_{ij}$, then jumps to $j$ with chance $\frac{q_{ij}}{q_i}$, independently of how long it held; and explain why memorylessness forces exponential holding times | A market-data feed is Live, Lagging or Down. Live turns Lagging at rate 2 an hour; Lagging recovers to Live at rate 3 an hour and fails to Down at rate 1 an hour; Down is restored to Live at rate 4 an hour. The feed has just started lagging. Expected time until it changes state? Chance the change is a recovery? (15 minutes; $\frac34$) | `ctmc-clock` (racing clocks), `markov-chain` (continuous time) [mode to add], `trial-strip` | Trap: the faster clock's mean, 20 minutes, taken as the expected time; the race ends at the first ring, an $\mathrm{Expo}(3 + 1)$ time (lesson 73). Edge: a rate is not a probability (a rate of 3 an hour is not a chance of 3); the jump chain never jumps from a state to itself; a state with no exits is absorbing | N, N, C, M |
| 158 | `lsn.markov.generator` The generator and the forward equation | Write the generator $Q$ (rates off the diagonal, $-q_i$ on it, rows summing to 0), derive $P'(t) = P(t)Q$ for a two-state chain and solve it with 1.3 level I6's integrating factor, and compute $P(t) = e^{tQ}$ for small chains by 1.4's diagonalisation | The feed's network link on its own: when up it fails at rate 2 a day; when down it is repaired at rate 6 a day. It is up now. Chance it is up 6 hours from now? In the long run? ($\frac34 + \frac14e^{-2} \approx 0.784$; $\frac34$) | `markov-chain` (generator) [mode to add], `plot-2d`, `derivation-steps` | Trap: $Q$ read as a transition matrix, or $P(t) = I + tQ$ used for every $t$ (at 6 hours it gives 0.5, not 0.784; it holds only as $t \to 0$). Edge: P8 (differential equations) was not adopted, so larger chains get $e^{tQ}$ only by diagonalising $Q$, never as a system of equations; the eigenvalue $-8$ sets how fast the start is forgotten, as 0.6 did in lesson 124 | N, X, A, C |
| 159 | `lsn.markov.ctmc-stationary` Long-run shares of time | Solve $\pi Q = 0$ with $\sum_i\pi_i = 1$ as a balance of flows (out of $i$, $\pi_iq_i$; into $i$, $\sum_{j \ne i}\pi_jq_{ji}$), read $\pi$ as long-run shares of time, and relate it to the jump chain's stationary distribution $\tilde\pi$ by $\pi_i \propto \frac{\tilde\pi_i}{q_i}$ | In lesson 157's feed, what share of the time is it Down in the long run? ($\frac1{13} \approx 7.7\%$; Live $\frac8{13}$, Lagging $\frac4{13}$) | `markov-chain` (generator, flows) [mode to add], `ctmc-clock` | Trap: the jump chain's stationary distribution taken as the share of time, giving Down $\frac19$: a ninth of the visits are to Down, but each lasts 15 minutes against 30 for a visit to Live. Edge: the mean time between entries to $i$ is $\frac1{\pi_iq_i}$ (lesson 126's return time in continuous form): $\frac{13}4$ hours for Down | N, A, C, N |
| 160 | `lsn.markov.birth-death` Birth-death chains and queues | Find stationary distributions of birth-death chains by balancing the flow across each cut, $\pi_{n+1}\mu_{n+1} = \pi_n\lambda_n$, and apply it to the single-server queue, $\pi_n = (1 - \rho)\rho^n$ when $\rho = \frac\lambda\mu < 1$ | Limit orders join the queue at one price level at rate 8 a minute, and the order at the front is filled at rate 10 a minute (ignore cancellations). Long-run chance the level is empty? Mean number of orders resting there? ($\frac15$; 4) | `markov-chain` (birth-death) [mode to add], `ctmc-clock`, `stat-chart` | Trap: a stationary distribution claimed when $\rho \ge 1$; at 10 arrivals a minute against 10 fills there is none, and the queue wanders without bound. Edge: an order rests for $\frac1{\mu - \lambda} = \frac12$ minute on average, the mean number divided by the arrival rate (Little's law, stated); real queues also lose orders to cancellation from any position, so the departure rate grows with the queue | N, X, C, M |
| 161 | `lsn.markov.ctmc-hitting` Hitting times in continuous time | Compute expected hitting times by first-step analysis with the holding time added, $h_i = \frac1{q_i} + \sum_{j \ne i}\frac{q_{ij}}{q_i}h_j$ (equivalently $\sum_jq_{ij}h_j = -1$ away from the target), and absorption probabilities from the jump chain | Lesson 157's feed is Live. Expected time until it first goes Down? (3 hours) | `first-step-solver` (continuous time) [mode to add], `markov-chain` (generator) [mode to add] | Trap: each jump counted as one unit of time: from Live the feed makes 8 jumps on average before Down, a count of jumps, not hours. Edge: a check by lesson 159: entries to Down are $\frac{13}4$ hours apart on average, which is 15 minutes Down plus 3 hours from Live back to Down | N, A, N, C |

Transfer screens: lesson 157 is a reliability model of a trading system's data feed; lesson 160 is the queue at a price level, where order arrivals are modelled as Poisson streams (Avellaneda and Stoikov, 3.1); lesson 161 sizes the expected time to an outage.

Then the Level review and homework `set.markov.continuous-chains`.

**Level Z4c: Brownian motion** (`lvl.markov.brownian`, 7 lessons; canon-gap P2)

| # | Lesson | Objective | Hook | Widgets | Trap and edge case | Quiz |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| 162 | `lsn.markov.brownian-motion` Brownian motion | State the definition of standard Brownian motion ($B_0 = 0$; independent increments; $B_t - B_s \sim \mathcal N(0, t - s)$; continuous paths), derive $\mathrm{Cov}(B_s, B_t) = \min(s, t)$ by writing the later value as the earlier one plus an increment, and state that its quadratic variation over $[0, t]$ is $t$ | $\mathrm{Var}(B_1 + B_4)$? $\mathrm{Corr}(B_1, B_4)$? (7; $\frac12$) | `brownian-path` (refine), `path-simulator` (scaled walk), `covariance-lab` | Trap: $B_1$ and $B_4$ treated as independent, giving 5. Edge: on finer and finer grids over $[0, t]$ the sum of squared increments tends to $t$ while the sum of absolute increments grows without bound, so paths are continuous but nowhere smooth (stated here, proved in 3.2 level BR4); lesson 140's scaled walk converges to $B$ (stated) | N, N, C, M |
| 163 | `lsn.markov.brownian-martingales` Martingales of Brownian motion | Show from independent increments and the normal MGF that $B_t$, $B_t^2 - t$ and $e^{\theta B_t - \theta^2t/2}$ are martingales given the path so far, and test other candidates | Which are martingales: $B_t^2$; $B_t^2 - t$; $e^{2B_t - 2t}$; $e^{2B_t - t}$; $tB_t$? ($B_t^2 - t$ and $e^{2B_t - 2t}$) | `brownian-path` (average of paths), `derivation-steps` | Trap: a constant mean taken as enough: $E[tB_t] = 0$ for every $t$, but given the path to time $s$ its expectation is $tB_s$, not $sB_s$. Edge: the $-\frac{\theta^2}2t$ is lesson 86's lognormal correction; every $\theta$ gives a martingale, which lesson 166 uses to cancel a drift | M, X, N, C |
| 164 | `lsn.markov.brownian-exit` Leaving an interval | Use optional stopping on $B_t$ and $B_t^2 - t$, with the theorem's conditions checked, to show that Brownian motion from 0 reaches $a$ before $-b$ with chance $\frac b{a + b}$ and leaves $(-b, a)$ after an expected time $ab$ | A P&L moves as Brownian motion with no drift and variance 1 a day, from 0, with a stop-loss at $-2$ and a target at $+3$. Chance it reaches the target first? Expected days until it stops? ($\frac25$; 6) | `brownian-path` (barriers), `path-simulator` (barrier) | Trap: optional stopping applied with no stop-loss: "stop at $+3$" would give $E[B_T] = 0$, yet $B_T = 3$ every time; the theorem fails because $T$ has infinite mean and the path is unbounded below. Edge: the answers are lesson 143's for the walk ($\frac7{10}$ and 21 for $+3$ and $-7$): the walk's two martingales survive the limit, so its formulas do | N, N, C, M |
| 165 | `lsn.markov.brownian-reflection` The running maximum by reflection | Derive $P(M_t \ge a) = 2P(B_t \ge a)$ for $M_t = \max_{s \le t}B_s$ and $a > 0$ by reflecting the path after its first visit to $a$, and deduce that $M_t$ has the law of $\lvert B_t \rvert$ and that the first-passage time $T_a$ has $P(T_a \le t) = 2\left(1 - \Phi\left(\frac a{\sqrt t}\right)\right)$ | Lesson 164's P&L with no stops: chance it stands at $+3$ or more at some time in the next 4 days? ($2(1 - \Phi(1.5)) \approx 0.134$) | `brownian-path` (reflect), `lattice-walk` (reflect) | Trap: $P(B_4 \ge 3) \approx 0.067$, forgetting the paths that touch 3 and fall back. Edge: lesson 136's $P(M_n \ge a) = P(S_n \ge a) + P(S_n > a)$ becomes $2P(B_t \ge a)$ because $P(B_t = a) = 0$; $E[M_t] = \sqrt{\frac{2t}\pi}$; $T_a$ is finite with probability 1 yet has infinite mean (lesson 139 in continuous time) | N, X, C, N |
| 166 | `lsn.markov.drifted-exit` Drift: the exponential martingale | For $X_t = \mu t + \sigma B_t$ with $\mu \ne 0$, find the $\theta$ that makes $e^{-\theta X_t}$ a martingale ($\theta = \frac{2\mu}{\sigma^2}$), and use it and $X_t - \mu t$ with optional stopping to find the chance of reaching $a$ before $-b$ and the expected exit time | A strategy's P&L drifts at 0.5 a day with variance 1 a day, from 0, with a stop-loss at $-2$ and a target at $+2$. Chance it reaches the target first? Expected days until it stops? ($\frac1{1 + e^{-2}} \approx 0.881$; $4\tanh 1 \approx 3.05$) | `brownian-path` (drift, barriers), `derivation-steps` | Trap: $e^{X_t}$ or $X_t$ taken as the martingale, or the driftless answers $\frac12$ and 4 kept; $\theta = \frac\mu{\sigma^2}$, a dropped factor 2, gives 0.731. Edge: as $\mu \to 0$ the answers tend to $\frac b{a + b}$ and $\frac{ab}{\sigma^2}$; with $\mu < 0$ and no stop-loss the chance of ever reaching $a$ is $e^{2\mu a/\sigma^2}$, lesson 143's $\left(\frac qp\right)^{S_n}$ in continuous time (3.2 lesson 50 proves the limit) | N, N, X, C |
| 167 | `lsn.markov.brownian-two-times` Brownian motion at two times | Answer questions about $(B_s, B_t)$ by rewriting them in independent increments or as a correlated normal pair (lesson 105), including $P(X > 0, Y > 0) = \frac14 + \frac{\arcsin\rho}{2\pi}$ for standard normals with correlation $\rho$, by rotational symmetry | Lesson 164's P&L is up after 1 day. Chance it is still up after 3 days? ($\frac12 + \frac1\pi\arcsin\frac1{\sqrt3} \approx 0.696$) | `brownian-path` (two times), `gaussian-2d`, `region-select` | Trap: $\frac12$, as if the two days were independent. Edge: in coordinates where the pair is two independent standard normals, the event is a wedge of angle $\frac\pi2 + \arcsin\rho$, the creative step; $\rho = 0$ gives $\frac14$ and $\rho \to 1$ gives $\frac12$ | N, X, C, N |
| 168 | `lsn.markov.continuous-which-tool` Choosing the tool in continuous time | Classify a fresh continuous-time problem by the tool that cracks it (an exponential race, flow balance, first step with holding times, independent increments, a martingale with optional stopping, reflection), and check each answer by a second route | Three problems: two servers fail after independent exponential times with means 1 hour and 2 hours: expected time until both have failed; Brownian motion from 0: chance it reaches $+1$ before $-4$; chance it reaches $+3$ at some time before time 9. Which tool for each? (A race, then memorylessness, $\frac73$ hours; optional stopping, $\frac45$; reflection, $2(1 - \Phi(1)) \approx 0.317$) | `card-sort`, `ctmc-clock`, `brownian-path` | Trap: reaching for a simulation or a full distribution first. Edge: two tools that agree are the cheapest check: $E[\max] = E[X] + E[Y] - E[\min] = 1 + 2 - \frac23$ confirms the race; lesson 143's walk confirms $\frac45$ | S, N, N, C |

Transfer screens: lesson 164 sets a stop-loss and a target on a P&L; lesson 165 is the closed form behind barrier and one-touch prices (3.2); lesson 166 sizes the chance a strategy with an edge reaches its target before its stop (3.3 lesson 33 drills it at interview speed).

Then the Level review and homework `set.markov.brownian`.

**Level Z5: Geometric probability** (`lvl.markov.geometric`, 5 lessons)

| # | Lesson | Objective | Hook | Widgets | Trap and edge case | Quiz |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| 146 | `lsn.markov.area` Probability as area | Compute probabilities for uniform points in a square as areas of regions, including regions bounded by curves | Two numbers are uniform on $[0, 1]$. Chance their product is below $\frac12$? ($\frac12 + \frac12\ln 2 \approx 0.847$) | `geo-prob`, `region-select` | Trap: the uniform measure put on the wrong space (on the product, not the square). Edge: the meeting problem of 1.3 lesson 135 is the same idea with a band | N, R, C, N |
| 147 | `lsn.markov.broken-stick` The broken stick | Find the chance that three pieces of a randomly broken stick form a triangle, and the variant in which the longer piece is broken again | Break a stick at two uniform points. Chance the three pieces form a triangle? ($\frac14$) | `geo-prob` (broken stick) | Trap: $\frac12$. Edge: breaking once and then breaking the longer piece at a uniform point gives $2\ln 2 - 1 \approx 0.386$ | N, R, C, N |
| 148 | `lsn.markov.semicircle` Points on a circle | Show that $n$ uniform points on a circle lie in some semicircle with chance $\frac n{2^{n-1}}$, by splitting into disjoint events | Three random points on a circle. Chance their triangle contains the centre? ($\frac14$) | `geo-prob` (circle) [mode to add], `indicator-board` | Trap: $\frac12$, or adding overlapping semicircle events. Edge: the events "every point lies in the semicircle clockwise from point $i$" are disjoint: the creative step | N, X, C, N |
| 149 | `lsn.markov.buffon` Buffon's needle | Derive $P(\text{cross}) = \frac{2\ell}{\pi d}$ from the joint distribution of position and angle, and the expected number of crossings of any curve by linearity | A needle of length 1 is dropped on a floor of boards 1 wide. Chance it crosses a crack? ($\frac2\pi \approx 0.637$) | `geo-prob` (Buffon) | Trap: ignoring the angle. Edge: a bent needle of total length $\ell$ crosses $\frac{2\ell}{\pi d}$ cracks on average, whatever its shape (Buffon's noodle) | N, X, C, M |
| 150 | `lsn.markov.bertrand` Bertrand's paradox | Show that "a random chord" has different answers under different constructions, and state the construction a model must name | A random chord of a circle: chance it is longer than a side of the inscribed equilateral triangle? ($\frac13$, $\frac12$ or $\frac14$, depending on how the chord is chosen) | `geo-prob` (Bertrand) | Trap: believing there is one answer. Edge: "uniform" must name its variable: a flat prior on $p$ is not flat on the log-odds (1.6) | S, C, M, N |

Transfer screens: lesson 149 is Monte Carlo estimation in its oldest form; lesson 150 is why every model must state its parametrisation and its prior.

Then the Level review and homework `set.markov.geometric`.

**Level Z6: Games, stopping and betting** (`lvl.markov.games`, 6 lessons)

| # | Lesson | Objective | Hook | Widgets | Trap and edge case | Quiz |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| 151 | `lsn.markov.dice-stopping` Optimal stopping by backward induction | Find the optimal stopping rule and its value by working backwards from the last decision | Roll a die up to three times; you may stop after any roll and take its value. Best strategy and its value? (Stop on 5 or 6 after the first roll, on 4 or more after the second: $\frac{14}3$) | `game-lab` (dice stop, induction) | Trap: one threshold for every roll. Edge: the threshold rises with the rolls left; with unlimited rolls and a cost per roll the rule becomes a single threshold | N, N, C, X |
| 152 | `lsn.markov.secretary` The secretary problem | Derive the success chance of "reject the first $r$, then take the first candidate better than all before" and its optimum near $\frac ne$ | 100 candidates arrive in random order; you must accept or reject each on the spot. How do you maximise the chance of hiring the best? (Reject the first 37: success chance about 0.371) | `game-lab` (secretary) | Trap: expecting the chance to shrink to 0 as $n$ grows. Edge: the chance tends to $\frac1e$ for every large $n$ | N, C, X, N |
| 153 | `lsn.markov.two-envelopes` The two-envelope paradox | Find the error in the switching argument and resolve it with a proper prior | One envelope holds twice the other. You open one and see £100. "The other holds £50 or £200, equally likely, so switching gains on average." Where is the error? | `game-lab` (two envelopes) | Trap: treating one amount as both fixed and random in the same argument. Edge: with any proper prior, switching helps for some amounts and hurts for others, and gains nothing on average | C, M, O, N |
| 154 | `lsn.markov.kelly-preview` Betting with an edge: the Kelly fraction | Compare long-run growth $g(f) = p\ln(1 + f) + q\ln(1 - f)$ for different bet fractions, and see that betting more than Kelly lowers growth | A coin lands heads 60% of the time and pays even money. Bet 20% of wealth each time, or 50%? (Growth about 2.0% a bet against $-3.4\%$: after 100 bets the median wealth is about 7.5 times the start at 20%, and shrinks at 50%) | `game-lab` (Kelly) [priority P2 to P0] | Trap: "a bigger bet on an edge makes more money". Edge: expected wealth is maximised by betting everything, while the chance of eventual ruin tends to 1; the full treatment is in 3.3 | N, C, N, M |
| 155 | `lsn.markov.streaks` Streaks | Compute the chance of a run of $k$ successes in $n$ trials with a chain on the current run length, and judge whether a streak is evidence of skill | A trader with no edge has 250 independent days, each a win with chance $\frac12$. Chance of at least one run of 7 winning days? (about 0.63) | `markov-chain` (run-length preset) [mode to add], `trial-strip`, `code-cell` | Trap: "7 wins in a row has chance $\frac1{128}$, so it is rare", ignoring the many places a run can start. Edge: the expected longest run in $n$ fair trials grows like $\log_2n$ (about 7.3 for 250) | N, C, N, K |
| 156 | `lsn.markov.which-tool` Choosing the tool | Classify a fresh problem by the tool that cracks it (symmetry, complement, indicators, conditioning or first step, a chain, a martingale), and check the answer with a second tool | Three problems: the expected number of distinct faces in 10 rolls; the chance the first ace comes before the first king; the chance of 3 heads before 2 tails. Which tool for each? (Indicators, about 5.03; symmetry, $\frac12$; the problem of points, $\frac5{16}$) | `card-sort`, `indicator-board`, `first-step-solver` | Trap: reaching for the full distribution first. Edge: two tools that agree are the cheapest check there is | S, N, N, C |

Transfer screens: lesson 155 is the base rate behind every "hot hand" on a trading desk; lesson 151 is the logic of exercising an American option (1.7); lesson 154 previews position sizing (3.3).

Then the Level review and homework `set.markov.games`.

---

## 3. Homework blueprint per level

### 3.0 Rules for every set

- **Issue, due and marking** follow ASSESSMENT.md 2.5: issued when the level's last lesson is done, due 7 days later, no feedback before submission, code items run their visible examples freely and their hidden tests at submission, long-form items self-marked against the rubric with Bertie's suggested mark and evidence quote beside the learner's.
- **Size and mix.** Levels of 4 or 5 lessons get **8 items: 3 at difficulty 3, 3 at difficulty 4, 2 at difficulty 5**, about 2 hours. Levels of 6 or 7 lessons get **10 items: 4 at difficulty 3, 4 at difficulty 4, 2 at difficulty 5**, about 2 hours 30 minutes. Probability sets run longer than calculus sets of the same size because most items need a model built before any computation. Exactly one long-form item (a derivation, proof or written explanation with a 10-point rubric, usually difficulty 4) and exactly one code drill (difficulty 3 or 4) sit inside those counts. At least half the items are generator slots, so a redo is a fresh variant. At most two choice items per set. Items marked "exact" ban the calculator, so the set rehearses the finals-style paper.
- **Story variety.** Each set mixes at least three settings among dice, cards, coins, urns, people, trades, defaults and arrivals (widget catalogue section 9, rule 5), so a set tests the idea rather than the wording.
- **Long-form marking.** Every rubric has 4 to 10 points totalling 10 marks, and each point names the model-answer lines that evidence it (CONTENT-FORMAT.md 3.4). 7 of 10 is the bar wherever an outcome cites a rubric.
- **Code drills** list their hidden tests here so CI can check that the reference solution passes and each known-wrong solution fails its named test (CONTENT-FORMAT.md 3.9). All 29 drills use the standard library only (`math`, `fractions`, `itertools`, and `zqtest`'s seeded generator for any randomness) and are written to be `micropython-ok`; CI confirms the tag per drill. Exact answers compare as `Fraction`s; floating-point answers use `zqtest`'s `isclose` with relative tolerance $10^{-9}$ unless the test states another. Tests on a simulation fix the seed and check the estimate against the exact value within 4 standard errors, so they are deterministic.
- **Resources held back.** No item reproduces a Stat 110 final or a Zhou problem (decision 13); items "after" a Blitzstein and Hwang or Mosteller problem are recreated with new numbers and wording (CONTENT-FORMAT.md 9.4).

Below, each level gives its set id, item mix, long-form item, code drill with its hidden tests, and two example problems in full: example A is the long-form item with its model answer and rubric, example B a difficulty-5 item with its worked answer and decision point.

### 3.1 The 29 sets

**N1 Outcomes and the naive definition** (`set.count.naive`). 10 items (4 d3, 4 d4, 2 d5). Long-form: the Bose-Einstein argument. Code drill `drl.prob.enumerate` (d3): `prob(event, n_dice)` enumerates the ordered outcomes of `n_dice` fair dice and returns a `Fraction`. Hidden tests: a total of 10 with three dice gives $\frac18$; a total of 9 gives $\frac{25}{216}$; "all different" with three dice gives $\frac59$; a total of 13 with two dice gives 0; `n_dice < 1` raises `ValueError`. Known-wrong: enumerating unordered multisets (`combinations_with_replacement`) returns $\frac3{28}$ for a total of 10 and fails the first test.

- *Example A (long-form, d4).* Two numbers are drawn from $\{1, \dots, n\}$ with replacement, and only the unordered pair is recorded. (a) How many different records are possible? (b) Find the probability of each record, and explain why the naive definition applied to records gives wrong answers.
  *Model answer.* (1) A record is a pair $\{i, j\}$ with $i < j$ or a double $\{i, i\}$: $\binom n2 + n = \binom{n + 1}2$ records (stars and bars gives the same, $\binom{n + 2 - 1}2$). (2) The draws themselves are ordered pairs $(i, j)$, $n^2$ of them, equally likely because each draw is uniform and the draws are independent. (3) The record $\{i, i\}$ comes from one ordered pair, so it has probability $\frac1{n^2}$. (4) A record $\{i, j\}$ with $i \ne j$ comes from $(i, j)$ and $(j, i)$, so it has probability $\frac2{n^2}$. (5) Check: $n \cdot \frac1{n^2} + \binom n2\frac2{n^2} = \frac1n + \frac{n - 1}n = 1$. (6) The naive definition on records would give each one $\frac1{\binom{n + 1}2}$, too much for doubles and too little for mixed pairs: records are events of different sizes in the equally likely space, not outcomes.
  *Rubric (10).* 2: the count of records (line 1). 2: the ordered pairs as the equally likely space, with the reason (line 2). 1: doubles (line 3). 2: mixed pairs (line 4). 1: the check (line 5). 2: why the naive definition fails (line 6).
- *Example B (d5).* A fair die is rolled four times. Find the probability that the results never decrease (each roll is at least the one before).
  *Answer.* The equally likely space is the $6^4 = 1{,}296$ ordered sequences. A non-decreasing sequence is fixed by which values appear and how often, so non-decreasing sequences correspond one to one with multisets of size 4 from $\{1, \dots, 6\}$: $\binom{6 + 4 - 1}4 = \binom94 = 126$ of them. The probability is $\frac{126}{1{,}296} = \frac7{72} \approx 0.097$.
