Decision point: multisets are the wrong space for probability (example A) but the right tool for counting this event inside the right space, because each multiset is exactly one ordered outcome here.

**N2 Stories and symmetry** (`set.count.stories`). 8 items (3 d3, 3 d4, 2 d5). Long-form: a story proof. Code drill `drl.prob.birthday` (d3): `p_shared(k, days=365)`, `min_group(target, days=365)` and `min_birthmate(target, days=365)`. Hidden tests: `p_shared(23)` within $10^{-12}$ of 0.5072972343239857; `min_group(0.5) == 23`; `min_group(0.99) == 57`; `p_shared(366) == 1.0`; `min_birthmate(0.5) == 253`. Known-wrong: a `min_birthmate` built on $\frac k{365}$ returns 183 and fails the last test.

- *Example A (long-form, d4).* Prove $\sum_{k=0}^nk\binom nk = n2^{n-1}$ by counting one set in two ways, and check it for $n = 3$.
  *Model answer.* (1) Count the ways to choose, from a club of $n$ people, a committee of any size together with a chair who sits on it. (2) First way: choose the size $k$, then the committee in $\binom nk$ ways, then the chair among its $k$ members: $\sum_kk\binom nk$. (3) Second way: choose the chair first, in $n$ ways; each of the other $n - 1$ people is then on the committee or not: $2^{n-1}$ ways. (4) Both count the same set, so the totals are equal. (5) Check for $n = 3$: $0 + 3 + 6 + 3 = 12 = 3 \times 4$.
  *Rubric (10).* 3: a set defined so that both sides count it (line 1), the creative step. 2: the first count (line 2). 3: the second count with the in-or-out choice (line 3). 1: the conclusion (line 4). 1: the check (line 5).
- *Example B (d5).* A shuffled deck is dealt face up. Show that the card straight after the first ace is as likely to be the ace of spades as the two of clubs, and find that probability.
  *Answer.* Two of clubs: take the other 51 cards in a random order and insert the two of clubs straight after their first ace. Every ordering of the full deck in which the two of clubs follows the first ace arises exactly once, so there are $51!$ such orderings and the probability is $\frac{51!}{52!} = \frac1{52}$. Ace of spades: take the other 51 cards (which contain three aces) in a random order and insert the ace of spades straight after their first ace; it becomes the second ace, immediately after the first. Again each qualifying ordering arises exactly once: $\frac1{52}$. Decision point: intuition says the ace of spades is less likely because it must not itself be the first ace; the insertion bijection replaces that worry with a count.

**N3 The axioms of probability** (`set.count.axioms`). 10 items (4 d3, 4 d4, 2 d5). Long-form: inclusion-exclusion for three events from the axioms. Code drill `drl.prob.all-faces` (d4): `p_all_faces(rolls, faces=6)` by inclusion-exclusion with `Fraction`s, and `p_all_faces_brute(rolls, faces)` by enumeration. Hidden tests: `p_all_faces(6) == Fraction(5, 324)`; `p_all_faces(10) == Fraction(38045, 139968)`; `p_all_faces(5) == 0`; `p_all_faces(3, faces=1) == 1`; the two functions agree for `faces=3` and `rolls` from 1 to 7. Known-wrong: a sum without the alternating sign fails the first test.

- *Example A (long-form, d4).* From the axioms, prove $P(A \cup B \cup C) = P(A) + P(B) + P(C) - P(A \cap B) - P(A \cap C) - P(B \cap C) + P(A \cap B \cap C)$.
  *Model answer.* (1) Two events first: $A \cup B$ is the disjoint union of $A$ and $B \cap A^c$, and $B$ is the disjoint union of $A \cap B$ and $B \cap A^c$. By additivity $P(A \cup B) = P(A) + P(B \cap A^c)$ and $P(B) = P(A \cap B) + P(B \cap A^c)$; subtracting, $P(A \cup B) = P(A) + P(B) - P(A \cap B)$. (2) Apply this to the events $A \cup B$ and $C$: $P(A \cup B \cup C) = P(A \cup B) + P(C) - P((A \cup B) \cap C)$. (3) Distribute: $(A \cup B) \cap C = (A \cap C) \cup (B \cap C)$. (4) Apply the two-event rule again: $P((A \cap C) \cup (B \cap C)) = P(A \cap C) + P(B \cap C) - P(A \cap B \cap C)$. (5) Substitute lines 1 and 4 into line 2 and collect terms.
  *Rubric (10).* 3: the two-event rule from additivity on disjoint pieces (line 1). 2: applying it to $A \cup B$ and $C$ (line 2). 2: the distributive law (line 3). 2: the second application, where the triple intersection appears (line 4). 1: assembly (line 5).
- *Example B (d5).* Five people put their hats in a box and each takes one at random. Find the probability that exactly two get their own hat.
  *Answer.* Choose the two who match: $\binom52 = 10$ ways. The other three must all miss, a derangement of 3: $D_3 = 3!\left(1 - 1 + \frac12 - \frac16\right) = 2$. So $P = \frac{10 \times 2}{5!} = \frac{20}{120} = \frac16$. Decision point: "exactly two" is neither "at least two" nor a direct inclusion-exclusion over pairs; fixing who matches and deranging the rest makes the count clean. In general $P(\text{exactly } k) = \frac1{k!}\sum_{j=0}^{n-k}\frac{(-1)^j}{j!} \to \frac{e^{-1}}{k!}$, a Poisson limit met again in lesson 62.

**N4 First interview patterns** (`set.count.patterns`). 8 items (3 d3, 3 d4, 2 d5). Long-form: the problem of points in general. Code drill `drl.prob.prisoners` (d4): `success_prob(n)` exactly, and `simulate(n, trials, seed)`. Hidden tests: `success_prob(2) == Fraction(1, 2)`; `success_prob(4) == Fraction(5, 12)`; `success_prob(100)` within $10^{-12}$ of 0.3118278206898048; an odd `n` raises `ValueError`; `simulate(10, 20000, seed=7)` lies within 4 standard errors of `success_prob(10)` (0.354). Known-wrong: `0.5 ** n` fails the first test (it returns $\frac14$ for $n = 2$).

- *Example A (long-form, d4).* Ann and Bob play fair rounds; the first to win a set number takes the pot. Play stops when Ann needs $a$ more wins and Bob needs $b$. Show that Ann's fair share is the chance of at least $a$ heads in $a + b - 1$ fair tosses, explain why it is valid to imagine all $a + b - 1$ rounds played, and evaluate it for $a = 2$, $b = 3$.
  *Model answer.* (1) The fair share is Ann's chance of winning had play continued. (2) Within $a + b - 1$ more rounds exactly one of them reaches their target: if Ann wins at least $a$ of them, Bob wins at most $b - 1$, and the reverse. (3) Imagine all $a + b - 1$ rounds played even if the winner is settled sooner. The extra rounds cannot change the winner: whoever reaches the target in the real game also has the required number in the full sequence, and by line 2 the other cannot. (4) The $2^{a+b-1}$ full sequences are equally likely, which the real, stopped sequences are not (they have different lengths). (5) So Ann's chance is $\sum_{k=a}^{a+b-1}\binom{a + b - 1}k2^{-(a+b-1)}$. (6) For $a = 2$, $b = 3$: 4 rounds, $\frac{\binom42 + \binom43 + \binom44}{16} = \frac{11}{16}$, so Ann takes £68.75 of £100.
  *Rubric (10).* 1: fair share as winning chance (line 1). 2: exactly one winner within $a + b - 1$ rounds (line 2). 3: why imagined rounds change no outcome (line 3), the creative step. 2: equally likely sequences (line 4). 1: the formula (line 5). 1: the number (line 6).
- *Example B (d5).* Six fair dice are rolled. Find the probability that exactly four different faces appear.
  *Answer.* Choose the four faces: $\binom64 = 15$. Count the sequences of 6 rolls that use all four chosen faces (onto maps) by inclusion-exclusion over missing faces: $4^6 - \binom413^6 + \binom422^6 - \binom431^6 = 4{,}096 - 2{,}916 + 384 - 4 = 1{,}560$. So $P = \frac{15 \times 1{,}560}{6^6} = \frac{23{,}400}{46{,}656} = \frac{325}{648} \approx 0.502$. Decision point: "sequences using only these four faces" ($4^6$) overcounts sequences that use fewer; the onto count is needed, and inclusion-exclusion gives it. Exactly four distinct faces is, surprisingly, more likely than not.

**H1 Updating on evidence** (`set.cond.updating`). 8 items (3 d3, 3 d4, 2 d5). Long-form: evidence is symmetric. Code drill `drl.prob.p-given` (d3): `p_given(event, condition)` over two fair dice, exact (the example of CONTENT-FORMAT.md 3.9). Hidden tests: a total of 10 given at least one 5 gives $\frac1{11}$; a total of 7 given at least one 5 gives $\frac2{11}$; a condition that never holds raises `ZeroDivisionError`; conditioning on an event that always holds returns the unconditional probability. Known-wrong: dividing by 36 instead of by the condition's count fails the first test.

- *Example A (long-form, d4).* Suppose $0 < P(A) < 1$ and $0 < P(B) < 1$. Prove (a) $P(A \mid B) > P(A)$ if and only if $P(B \mid A) > P(B)$; (b) if $P(A \mid B) > P(A)$ then $P(A \mid B^c) < P(A)$. Interpret both in words.
  *Model answer.* (1) $P(A \mid B) > P(A) \iff \frac{P(A \cap B)}{P(B)} > P(A) \iff P(A \cap B) > P(A)P(B)$, multiplying by $P(B) > 0$. (2) The last condition is symmetric in $A$ and $B$, so dividing by $P(A) > 0$ it is equivalent to $P(B \mid A) > P(B)$. (3) For (b), total probability gives $P(A) = P(A \mid B)P(B) + P(A \mid B^c)P(B^c)$, a weighted average with positive weights summing to 1. (4) A weighted average lies between its two parts, so if one part exceeds $P(A)$ the other is below it. (5) In words: evidence is symmetric (if $B$ makes $A$ more likely, $A$ makes $B$ more likely), and you cannot expect evidence both ways: if seeing $B$ would raise your belief in $A$, not seeing it must lower it.
  *Rubric (10).* 3: the reduction to $P(A \cap B) > P(A)P(B)$, with the sign of $P(B)$ noted (line 1). 2: symmetry (line 2). 2: total probability as a weighted average (line 3). 2: the averaging argument (line 4). 1: interpretation (line 5).
- *Example B (d5).* Three cards are dealt from a shuffled deck. (a) Given that at least one is an ace, find the chance that all three are aces. (b) Given that the ace of spades is among them, find the same chance. Explain why the answers differ.
  *Answer.* (a) $P(\text{at least one ace}) = 1 - \frac{\binom{48}3}{\binom{52}3} = \frac{22{,}100 - 17{,}296}{22{,}100} = \frac{4{,}804}{22{,}100}$ and $P(\text{three aces}) = \frac4{22{,}100}$, so the answer is $\frac4{4{,}804} = \frac1{1{,}201}$. (b) Given the ace of spades, the other two cards are a uniform pair from the other 51, so the chance both are aces is $\frac{\binom32}{\binom{51}2} = \frac3{1{,}275} = \frac1{425}$. Decision point: "at least one ace" is met by a great many one-ace hands, which dilute the three-ace hands; "the ace of spades is here" is a more specific fact that removes most of those hands (the structure of lesson 40's Tuesday boy).

**H2 Bayes' rule** (`set.cond.bayes`). 10 items (4 d3, 4 d4, 2 d5). Long-form: sequential updating is coherent. Code drill `drl.prob.bayes-update` (d4): `posterior(prior, likelihood, observations)` over a finite set of hypotheses, exact. Hidden tests: fair against two-headed coin with prior $\left(\frac{99}{100}, \frac1{100}\right)$ after 5 heads gives $\frac{32}{131}$ for two-headed; one tail sends it to 0; priors that do not sum to 1 raise `ValueError`; the same observations in any order give the same posterior; an observation impossible under every hypothesis raises `ZeroDivisionError`.

- *Example A (long-form, d4).* Two pieces of evidence $E_1$, $E_2$ are conditionally independent given $H$ and given $H^c$. Show that updating on $E_1$ then $E_2$, on $E_2$ then $E_1$, and on both at once give the same posterior; then say what fails without conditional independence.
  *Model answer.* (1) At once, in odds form: $\frac{P(H \mid E_1, E_2)}{P(H^c \mid E_1, E_2)} = \frac{P(H)}{P(H^c)} \cdot \frac{P(E_1, E_2 \mid H)}{P(E_1, E_2 \mid H^c)}$. (2) By conditional independence $P(E_1, E_2 \mid H) = P(E_1 \mid H)P(E_2 \mid H)$, and the same given $H^c$, so the likelihood ratio factorises as $L_1L_2$. (3) Updating on $E_1$ first multiplies the prior odds by $L_1$. (4) The second update needs $P(E_2 \mid H, E_1)$, which by conditional independence equals $P(E_2 \mid H)$; likewise given $H^c$; so it multiplies by $L_2$. (5) Multiplication commutes, so either order and the joint update all give prior odds $\times\,L_1L_2$. (6) Without conditional independence line 4 fails: the second update must use $P(E_2 \mid H, E_1)$, which can be far from $P(E_2 \mid H)$ (two tests in one lab). Updating stays coherent provided each step conditions on everything already seen; the error is a wrong likelihood, not the order.
  *Rubric (10).* 2: the joint update in odds form (line 1). 2: factorisation (line 2). 1: the first update (line 3). 2: the second update with its conditioning explicit (line 4). 1: conclusion (line 5). 2: what fails and why (line 6).
- *Example B (d5).* A disease affects 1% of people. A test catches 99% of cases and falsely flags 5% of healthy people. Two tests run in the same lab: among the healthy, both are falsely positive 2% of the time (not $0.05^2 = 0.25\%$); among the ill, both are positive 98.01% of the time. Both tests are positive. Find the chance of disease and compare it with the answer under conditional independence.
  *Answer.* $P(D \mid ++) = \frac{0.01 \times 0.9801}{0.01 \times 0.9801 + 0.99 \times 0.02} = \frac{0.009801}{0.029601} \approx 0.331$. Under conditional independence the second term of the denominator would be $0.99 \times 0.0025 = 0.002475$, giving $\frac{0.009801}{0.012276} \approx 0.798$. Decision point: what the pair of tests is worth is the likelihood ratio of the pair, and shared false alarms cut it from about 392 to 49.

**H3 Independence** (`set.cond.independence`). 8 items (3 d3, 3 d4, 2 d5). Long-form: Simpson's paradox explained. Code drill `drl.prob.independence-check` (d3): `pairwise_independent(events, space)` and `mutually_independent(events, space)` on a finite equally likely space, testing every sub-collection. Hidden tests: two coins with A, B and C ("they match") give `True` and `False`; "first die even", "second die even" and "third die even" on three dice give `True` for both; two disjoint events of positive probability give `False`; an event of probability 1 with any other gives `True`. Known-wrong: a `mutually_independent` that checks only pairs fails the first test.

- *Example A (long-form, d4).* Kidney-stone data: treatment A cured 81 of 87 small stones and 192 of 263 large ones; treatment B cured 234 of 270 small and 55 of 80 large. (a) Compare cure rates within each size and overall. (b) Explain the reversal. (c) Which comparison should guide a doctor, and why?
  *Model answer.* (1) Small: A $\frac{81}{87} \approx 93\%$, B $\frac{234}{270} \approx 87\%$. Large: A $\frac{192}{263} \approx 73\%$, B $\frac{55}{80} \approx 69\%$. (2) Overall: A $\frac{273}{350} = 78\%$, B $\frac{289}{350} \approx 83\%$. (3) Each overall rate is a weighted average of the size-specific rates, weighted by that treatment's mix of cases. (4) A was used mostly on large stones (263 of 350), which are harder to cure, and B mostly on small ones (270 of 350), so A's average is pulled towards the lower large-stone rate. (5) Stone size affected both the choice of treatment and the chance of cure: a confounder. (6) Compare within sizes: the doctor knows the stone's size, and size is a cause of both, not a consequence of the treatment. A is better for both sizes.
  *Rubric (10).* 2: within-size rates (line 1). 1: overall rates (line 2). 2: weighted averages (line 3). 2: case mix (line 4). 1: the confounder named (line 5). 2: which comparison and why (line 6).
- *Example B (d5).* Three events each have probability $\frac12$ and every pair intersects with probability $\frac14$. Find every possible value of $P(A \cap B \cap C)$, and the value that makes the events mutually independent.
  *Answer.* Let $t = P(A \cap B \cap C)$. The atom "$A$ and $B$ but not $C$" has probability $\frac14 - t$, and likewise for the other pairs. Then "$A$ only" has $\frac12 - 2\left(\frac14 - t\right) - t = t$, and likewise for $B$ and $C$; "none" has $1 - \left[3t + 3\left(\frac14 - t\right) + t\right] = \frac14 - t$. All eight atoms are non-negative exactly when $0 \le t \le \frac14$, so $P(A \cap B \cap C)$ can be any value in $\left[0, \frac14\right]$; mutual independence needs $t = \frac18$. Decision point: pairwise information leaves one free parameter, and writing all eight atoms in terms of it turns a puzzle into a list of inequalities.

**H4 Puzzles that fool experts** (`set.cond.puzzles`). 10 items (4 d3, 4 d4, 2 d5). Long-form: Monty Hall with a biased host. Code drill `drl.prob.gamblers-ruin` (d4): `win_prob(i, N, p)` and `expected_duration(i, N, p)`, exact. Hidden tests: `win_prob(3, 5, Fraction(2, 5)) == Fraction(76, 211)`; `win_prob(5, 10, Fraction(1, 2)) == Fraction(1, 2)`; `win_prob(0, N, p) == 0` and `win_prob(N, N, p) == 1`; `expected_duration(3, 5, Fraction(2, 5)) == Fraction(1265, 211)`; `expected_duration(2, 4, Fraction(1, 2)) == 4`; `i` outside $[0, N]$ raises `ValueError`. Known-wrong: using $\frac pq$ in place of $\frac qp$ fails the first test.

- *Example A (long-form, d4).* In Monty Hall you pick door 1. The host knows where the car is and always opens a goat door other than yours; when he has a choice (the car is behind door 1) he opens door 3 with probability $q$. He opens door 3. Find the probability that switching wins for every $q$, and comment on $q = 0$, $\frac12$ and 1.
  *Model answer.* (1) Let $C_i$ be "the car is behind door $i$", each with probability $\frac13$, and $O_3$ "the host opens door 3". (2) $P(O_3 \mid C_1) = q$, $P(O_3 \mid C_2) = 1$ (forced), $P(O_3 \mid C_3) = 0$. (3) $P(O_3) = \frac q3 + \frac13$. (4) Switching wins exactly when the car is behind door 2: $P(C_2 \mid O_3) = \frac{1/3}{(q + 1)/3} = \frac1{1 + q}$. (5) $q = \frac12$ gives $\frac23$, the usual answer; $q = 1$ gives $\frac12$, because he opens door 3 whenever he can, so it says nothing between doors 1 and 2; $q = 0$ gives 1, because he would never open door 3 by choice, so the car must be behind door 2. (6) Since $\frac1{1 + q} \ge \frac12$ for every $q$, switching is never worse.
  *Rubric (10).* 1: events (line 1). 3: the host's behaviour as likelihoods (line 2). 1: total probability (line 3). 2: Bayes' rule (line 4). 2: the three cases interpreted (line 5). 1: switching never worse (line 6).
- *Example B (d5).* A tennis player wins each point independently with probability $p = 0.6$. Find the probability that she wins a game from 0-0 (first to 4 points, but she must lead by 2).
  *Answer.* She wins without reaching deuce by taking 4 points while her opponent takes 0, 1 or 2; her last point ends the game, so the opponent's points sit among the earlier ones: $p^4\left(1 + 4q + 10q^2\right) = 0.1296 \times 4.2 = 0.54432$. Deuce (3-3) is reached with probability $\binom63p^3q^3 = 20 \times 0.216 \times 0.064 = 0.27648$, and from deuce she wins with probability $\frac{p^2}{p^2 + q^2} = \frac{0.36}{0.52}$ (lesson 44). Total: $0.54432 + 0.27648 \times 0.69231 \approx 0.736$. Decision point: split the game at deuce; before it the game is a finite count (negative binomial), after it a state that repeats (first-step analysis).

**V1 Random variables and distributions** (`set.rv.distributions`). 10 items (4 d3, 4 d4, 2 d5). Long-form: Fisher's tea taster. Code drill `drl.prob.pmf-tools` (d3): `pmf(outcomes, X)` (outcomes with their probabilities, and a function $X$) returning a dictionary of `Fraction`s, `cdf(pmf, x)` and `push(pmf, g)`. Hidden tests: the larger of two dice gives $\frac{2k - 1}{36}$; `cdf` at 5 minus `cdf` at 2 gives $\frac7{12}$; pushing the uniform on $\{-2, \dots, 2\}$ through $x^2$ gives $\left\{0: \frac15, 1: \frac25, 4: \frac25\right\}$; masses not summing to 1 raise `ValueError`.

- *Example A (long-form, d4).* A taster claims she can tell whether milk or tea was poured first. She is given 8 cups, 4 of each, knows this, and must name the 4 milk-first cups. Suppose she is guessing. (a) Find the distribution of $X$, the number of milk-first cups she names correctly. (b) Find $P(X = 4)$. (c) Explain why $X$ is hypergeometric, not binomial.
  *Model answer.* (1) Guessing means every choice of 4 cups from 8 is equally likely: $\binom84 = 70$ choices. (2) $X = k$ when she picks $k$ of the 4 milk-first cups and $4 - k$ of the 4 tea-first cups: $\binom4k\binom4{4 - k}$ ways. (3) $P(X = k) = \frac{\binom4k\binom4{4 - k}}{70}$, which is $\frac1{70}, \frac{16}{70}, \frac{36}{70}, \frac{16}{70}, \frac1{70}$ for $k = 0$ to 4, summing to 1. (4) $P(X = 4) = \frac1{70} \approx 0.014$. (5) Her picks are made without replacement from a fixed set holding exactly 4 milk-first cups; whether one pick is right changes the chance for the next, so they are not independent trials: $X \sim \mathrm{HGeom}(4, 4, 4)$. (6) A taster who names all 4 has done what a guesser does 1.4% of the time: Fisher's exact test (1.6).
  *Rubric (10).* 2: the equally likely choices (line 1). 2: the count (line 2). 2: the distribution with its check (line 3). 1: the answer (line 4). 2: why not binomial (line 5). 1: the test interpretation (line 6).
- *Example B (d5).* 50 fish are tagged and released into a lake of unknown size $N$. Later 40 are caught, without replacement, and 7 are tagged. Find the $N$ that makes this observation most likely.
  *Answer.* $L(N) = \frac{\binom{50}7\binom{N - 50}{33}}{\binom N{40}}$ for $N \ge 83$. Compare consecutive values: $\frac{L(N)}{L(N - 1)} = \frac{(N - 50)(N - 40)}{N(N - 83)}$, which exceeds 1 exactly when $(N - 50)(N - 40) > N(N - 83)$, that is $2{,}000 > 7N$, that is $N < 285.7$. So $L$ rises up to $N = 285$ and falls after: $\hat N = 285$, the estimate $\frac{50 \times 40}7 \approx 285.7$ rounded down. Decision point: $N$ is a whole number and $L$ has no convenient derivative; the ratio of consecutive terms finds the maximum exactly.

**V2 Expectation** (`set.rv.expectation`). 10 items (4 d3, 4 d4, 2 d5). Long-form: linearity and fixed points. Code drill `drl.prob.moments` (d3): `mean(pmf)`, `variance(pmf)` and `lotus(pmf, g)` with `Fraction`s. Hidden tests: a fair die gives $\frac72$, $\frac{35}{12}$ and $E[X^2] = \frac{91}6$; $\mathrm{Var}(3X + 2) = 9\,\mathrm{Var}(X)$ on three seeded PMFs; a negative mass raises `ValueError`; the PMF $\left\{0: \frac12, 2: \frac12\right\}$ has variance 1.

- *Example A (long-form, d4).* Prove $E[X + Y] = E[X] + E[Y]$ for random variables on a finite sample space, explain why no independence is needed, and use it to find the expected number of fixed points of a random permutation of $n$.
  *Model answer.* (1) On a finite space $E[X] = \sum_sX(s)P(\{s\})$; grouping outcomes by the value of $X$ gives the usual $\sum_xxP(X = x)$. (2) $E[X + Y] = \sum_s(X(s) + Y(s))P(\{s\}) = \sum_sX(s)P(\{s\}) + \sum_sY(s)P(\{s\}) = E[X] + E[Y]$. (3) The sum runs over outcomes and $X$ and $Y$ are evaluated at the same outcome; how they relate across outcomes never enters, so dependence is irrelevant. (4) Fixed points: $X = \sum_{j=1}^nI_j$, where $I_j$ indicates that $j$ is fixed. (5) $E[I_j] = P(j \text{ fixed}) = \frac{(n - 1)!}{n!} = \frac1n$. (6) $E[X] = n \cdot \frac1n = 1$, although the $I_j$ are dependent.
  *Rubric (10).* 2: expectation as a sum over outcomes (line 1). 2: the computation (line 2). 2: why no independence is needed (line 3). 1: indicators (line 4). 2: the indicator mean (line 5). 1: conclusion (line 6).
- *Example B (d5).* Five red and five black cards are shuffled and laid in a row. A run is a maximal block of one colour. Find the expected number of runs.
  *Answer.* Runs $= 1 +$ the number of colour changes between neighbours. For each of the 9 neighbouring pairs, $P(\text{different colours}) = 2 \cdot \frac5{10} \cdot \frac59 = \frac59$. So $E[\text{runs}] = 1 + 9 \cdot \frac59 = 6$. Decision point: runs are awkward to count directly; counting the boundaries between runs turns the problem into indicators on neighbouring pairs.

**V3 Waiting times and rare events** (`set.rv.waiting`). 10 items (4 d3, 4 d4, 2 d5). Long-form: memorylessness characterises the geometric. Code drill `drl.prob.pattern-wait` (d4): `expected_wait(pattern, p=Fraction(1, 2))` builds the chain of partial matches and solves it exactly. Hidden tests: HH gives 6, HT 4, HTH 10, HHT 8, HHH 14; HH with $p = \frac13$ gives 12; a character other than H or T raises `ValueError`. Known-wrong: a chain that resets to the start after every mismatch returns 6 for HT and 14 for HTH, so it fails the second test first and the third as well.

- *Example A (long-form, d4).* Let $X$ take values in $\{0, 1, 2, \dots\}$ with $P(X \ge 1) = q \in (0, 1)$, and suppose $P(X \ge n + k \mid X \ge n) = P(X \ge k)$ for all $n, k \ge 0$. Prove that $X \sim \mathrm{Geom}(p)$ with $p = 1 - q$.
  *Model answer.* (1) Write $G(n) = P(X \ge n)$, so $G(0) = 1$ and $G(1) = q$. (2) Memorylessness says $\frac{G(n + k)}{G(n)} = G(k)$ whenever $G(n) > 0$, so $G(n + k) = G(n)G(k)$. (3) With $k = 1$: $G(n + 1) = qG(n)$, so by induction $G(n) = q^n > 0$, which also justifies the division in line 2. (4) $P(X = n) = G(n) - G(n + 1) = q^n - q^{n+1} = q^np$. (5) This is the $\mathrm{Geom}(p)$ PMF (failures before the first success).
  *Rubric (10).* 1: notation (line 1). 3: the functional equation (line 2). 3: the induction, with positivity (line 3). 2: the PMF (line 4). 1: identification (line 5).
- *Example B (d5).* In a best-of-seven series a team wins each game independently with probability 0.55. Find the probability that it wins the series, and the expected number of games played.
  *Answer.* It wins in game $4 + k$ after $k$ losses ($k = 0$ to 3) with probability $\binom{3 + k}k0.55^4\,0.45^k$; the sum is about 0.608, equal to $P(\mathrm{Bin}(7, 0.55) \ge 4)$ by imagining all seven games played. The series lasts $g$ games when either team takes its fourth win in game $g$: $P(G = g) = \binom{g - 1}3\left(p^4q^{g-4} + q^4p^{g-4}\right)$, which is 0.133, 0.255, 0.309 and 0.303 for $g = 4$ to 7. So $E[G] \approx 5.78$. Decision point: imagining all seven games gives the winner but not the length; the length needs the real stopping rule.

**V4 Expectation puzzles** (`set.rv.puzzles`). 10 items (4 d3, 4 d4, 2 d5). Long-form: coupon collector's mean and variance. Code drill `drl.prob.coupon` (d3): `expected_draws(n)` and `variance_draws(n)`, exact. Hidden tests: $n = 6$ gives $\frac{147}{10}$ and $\frac{3{,}899}{100}$; $n = 1$ gives 1 and 0; $n = 2$ gives 3 and 2; $n < 1$ raises `ValueError`.

- *Example A (long-form, d4).* Derive the mean and variance of the number of rolls needed to see all six faces of a fair die.
  *Model answer.* (1) Split the wait into stages: $T = G_1 + \dots + G_6$, where $G_k$ counts the rolls taken to see a new face once $k - 1$ faces have been seen. (2) In stage $k$ each roll is new with probability $p_k = \frac{7 - k}6$, independently, so $G_k \sim \mathrm{FS}(p_k)$ with $E[G_k] = \frac1{p_k}$ and $\mathrm{Var}(G_k) = \frac{1 - p_k}{p_k^2}$. (3) The stages use disjoint sets of rolls, so they are independent. (4) $E[T] = \sum_k\frac6{7 - k} = 6\left(1 + \frac12 + \dots + \frac16\right) = 14.7$. (5) $\mathrm{Var}(T) = 0 + 0.24 + 0.75 + 2 + 6 + 30 = 38.99$, a standard deviation of about 6.2. (6) The last stage alone contributes 6 of the 14.7 to the mean and 30 of the 38.99 to the variance: the final face dominates both.
  *Rubric (10).* 2: the decomposition (line 1). 2: stage distributions (line 2). 2: independence of the stages, needed for the variance (line 3). 1: mean (line 4). 2: variance (line 5). 1: comment (line 6).
- *Example B (d5).* Toss a fair coin 10 times. Find the expected number and the variance of the number of occurrences of HH, overlaps allowed.
  *Answer.* $X = \sum_{i=1}^9I_i$, where $I_i$ indicates that tosses $i$ and $i + 1$ are both heads; $E[X] = \frac94$. By lesson 66, $E[X^2] = \sum_iP(A_i) + \sum_{i \ne j}P(A_i \cap A_j)$. For $\lvert i - j \rvert = 1$, $P(A_i \cap A_j) = \frac18$ (three heads), and there are 16 such ordered pairs; for $\lvert i - j \rvert \ge 2$, $P = \frac1{16}$, and there are $72 - 16 = 56$. So $E[X^2] = \frac94 + \frac{16}8 + \frac{56}{16} = 7.75$ and $\mathrm{Var}(X) = 7.75 - \frac{81}{16} = \frac{43}{16} \approx 2.69$. Decision point: overlapping indicators are dependent only when they share a toss, and the second-moment identity handles that without covariances.

**V5 Continuous random variables** (`set.rv.continuous`). 10 items (4 d3, 4 d4, 2 d5). Long-form: universality of the uniform. Code drill `drl.prob.inverse-transform` (d3): `expo_from_uniform(u, rate)` and `sample_mean(rate, n, seed)` using `zqtest`'s seeded generator. Hidden tests: $u = 0$ gives 0; $u = 1 - e^{-1}$ with rate 2 gives $\frac12$ within $10^{-12}$ (a rate other than 1, so the known-wrong cannot pass by accident); a rate of 0 or less raises `ValueError`; `sample_mean(0.5, 100000, seed=3)` lies within 4 standard errors of 2; the same seed gives the same mean. Known-wrong: multiplying by the rate instead of dividing fails the second test and the mean test.

- *Example A (long-form, d4).* Let $F$ be a continuous CDF, strictly increasing where $0 < F < 1$, and $U \sim \mathrm{Unif}(0, 1)$. Prove (a) $F^{-1}(U)$ has CDF $F$; (b) if $X$ has CDF $F$ then $F(X) \sim \mathrm{Unif}(0, 1)$. Then give a way to simulate $\mathrm{Expo}(\lambda)$.
  *Model answer.* (1) $F$ is continuous and strictly increasing there, so $F^{-1}: (0, 1) \to \mathbb R$ exists and is increasing. (2) (a) $P(F^{-1}(U) \le x) = P(U \le F(x)) = F(x)$: applying the increasing $F$ to both sides keeps the inequality, and $P(U \le u) = u$. (3) (b) For $u \in (0, 1)$, $P(F(X) \le u) = P(X \le F^{-1}(u)) = F(F^{-1}(u)) = u$, using that $F^{-1}$ is increasing; this is the $\mathrm{Unif}(0, 1)$ CDF. (4) For $\mathrm{Expo}(\lambda)$, $F(x) = 1 - e^{-\lambda x}$ and $F^{-1}(u) = -\frac{\ln(1 - u)}\lambda$. (5) So $-\frac{\ln(1 - U)}\lambda \sim \mathrm{Expo}(\lambda)$; since $1 - U$ is also uniform, $-\frac{\ln U}\lambda$ works too.
  *Rubric (10).* 1: the inverse exists (line 1). 3: part (a) with the monotonicity step (line 2). 3: part (b) (line 3). 2: the exponential's inverse (line 4). 1: the remark (line 5).
- *Example B (d5).* Buy orders arrive as a Poisson process at 3 a minute and sell orders independently at 2 a minute. Find the chance that 3 buy orders arrive before 2 sell orders.
  *Answer.* Merge the streams: orders arrive at rate 5 and each is a buy with probability $\frac35$, independently of the others (lesson 74). Three buys come before two sells exactly when at least 3 of the first 4 orders are buys (the problem of points, lesson 19): $\binom43\left(\frac35\right)^3\frac25 + \left(\frac35\right)^4 = \frac{216 + 81}{625} = \frac{297}{625} \approx 0.475$. Decision point: working with arrival times means comparing Gamma variables; the merged stream turns timing into a sequence of coin tosses.

**V6 Moments and generating functions** (`set.rv.moments`). 10 items (4 d3, 4 d4, 2 d5). Long-form: the normal MGF. Code drill `drl.prob.extinction` (d3): `extinction_prob(offspring, tol=1e-12)` iterates $s \mapsto G(s)$ from 0. Hidden tests: offspring masses $\left(\frac14, \frac14, \frac12\right)$ give $\frac12$ within $10^{-9}$; $(0.2, 0.3, 0.5)$ gives 0.4; $(0.5, 0.25, 0.25)$ gives 1 within $10^{-6}$; $(0, 1)$, exactly one child each, gives 0; masses not summing to 1 raise `ValueError`. Known-wrong: iterating from 1 returns 1 for the first test.

- *Example A (long-form, d4).* Derive the MGF of $X \sim \mathcal N(\mu, \sigma^2)$, and use it to show that the sum of independent $\mathcal N(\mu_1, \sigma_1^2)$ and $\mathcal N(\mu_2, \sigma_2^2)$ variables is $\mathcal N(\mu_1 + \mu_2, \sigma_1^2 + \sigma_2^2)$.
  *Model answer.* (1) For $Z \sim \mathcal N(0, 1)$, $M_Z(t) = \int_{-\infty}^\infty\frac1{\sqrt{2\pi}}e^{tz - z^2/2}\,dz$. (2) Complete the square: $tz - \frac{z^2}2 = -\frac{(z - t)^2}2 + \frac{t^2}2$. (3) So $M_Z(t) = e^{t^2/2}\int\frac1{\sqrt{2\pi}}e^{-(z - t)^2/2}\,dz = e^{t^2/2}$, the integral being that of the $\mathcal N(t, 1)$ density. (4) $X = \mu + \sigma Z$, so $M_X(t) = E\left[e^{t\mu + t\sigma Z}\right] = e^{\mu t}M_Z(\sigma t) = e^{\mu t + \sigma^2t^2/2}$. (5) For independent $X_1$, $X_2$: $M_{X_1 + X_2}(t) = M_{X_1}(t)M_{X_2}(t) = e^{(\mu_1 + \mu_2)t + (\sigma_1^2 + \sigma_2^2)t^2/2}$. (6) This is the MGF of $\mathcal N(\mu_1 + \mu_2, \sigma_1^2 + \sigma_2^2)$, and an MGF that exists near 0 determines the distribution.
  *Rubric (10).* 1: the integral (line 1). 3: completing the square (line 2), the creative step. 2: the shifted density (line 3). 2: location and scale (line 4). 1: the product (line 5). 1: uniqueness cited (line 6).
- *Example B (d5).* Find every pair of six-sided dice with positive whole-number faces whose total has the same distribution as the total of two ordinary dice.
  *Answer.* A die with faces $a_1, \dots, a_6$ has generating polynomial $p(x) = \sum_ix^{a_i}$ with $p(1) = 6$ and $p(0) = 0$. We need $p(x)q(x) = (x + \dots + x^6)^2 = x^2(1 + x)^2(1 + x + x^2)^2(1 - x + x^2)^2$. At $x = 1$ the factors are worth 1, 2, 3 and 1, so each die needs one factor $x$ (no zero faces) and, for $p(1) = 6$, exactly one $(1 + x)$ and one $(1 + x + x^2)$. The two factors $(1 - x + x^2)$ split one each (ordinary dice) or two and none. The second split gives $x(1 + x)(1 + x + x^2) = x + 2x^2 + 2x^3 + x^4$, faces $\{1, 2, 2, 3, 3, 4\}$, and $x(1 + x)(1 + x + x^2)(1 - x + x^2)^2 = x + x^3 + x^4 + x^5 + x^6 + x^8$, faces $\{1, 3, 4, 5, 6, 8\}$: Sicherman's dice, the only alternative. Decision point: listing faces is hopeless; the generating function turns "same distribution of totals" into "same polynomial product", and the factorisation is unique.

**V7 Gamma, Beta and their relatives** (`set.rv.families`). 10 items (4 d3, 4 d4, 2 d5). Long-form: Bayes' billiards. Code drill `drl.prob.beta-binomial` (d3): `posterior(a, b, k, n)`, `predict_next(a, b, k, n)` and `predictive_pmf(a, b, m)` for the number of successes in the next $m$ trials. Hidden tests: `predict_next(1, 1, 7, 10) == Fraction(2, 3)`; `posterior(2, 3, 4, 6) == (6, 5)`; `predictive_pmf(1, 1, 4)` is uniform, $\frac15$ each (Pólya's urn, lesson 85); `a <= 0` raises `ValueError`.

- *Example A (long-form, d4).* Show that $\int_0^1\binom nkp^k(1 - p)^{n - k}\,dp = \frac1{n + 1}$ for $k = 0, \dots, n$ without integrating, by a story with $n + 1$ balls thrown on a table.
  *Model answer.* (1) Throw $n + 1$ balls independently and uniformly on $[0, 1]$, and call the first one pink. (2) Let $X$ be the number of the other $n$ balls landing left of the pink one. (3) Given the pink ball's position $p$, the others are independently left of it with probability $p$, so $P(X = k \mid p) = \binom nkp^k(1 - p)^{n - k}$. (4) Averaging over $p \sim \mathrm{Unif}(0, 1)$: $P(X = k) = \int_0^1\binom nkp^k(1 - p)^{n - k}\,dp$. (5) Now throw all $n + 1$ balls first and choose which is pink afterwards, uniformly at random: the same experiment. (6) The pink ball is then equally likely to be any of the $n + 1$ in left-to-right order, so $X$ is uniform on $\{0, \dots, n\}$: $P(X = k) = \frac1{n + 1}$. (7) Lines 4 and 6 give the identity.
  *Rubric (10).* 1: the experiment (line 1). 1: the count (line 2). 2: conditioning (line 3). 1: averaging (line 4). 3: reordering the experiment, the creative step (line 5). 1: the uniform rank (line 6). 1: conclusion (line 7).
- *Example B (d5).* Pólya's urn starts with one red and one black ball; each ball drawn is returned with one more of its colour. Show that any sequence of $n$ draws with $k$ reds has probability $\frac{k!(n - k)!}{(n + 1)!}$, and deduce the distribution of the number of reds.
  *Answer.* Before the $j$th draw the urn holds $j + 1$ balls, so the denominators multiply to $2 \cdot 3 \cdots (n + 1) = (n + 1)!$ whatever the sequence. The red numerators are $1, 2, \dots, k$ in the order the reds come, and the black ones $1, \dots, n - k$, with products $k!$ and $(n - k)!$. So every sequence with $k$ reds has probability $\frac{k!(n - k)!}{(n + 1)!}$, and $P(k \text{ reds}) = \binom nk\frac{k!(n - k)!}{(n + 1)!} = \frac1{n + 1}$. Decision point: the order of draws moves the factors around but never changes their product (exchangeability); the uniform answer matches Bayes' billiards because the urn behaves like a uniform $p$ chosen once followed by $p$-coin tosses.

**J1 Joint, marginal and conditional** (`set.joint.joint`). 10 items (4 d3, 4 d4, 2 d5). Long-form: the multinomial covariance. Code drill `drl.prob.joint-table` (d3): `marginals(joint)`, `conditional(joint, x)` and `independent(joint)` with `Fraction`s. Hidden tests: the smaller and larger of two dice give $P(Y = 6 \mid X = 2) = \frac29$ and `independent` is `False`; a product table is independent; conditioning on a row of zero mass raises `ZeroDivisionError`; masses not summing to 1 raise `ValueError`.

- *Example A (long-form, d4).* $(N_1, \dots, N_k) \sim \mathrm{Mult}_k(n, \mathbf p)$. Show $\mathrm{Cov}(N_i, N_j) = -np_ip_j$ for $i \ne j$ using indicators, and explain the sign.
  *Model answer.* (1) $N_i = \sum_{r=1}^nI_r$ and $N_j = \sum_{s=1}^nJ_s$, where $I_r$ indicates that trial $r$ lands in category $i$ and $J_s$ that trial $s$ lands in $j$. (2) By bilinearity $\mathrm{Cov}(N_i, N_j) = \sum_r\sum_s\mathrm{Cov}(I_r, J_s)$. (3) For $r \ne s$ the trials are independent, so $\mathrm{Cov}(I_r, J_s) = 0$. (4) For $r = s$, $I_rJ_r = 0$ because one trial cannot land in two categories, so $\mathrm{Cov}(I_r, J_r) = 0 - p_ip_j$. (5) Summing the $n$ diagonal terms: $-np_ip_j$. (6) The counts compete for a fixed total $n$: more in one category leaves fewer for the rest.
  *Rubric (10).* 2: indicators (line 1). 2: bilinearity (line 2). 2: independent trials (line 3). 2: the same trial (line 4). 1: the sum (line 5). 1: the sign explained (line 6).
- *Example B (d5).* $X \sim \mathrm{Unif}(0, 1)$ and, given $X = x$, $Y \sim \mathrm{Unif}(0, x)$. Find the density of $Y$ and $E[Y]$.
  *Answer.* The joint density is $f(x, y) = 1 \cdot \frac1x$ on $0 < y < x < 1$. For fixed $y$, $x$ runs from $y$ to 1, so $f_Y(y) = \int_y^1\frac{dx}x = -\ln y$ for $0 < y < 1$. Then $E[Y] = \int_0^1y(-\ln y)\,dy = \frac14$ (by parts), agreeing with $E[E[Y \mid X]] = E\left[\frac X2\right] = \frac14$ (lesson 107). Decision point: the limits for $x$ come from the support, $y < x < 1$, not from $(0, 1)$; the wrong limits give $\int_0^1\frac{dx}x$, which diverges, and dropping the factor $\frac1x$ as well gives a constant density and the wrong $E[Y] = \frac12$.

**J2 Covariance and correlation** (`set.joint.covariance`). 8 items (3 d3, 3 d4, 2 d5). Long-form: correlation lies in $[-1, 1]$. Code drill `drl.prob.portfolio` (d4): `portfolio_vol(w, vols, corr)`, `min_var_weight(s1, s2, rho)` and `is_valid_corr(C)` by attempting a Cholesky factorisation. Hidden tests: equal weights with volatilities 0.2, 0.2 and correlation 0.3 give 0.161245154966 within $10^{-12}$; `min_var_weight(0.2, 0.3, 0.5)` gives $\frac67$; the matrix with correlations 0.9, 0.9 and $-0.9$ is invalid; the identity is valid; weights not summing to 1 raise `ValueError`.

- *Example A (long-form, d4).* Prove $-1 \le \mathrm{Corr}(X, Y) \le 1$, and that $\lvert\mathrm{Corr}(X, Y)\rvert = 1$ only when $Y = a + bX$ with probability 1.
  *Model answer.* (1) Standardise: $U = \frac{X - \mu_X}{\sigma_X}$ and $V = \frac{Y - \mu_Y}{\sigma_Y}$ have variance 1 and $\mathrm{Cov}(U, V) = \rho$. (2) $0 \le \mathrm{Var}(U + V) = 2 + 2\rho$, so $\rho \ge -1$. (3) $0 \le \mathrm{Var}(U - V) = 2 - 2\rho$, so $\rho \le 1$. (4) If $\rho = 1$ then $\mathrm{Var}(U - V) = 0$, so $U - V$ is constant with probability 1, and the constant is 0 because its mean is 0; hence $Y = \mu_Y + \frac{\sigma_Y}{\sigma_X}(X - \mu_X)$, a line with positive slope. (5) If $\rho = -1$, likewise $U + V = 0$: a line with negative slope.
  *Rubric (10).* 2: standardising (line 1). 3: the two variance inequalities (lines 2 and 3), the creative step. 3: the equality case (line 4). 2: the negative case (line 5).
- *Example B (d5).* $n$ assets each have variance $\sigma^2$ and every pair has correlation $\rho$. Find the variance of the equally weighted portfolio, its limit as $n \to \infty$, and the smallest $\rho$ possible for a given $n$.
  *Answer.* $\mathrm{Var}\left(\frac1n\sum X_i\right) = \frac1{n^2}\left(n\sigma^2 + n(n - 1)\rho\sigma^2\right) = \sigma^2\left(\rho + \frac{1 - \rho}n\right) \to \rho\sigma^2$. A variance cannot be negative, so $\rho + \frac{1 - \rho}n \ge 0$, that is $\rho \ge -\frac1{n - 1}$. Decision point: the bound on $\rho$ falls out of a variance being non-negative: a common correlation of $-0.5$ is possible for three assets but not for four.

**J3 Transformations and the multivariate normal** (`set.joint.transform`). 10 items (4 d3, 4 d4, 2 d5). Long-form: Box-Muller. Code drill `drl.prob.convolve` (d3): `convolve(pmf1, pmf2)` for discrete PMFs. Hidden tests: two dice give $P(7) = \frac16$; three dice give $P(10) = \frac18$ and $P(9) = \frac{25}{216}$ (Galileo's problem, lesson 1); Sicherman's dice convolve to the same distribution as ordinary dice; an empty PMF raises `ValueError`.

- *Example A (long-form, d4).* Let $U_1$, $U_2$ be independent $\mathrm{Unif}(0, 1)$, $R = \sqrt{-2\ln U_1}$, $\Theta = 2\pi U_2$, $X = R\cos\Theta$ and $Y = R\sin\Theta$. Show that $X$ and $Y$ are independent $\mathcal N(0, 1)$.
  *Model answer.* (1) Work backwards: start from independent $X, Y \sim \mathcal N(0, 1)$, with joint density $\frac1{2\pi}e^{-(x^2 + y^2)/2}$, and find the joint distribution of their polar coordinates $(R, \Theta)$. (2) The map $(r, \theta) \mapsto (r\cos\theta, r\sin\theta)$ has Jacobian determinant $r$, so $f_{R,\Theta}(r, \theta) = \frac1{2\pi}e^{-r^2/2}\,r$ for $r > 0$ and $0 \le \theta < 2\pi$. (3) This factorises: $\Theta \sim \mathrm{Unif}(0, 2\pi)$, independent of $R$, which has density $re^{-r^2/2}$. (4) $P(R^2 > s) = P(R > \sqrt s) = e^{-s/2}$, so $R^2 \sim \mathrm{Expo}\left(\frac12\right)$. (5) By universality (lesson 71), $-2\ln U_1 \sim \mathrm{Expo}\left(\frac12\right)$, so $\sqrt{-2\ln U_1}$ has the distribution of $R$, and $2\pi U_2 \sim \mathrm{Unif}(0, 2\pi)$, independent of it. (6) So the $(R, \Theta)$ built from the uniforms has the joint distribution of the polar coordinates of two independent normals, and so $(X, Y)$ does too.
  *Rubric (10).* 2: the backwards strategy (line 1), the creative step. 3: the Jacobian (line 2). 2: factorisation (line 3). 1: $R^2$ (line 4). 1: matching the uniforms (line 5). 1: conclusion (line 6).
- *Example B (d5).* $X$ and $Y$ are independent $\mathrm{Expo}(1)$. Find the distribution of $\frac X{X + Y}$.
  *Answer.* By the bank and post office (lesson 103) with $a = b = 1$, $\frac X{X + Y} \sim \mathrm{Beta}(1, 1) = \mathrm{Unif}(0, 1)$. Directly, conditioning on $Y$: $P\left(\frac X{X + Y} \le u\right) = P\left(X \le \frac u{1 - u}Y\right) = \int_0^\infty e^{-y}\left(1 - e^{-uy/(1 - u)}\right)dy = 1 - (1 - u) = u$. Decision point: a ratio with a sum underneath looks like a two-dimensional Jacobian problem; conditioning on $Y$, or the Gamma story, does it in two lines.

**J4 Conditional expectation** (`set.joint.condexp`). 10 items (4 d3, 4 d4, 2 d5). Long-form: Eve's law from Adam's law. Code drill `drl.prob.random-sum` (d3): `random_sum_moments(n_mean, n_var, x_mean, x_var)` returns the mean and variance of $\sum_{i=1}^NX_i$. Hidden tests: $(20, 20, 50, 40{,}000)$ gives $(1{,}000, 850{,}000)$; a constant $N = 5$ with $\mathrm{Var}(X) = 4$ gives variance 20; Poisson $N$ with Bernoulli($p$) terms gives mean and variance both $\lambda p$; a negative variance raises `ValueError`.

- *Example A (long-form, d4).* Prove $\mathrm{Var}(Y) = E[\mathrm{Var}(Y \mid X)] + \mathrm{Var}(E[Y \mid X])$ from Adam's law.
  *Model answer.* (1) Let $g(X) = E[Y \mid X]$. By definition $\mathrm{Var}(Y \mid X) = E[Y^2 \mid X] - g(X)^2$. (2) Take expectations and use Adam's law: $E[\mathrm{Var}(Y \mid X)] = E[Y^2] - E[g(X)^2]$. (3) $\mathrm{Var}(g(X)) = E[g(X)^2] - (E[g(X)])^2 = E[g(X)^2] - (E[Y])^2$, by Adam's law again. (4) Adding lines 2 and 3: $E[Y^2] - (E[Y])^2 = \mathrm{Var}(Y)$. (5) In words: total spread is the spread within groups plus the spread between group means.
  *Rubric (10).* 2: conditional variance (line 1). 3: the first use of Adam's law (line 2). 3: the second use (line 3). 1: adding (line 4). 1: words (line 5).
- *Example B (d5).* A stick of length 1 is broken at a uniform point and the left piece kept; that piece is broken at a uniform point and its left piece kept; and so on. Find the mean and variance of the length after $n$ breaks.
  *Answer.* $L_n = U_nL_{n-1}$ with $U_n \sim \mathrm{Unif}(0, 1)$ independent of $L_{n-1}$. $E[L_n \mid L_{n-1}] = \frac{L_{n-1}}2$, so $E[L_n] = 2^{-n}$. $E[L_n^2 \mid L_{n-1}] = \frac{L_{n-1}^2}3$, so $E[L_n^2] = 3^{-n}$ and $\mathrm{Var}(L_n) = 3^{-n} - 4^{-n}$. Decision point: conditioning on the previous length makes each step one multiplication; the second moment needs its own recursion, not the square of the first.

**J5 Inequalities** (`set.joint.inequalities`). 8 items (3 d3, 3 d4, 2 d5). Long-form: Jensen's inequality and AM-GM. Code drill `drl.prob.tail-bounds` (d4): `exact_tail(n, p, k)`, `markov(n, p, k)`, `chebyshev(n, p, k)` (the two-sided bound $\frac{\mathrm{Var}}{(k - np)^2}$ for $k$ above the mean) and `chernoff(n, p, k)` with the optimal $t$. Hidden tests: for $n = 100$, $p = \frac12$, $k = 75$, `exact_tail` gives $2.818141017 \times 10^{-7}$ to 9 significant figures, `chernoff` gives $\left(\frac{16}{27}\right)^{25}$, `markov` gives $\frac23$ and `chebyshev` 0.04; every bound is at least the exact tail on 50 seeded cases; `chernoff` with $k$ at or below the mean raises `ValueError`.

- *Example A (long-form, d4).* Prove Jensen's inequality $E[g(X)] \ge g(E[X])$ for convex $g$ (with $E\lvert X \rvert$ and $E\lvert g(X) \rvert$ finite), and use it to prove that the geometric mean of positive numbers is at most their arithmetic mean.
  *Model answer.* (1) Let $\mu = E[X]$. A convex function lies above a supporting line at $\mu$: there is a slope $c$ with $g(x) \ge g(\mu) + c(x - \mu)$ for every $x$. (2) Put $x = X$ and take expectations: $E[g(X)] \ge g(\mu) + c(E[X] - \mu) = g(\mu)$. (3) For AM-GM let $X$ take the values $a_1, \dots, a_n > 0$, each with probability $\frac1n$, and let $g = -\ln$, which is convex. (4) Jensen: $-\frac1n\sum_i\ln a_i \ge -\ln\left(\frac1n\sum_ia_i\right)$. (5) Multiply by $-1$ and exponentiate: $\left(\prod_ia_i\right)^{1/n} \le \frac1n\sum_ia_i$.
  *Rubric (10).* 3: the supporting line (line 1), the creative step. 2: taking expectations (line 2). 2: the choice of $X$ and $g$ (line 3). 2: applying Jensen (line 4). 1: conclusion (line 5).
- *Example B (d5).* $X \sim \mathrm{Bin}\left(n, \frac12\right)$. Use the Chernoff method to prove $P\left(X \ge \frac{3n}4\right) \le \left(\frac{16}{27}\right)^{n/4}$, and compare with the exact value for $n = 100$.
  *Answer.* For $t > 0$, $P(X \ge a) \le e^{-ta}E\left[e^{tX}\right] = e^{-ta}\left(\frac{1 + e^t}2\right)^n$. With $a = \frac{3n}4$, minimise $h(t) = -\frac{3t}4 + \ln\frac{1 + e^t}2$: $h'(t) = -\frac34 + \frac{e^t}{1 + e^t} = 0$ gives $e^t = 3$. The bound becomes $3^{-3n/4}\left(\frac42\right)^n = \left(\frac{2^4}{3^3}\right)^{n/4} = \left(\frac{16}{27}\right)^{n/4}$. For $n = 100$ this is about $2.1 \times 10^{-6}$; the exact tail is about $2.8 \times 10^{-7}$, and Chebyshev gives only 0.04. Decision point: the free parameter $t$ is chosen after the bound is written down; choosing it to minimise the bound is what makes Chernoff exponential.

**J6 Laws of large numbers and the central limit theorem** (`set.joint.limits`). 10 items (4 d3, 4 d4, 2 d5). Long-form: the weak law. Code drill `drl.prob.normal-approx` (d3): `binom_tail(n, p, k)` exactly, summing in logarithms, and `normal_tail(n, p, k, correction=True)` with `math.erf`. Hidden tests: for $n = 1{,}000$, $p = \frac{18}{38}$, $k = 501$: exact 0.0447959 within $10^{-6}$, corrected 0.0447224, uncorrected 0.0418151; $k = 0$ gives 1; $p$ outside $[0, 1]$ raises `ValueError`. Known-wrong: a direct product of factorials overflows and fails the first test.

- *Example A (long-form, d4).* Prove the weak law of large numbers for independent draws with mean $\mu$ and finite variance $\sigma^2$, and say what it does not claim.
  *Model answer.* (1) $\bar X_n = \frac1n\sum_iX_i$ has $E[\bar X_n] = \mu$ by linearity. (2) By independence, $\mathrm{Var}(\bar X_n) = \frac{\sigma^2}n$. (3) Chebyshev: $P(\lvert\bar X_n - \mu\rvert \ge \varepsilon) \le \frac{\sigma^2}{n\varepsilon^2}$. (4) For fixed $\varepsilon > 0$ the bound tends to 0, so $\bar X_n \to \mu$ in probability. (5) It does not say the sum's error $\sum_iX_i - n\mu$ shrinks (its spread grows like $\sqrt n$), nor that later draws make up for earlier ones, nor that every path converges (that is the strong law).
  *Rubric (10).* 1: mean (line 1). 2: variance using independence (line 2). 3: Chebyshev applied (line 3). 2: the limit stated correctly (line 4). 2: what it does not claim (line 5).
- *Example B (d5).* A trader takes independent £1 bets that win with probability 0.505 and lose with probability 0.495. Roughly how many bets does she need for a 95% chance of being in profit?
  *Answer.* Each bet has mean 0.01 and standard deviation $\sqrt{1 - 0.01^2} \approx 1$. After $n$ bets the profit has mean $0.01n$ and standard deviation about $\sqrt n$. By the central limit theorem $P(\text{profit} > 0) \approx \Phi\left(\frac{0.01n}{\sqrt n}\right) = \Phi(0.01\sqrt n)$, which reaches 0.95 when $0.01\sqrt n = 1.645$: $n \approx 27{,}000$. Decision point: the edge grows like $n$ and the noise like $\sqrt n$; their ratio, not the edge alone, sets the horizon. (The exact binomial chance wobbles around 0.95 near this $n$ because ties are possible only for even $n$.)

**Z1 Markov chains** (`set.markov.chains`). 10 items (4 d3, 4 d4, 2 d5). Long-form: detailed balance implies stationarity. Code drill `drl.prob.stationary` (d4): `stationary(P)` by exact Gaussian elimination in `Fraction`s. Hidden tests: the bull-bear chain gives $\left(\frac34, \frac14\right)$; Ehrenfest with 4 balls gives $\frac1{16}(1, 4, 6, 4, 1)$; a matrix whose rows do not sum to 1 raises `ValueError`; a chain with two closed classes raises `ValueError` ("not unique").

- *Example A (long-form, d4).* Prove that if a probability vector $\pi$ satisfies $\pi_ip_{ij} = \pi_jp_{ji}$ for all $i, j$, then $\pi$ is stationary; and show that every birth-death chain with positive up and down probabilities has such a $\pi$.
  *Model answer.* (1) $(\pi P)_j = \sum_i\pi_ip_{ij} = \sum_i\pi_jp_{ji} = \pi_j\sum_ip_{ji} = \pi_j$, by detailed balance and because row $j$ sums to 1. (2) So $\pi P = \pi$. (3) A birth-death chain on $\{0, \dots, N\}$ moves only to neighbours: $p_{i,i+1} = b_i > 0$ and $p_{i+1,i} = d_{i+1} > 0$. (4) Detailed balance constrains only neighbours: $\pi_{i+1} = \pi_i\frac{b_i}{d_{i+1}}$, so $\pi_i = \pi_0\prod_{k=0}^{i-1}\frac{b_k}{d_{k+1}}$. (5) Choose $\pi_0$ so the entries sum to 1; all are positive. (6) Pairs that are not neighbours satisfy detailed balance trivially, both sides being 0.
  *Rubric (10).* 3: the computation (line 1). 1: conclusion (line 2). 1: the chain (line 3). 3: the recursion (line 4). 1: normalising (line 5). 1: non-neighbours (line 6).
- *Example B (d5).* A knight moves at random on an empty $8 \times 8$ board, choosing uniformly among its legal moves. Starting in a corner, find the expected number of moves until it first returns to that corner.
  *Answer.* The knight performs a random walk on the graph whose vertices are squares and whose edges are knight moves. Such a walk has stationary distribution $\pi_v = \frac{\deg(v)}{\sum_u\deg(u)}$: detailed balance holds because $\pi_up_{uv} = \frac{\deg(u)}D \cdot \frac1{\deg(u)} = \frac1D$ is symmetric in $u$ and $v$. Counting square by square, the degrees sum to 336 (twice the 168 edges), and a corner has degree 2. The walk is irreducible, so the expected return time is $\frac1{\pi_{\text{corner}}} = \frac{336}2 = 168$. Decision point: 64 first-step equations is the naive route; the degree formula and the return-time identity $\frac1{\pi_i}$ collapse it to one count.

**Z2 Markov chains at work** (`set.markov.applied`). 10 items (4 d3, 4 d4, 2 d5). Long-form: what $N = (I - Q)^{-1}$ counts. Code drill `drl.prob.absorbing` (d4): `absorb(Q, R)` returns $B = NR$ and $\mathbf t = N\mathbf 1$ exactly. Hidden tests: the loan chain of lesson 131 gives $B = \begin{pmatrix}\frac13 & \frac23\\ \frac23 & \frac13\end{pmatrix}$ (columns default and repaid) and $\mathbf t = \left(\frac{130}9, \frac{80}9\right)$; the fair gambler's ruin with $N = 4$ gives winning chances $\frac14, \frac12, \frac34$ and durations 3, 4, 3; a singular $I - Q$ raises `ValueError`.

- *Example A (long-form, d4).* For an absorbing chain with transient block $Q$ and transient-to-absorbing block $R$, show that $N = (I - Q)^{-1} = \sum_{n \ge 0}Q^n$, that $N_{ij}$ is the expected number of visits to $j$ starting from $i$, and that $B = NR$ gives the absorption probabilities.
  *Model answer.* (1) $(Q^n)_{ij}$ is the chance of being at transient state $j$ after $n$ steps from $i$, the paths staying among transient states. (2) The number of visits to $j$ is $\sum_{n \ge 0}I_{\{X_n = j\}}$, so by linearity its expectation is $\sum_n(Q^n)_{ij}$. (3) The series converges: from every transient state, absorption happens with positive probability within a fixed number of steps, so $Q^n \to 0$ geometrically. (4) $(I - Q)\sum_{n=0}^mQ^n = I - Q^{m+1} \to I$, so the sum is $(I - Q)^{-1}$. (5) Absorption in state $k$ happens by a single jump from some transient $j$, and the walk is absorbed only once, so $P(\text{absorbed in } k \mid X_0 = i) = \sum_j(\text{expected visits to } j)R_{jk} = (NR)_{ik}$.
  *Rubric (10).* 1: the meaning of $Q^n$ (line 1). 3: visits by linearity (line 2). 2: convergence (line 3). 2: the inverse (line 4). 2: absorption (line 5).
- *Example B (d5).* A fair coin is tossed repeatedly. Find the expected number of tosses until HTH appears, and the probability that HHT appears before HTH.
  *Answer.* For HTH, with states "start", H and HT: $e_0 = 1 + \frac12e_H + \frac12e_0$, $e_H = 1 + \frac12e_H + \frac12e_{HT}$, $e_{HT} = 1 + \frac12e_0$; solving gives $e_0 = 10$. For the race, let $x$ be the chance that HHT wins from state H and $y$ from state HT. From H, a head gives HH, after which HHT must win (heads keep HH, and the first tail completes HHT), and a tail gives HT: $x = \frac12 + \frac12y$. From HT, a head completes HTH and a tail returns to the start, from which the walk must reach H again: $y = \frac12 \cdot 0 + \frac12x$. So $x = \frac12 + \frac14x$, $x = \frac23$, and the chance from the start is also $\frac23$. Decision point: HHT takes 8 tosses on average and HTH 10, but a race is not decided by comparing averages; it needs the joint chain.

**Z3 Random walks** (`set.markov.walks`). 10 items (4 d3, 4 d4, 2 d5). Long-form: the ballot theorem by reflection. Code drill `drl.prob.lattice-paths` (d4): `count_paths(n, a, b, avoid=None, floor=None)` by dynamic programming over positions. Hidden tests: $(10, 2, 4)$ gives 210, and 165 when avoiding 0; the ballot count for 6 votes against 4 gives a share of $\frac15$; paths of length 10 from 0 to 0 never below 0 number 42; the share of 10-step walks whose maximum reaches 3 is $\frac{11}{32}$; a parity mismatch gives 0.

- *Example A (long-form, d4).* Prove the ballot theorem: if A receives $a$ votes and B receives $b$ with $a > b$, counted in a uniformly random order, then A is strictly ahead throughout the count with probability $\frac{a - b}{a + b}$.
  *Model answer.* (1) A counting order is a lattice path from height 0 with $a$ up-steps (A) and $b$ down-steps (B), ending at $a - b > 0$; all $\binom{a + b}a$ paths are equally likely. (2) A is strictly ahead throughout exactly when the path stays above 0 after time 0; such a path starts with an up-step. (3) Bad paths are those that are at 0 at some time after time 0. Every path starting with a down-step is bad (it is below 0 at time 1, and must cross 0 to end above it); there are $\binom{a + b - 1}a$ of them. (4) Bad paths starting with an up-step: reflect the part before their first return to 0. This maps them one to one onto the paths starting with a down-step, so there are also $\binom{a + b - 1}a$. (5) Good paths: $\binom{a + b}a - 2\binom{a + b - 1}a$. (6) Dividing: $1 - 2\frac{\binom{a + b - 1}a}{\binom{a + b}a} = 1 - \frac{2b}{a + b} = \frac{a - b}{a + b}$.
  *Rubric (10).* 1: paths equally likely (line 1). 1: the event as a path condition (line 2). 2: down-first paths (line 3). 3: the reflection bijection (line 4), the creative step. 1: good paths (line 5). 2: the ratio (line 6).
- *Example B (d5).* A fair $\pm1$ walk starts at 0. Find the probability that within 10 steps it reaches height 3.
  *Answer.* Let $M_{10}$ be the maximum. Reflecting each path after its first visit to 3 pairs the paths that reach 3 and end below 3 with the paths that end above 3, so $P(M_{10} \ge 3) = P(S_{10} \ge 3) + P(S_{10} > 3)$. $S_{10}$ is even, so both terms equal $P(S_{10} \ge 4) = \frac{\binom{10}7 + \binom{10}8 + \binom{10}9 + \binom{10}{10}}{1{,}024} = \frac{176}{1{,}024}$. The answer is $\frac{352}{1{,}024} = \frac{11}{32} \approx 0.344$. Decision point: the maximum looks like a question about whole paths; reflection turns it into the distribution of the endpoint, which is binomial.

**Z4 Fair games and stopping times** (`set.markov.martingales`). 8 items (3 d3, 3 d4, 2 d5). Long-form: ruin with drift by a martingale. Code drill `drl.prob.abracadabra` (d3): `expected_wait(pattern, m)` sums $m^k$ over every $k$ for which the first $k$ letters equal the last $k$. Hidden tests: `('HTH', 2)` gives 10; `('HHT', 2)` gives 8; `('HH', 2)` gives 6; `('ABRACADABRA', 26)` gives $26^{11} + 26^4 + 26 = 3{,}670{,}344{,}487{,}444{,}778$; an empty pattern raises `ValueError`. Known-wrong: counting only the full overlap returns 8 for HTH and fails the first test.

- *Example A (long-form, d4).* A walk moves $+1$ with probability $p \ne \frac12$ and $-1$ with probability $q = 1 - p$, starting at $i$, and stops at 0 or $N$. Using a martingale and optional stopping, find the probability of reaching $N$.
  *Model answer.* (1) Let $M_n = \left(\frac qp\right)^{S_n}$. (2) $E[M_{n+1} \mid S_0, \dots, S_n] = \left(\frac qp\right)^{S_n}\left(p\,\frac qp + q\,\frac pq\right) = M_n(q + p) = M_n$: a martingale. (3) The stopping time $T$ (the first visit to 0 or $N$) has $E[T] < \infty$, and before $T$ the martingale stays between $\min\left(1, \left(\frac qp\right)^N\right)$ and $\max\left(1, \left(\frac qp\right)^N\right)$, so optional stopping applies: $E[M_T] = M_0 = \left(\frac qp\right)^i$. (4) $M_T$ equals $\left(\frac qp\right)^N$ with probability $w$ (reaching $N$) and 1 otherwise: $w\left(\frac qp\right)^N + (1 - w) = \left(\frac qp\right)^i$. (5) So $w = \frac{1 - (q/p)^i}{1 - (q/p)^N}$, lesson 43's answer. (6) $S_n$ itself is not a martingale when $p \ne q$, which is why an exponential of it is needed.
  *Rubric (10).* 2: the martingale chosen (line 1), the creative step. 2: its verification (line 2). 2: optional stopping with its conditions (line 3). 2: the equation (line 4). 1: the solution (line 5). 1: why not $S_n$ (line 6).
- *Example B (d5).* In example A, with $i = 3$, $N = 5$ and $p = 0.4$, find the expected duration.
  *Answer.* $S_n - (p - q)n$ is a martingale, so $E[S_T] = i + (p - q)E[T]$. From example A, $w = \frac{76}{211}$, so $E[S_T] = 5w = \frac{380}{211}$. Then $E[T] = \frac{E[S_T] - i}{p - q} = \frac{380/211 - 3}{-0.2} = \frac{-253/211}{-0.2} = \frac{1{,}265}{211} \approx 6.00$. Decision point: a second martingale, the walk minus its drift, turns the duration into the mean of the endpoint, which the first martingale already gave.

**Z4b Continuous-time chains** (`set.markov.continuous-chains`). 8 items (3 d3, 3 d4, 2 d5). Long-form: time shares from the jump chain. Code drill `drl.prob.ctmc` (d4): `ctmc_stationary(Q)` solves $\pi Q = 0$ with $\sum_i\pi_i = 1$ by exact Gaussian elimination in `Fraction`s, and `ctmc_hitting(Q, target)` returns the expected time to reach `target` from every other state. Hidden tests: lesson 157's feed gives $\left(\frac8{13}, \frac4{13}, \frac1{13}\right)$; its times to Down are 3 from Live and $\frac52$ from Lagging; the two-state link with rates 2 and 6 gives $\left(\frac34, \frac14\right)$; example B's desk gives $\pi_4 = \frac{81}{653}$; a matrix whose rows do not sum to 0, or with a negative rate off the diagonal, raises `ValueError`. Known-wrong: solving the jump chain returns $\left(\frac49, \frac49, \frac19\right)$ and fails the first test; hitting times that charge each jump one unit of time (1 in place of the holding term $\frac1{q_i}$) return the jump counts 8 and 7 and fail the second.

- *Example A (long-form, d4).* A continuous-time chain on finitely many states has holding rates $q_i > 0$, and its jump chain (transition probabilities $\tilde p_{ij} = \frac{q_{ij}}{q_i}$ for $j \ne i$, and $\tilde p_{ii} = 0$) has stationary distribution $\tilde\pi$. Show that $\pi_i = \frac{\tilde\pi_i/q_i}{\sum_k\tilde\pi_k/q_k}$ satisfies $\pi Q = 0$, explain why it gives the long-run shares of time, and check it on lesson 157's feed.
  *Model answer.* (1) The generator has $Q_{ij} = q_{ij}$ for $j \ne i$ and $Q_{jj} = -q_j$, so $(\pi Q)_j = \sum_{i \ne j}\pi_iq_{ij} - \pi_jq_j$. (2) Write $\pi_i = c\,\frac{\tilde\pi_i}{q_i}$ with $c = \left(\sum_k\frac{\tilde\pi_k}{q_k}\right)^{-1}$. (3) Then $\sum_{i \ne j}\pi_iq_{ij} = c\sum_{i \ne j}\tilde\pi_i\frac{q_{ij}}{q_i} = c\sum_i\tilde\pi_i\tilde p_{ij} = c\,\tilde\pi_j$, using $\tilde p_{jj} = 0$ and $\tilde\pi\tilde P = \tilde\pi$. (4) Also $\pi_jq_j = c\,\tilde\pi_j$, so $(\pi Q)_j = 0$ for every $j$, and the $\pi_i$ are positive and sum to 1 by the choice of $c$. (5) Over many jumps a share $\tilde\pi_i$ of the visits are to $i$ (lesson 126), and each lasts $\frac1{q_i}$ on average, so the time spent in $i$ is proportional to $\frac{\tilde\pi_i}{q_i}$ (the long-run averaging is stated, not proved). (6) Check: the feed's jump chain has $\tilde\pi = \left(\frac49, \frac49, \frac19\right)$ and $q = (2, 4, 4)$, so $\pi \propto \left(\frac29, \frac19, \frac1{36}\right) \propto (8, 4, 1)$ and $\pi = \left(\frac8{13}, \frac4{13}, \frac1{13}\right)$, lesson 159's answer.
  *Rubric (10).* 2: the $j$th entry of $\pi Q$ (line 1). 1: the candidate and its constant (line 2). 3: the inflow turned into the jump chain's balance, with $\tilde p_{jj} = 0$ named (line 3), the creative step. 1: the conclusion (line 4). 2: the time-share reading (line 5). 1: the check (line 6).
- *Example B (d5).* A help desk has two staff and room for two callers on hold. Calls arrive as a Poisson process at rate 3 an hour; each member of staff finishes a call at rate 2 an hour; a caller who finds both staff busy and both hold places taken is turned away. In the long run, what share of callers is turned away?
  *Answer.* The number of callers present, 0 to 4, is a birth-death chain with birth rate 3 in states 0 to 3 and death rates $\mu_1 = 2$ and $\mu_2 = \mu_3 = \mu_4 = 4$, because at most two calls are served at once. Cut balance gives $\pi_n \propto 1, \frac32, \frac98, \frac{27}{32}, \frac{81}{128}$, which sum to $\frac{653}{128}$, so $\pi_4 = \frac{81}{653} \approx 0.124$. Calls arrive as a Poisson process independent of the desk's past, so an arriving caller finds the desk full with the long-run probability $\pi_4$ (stated: Poisson arrivals see time averages): about 12.4% are turned away. Decision point: the death rate is $2\min(n, 2)$, not $2n$, because callers on hold are not being served; $2n$ would give $\frac{27}{563} \approx 0.048$, as if four staff were on duty.

**Z4c Brownian motion** (`set.markov.brownian`). 10 items (4 d3, 4 d4, 2 d5). Long-form: the reflection principle and the first-passage time. Code drill `drl.prob.brownian-exit` (d3): `exit_drift(mu, sigma, a, b)` returns the chance that $X_t = \mu t + \sigma B_t$ from 0 reaches $a$ before $-b$ and the expected exit time, using the driftless formulas when `mu == 0`; `max_tail(a, t)` returns $P(M_t \ge a)$ with `math.erfc`. Hidden tests: `exit_drift(0, 1, 3, 2)` gives $(0.4, 6.0)$; `exit_drift(0, 2, 3, 2)` gives $(0.4, 1.5)$; `exit_drift(0.5, 1, 2, 2)` gives $\frac1{1 + e^{-2}}$ and $4\tanh 1$ to relative tolerance $10^{-9}$; `exit_drift(-0.5, 1, 2, 2)` gives $\frac{e^{-2}}{1 + e^{-2}}$ and the same time; `max_tail(3, 4)` is within relative $10^{-9}$ of 0.1336144025; a non-positive `sigma`, `a`, `b` or `t` raises `ValueError`. Known-wrong: $\theta = \frac\mu{\sigma^2}$ returns 0.7311 for the third test and fails it; `max_tail` without the factor 2 fails the fifth.

- *Example A (long-form, d4).* Let $M_t = \max_{s \le t}B_s$ and let $T_a$ be the first time Brownian motion reaches $a > 0$. Show that $P(M_t \ge a) = 2P(B_t \ge a)$, find the density of $T_a$, and show that $E[T_a] = \infty$.
  *Model answer.* (1) Paths are continuous, so $\{M_t \ge a\} = \{T_a \le t\}$. (2) Split: $P(T_a \le t) = P(T_a \le t, B_t \ge a) + P(T_a \le t, B_t < a)$. (3) $B_t \ge a$ forces $T_a \le t$, so the first term is $P(B_t \ge a)$. (4) Reflect each path after $T_a$: $\tilde B_s = 2a - B_s$ for $s \ge T_a$. After $T_a$ the path starts afresh from $a$ and is symmetric about it (the strong Markov property, stated), so the reflection maps paths with $T_a \le t$ and $B_t < a$ one to one onto paths with $T_a \le t$ and $B_t > a$, with equal probability: the second term is $P(B_t > a) = P(B_t \ge a)$. (5) Hence $P(T_a \le t) = 2P(B_t \ge a) = 2\left(1 - \Phi\left(\frac a{\sqrt t}\right)\right)$. (6) Differentiating, $f_{T_a}(t) = \frac a{\sqrt{2\pi}}t^{-3/2}e^{-a^2/(2t)}$ for $t > 0$. (7) For large $t$ the density is about $\frac a{\sqrt{2\pi}}t^{-3/2}$, so $tf_{T_a}(t)$ is about a constant times $t^{-1/2}$, whose integral diverges: $E[T_a] = \infty$, although $P(T_a \le t) \to 1$.
  *Rubric (10).* 1: the event identity (line 1). 1: the split (line 2). 1: the first term (line 3). 3: the reflection, with the restart at $T_a$ named (line 4), the creative step. 1: the result (line 5). 2: the density (line 6). 1: the infinite mean (line 7).
- *Example B (d5).* A driftless P&L with variance 1 a day starts at 0 and is closed when it first reaches $+2$ or $-2$. Its expected life is 4 days (lesson 164). Find the variance of its life.
  *Answer.* Expand the martingale $e^{\theta B_t - \theta^2t/2}$ in powers of $\theta$. It is a martingale for every $\theta$, so each coefficient is a martingale, and 24 times the $\theta^4$ coefficient is $B_t^4 - 6tB_t^2 + 3t^2$. The exit time $T$ of a bounded interval has finite moments of every order, and before $T$ this martingale is bounded by a polynomial in $T$, so optional stopping applies (stated): $E\left[B_T^4 - 6TB_T^2 + 3T^2\right] = 0$. Since $\lvert B_T \rvert = 2$, this reads $16 - 24E[T] + 3E[T^2] = 0$, so $E[T^2] = \frac{24 \times 4 - 16}3 = \frac{80}3$ and $\mathrm{Var}(T) = \frac{80}3 - 16 = \frac{32}3 \approx 10.7$: a standard deviation of about 3.3 days on a mean of 4. Decision point: no list of known martingales holds $B_t^4 - 6tB_t^2 + 3t^2$; reading the exponential martingale's $\theta^4$ coefficient produces it, the same way $\theta^2$ produces lesson 164's $B_t^2 - t$.

**Z5 Geometric probability** (`set.markov.geometric`). 8 items (3 d3, 3 d4, 2 d5). Long-form: Buffon's needle. Code drill `drl.prob.geo-estimate` (d3): `estimate(indicator, dims, n, seed)` returns a Monte Carlo estimate and its standard error. Hidden tests: the broken-stick triangle with $n = 200{,}000$ and seed 11 lies within 4 standard errors of $\frac14$; "the product is below $\frac12$" lies within 4 standard errors of $\frac12 + \frac12\ln 2$; the reported standard error equals $\sqrt{\hat p(1 - \hat p)/n}$; the same seed gives the same estimate.

- *Example A (long-form, d4).* A needle of length $\ell$ is dropped on a floor ruled with parallel lines a distance $d \ge \ell$ apart. Show that it crosses a line with probability $\frac{2\ell}{\pi d}$.
  *Model answer.* (1) Describe a drop by $X$, the distance from the needle's centre to the nearest line, and $\Theta$, the acute angle between the needle and the lines. (2) By symmetry $X \sim \mathrm{Unif}\left(0, \frac d2\right)$ and $\Theta \sim \mathrm{Unif}\left(0, \frac\pi2\right)$, independent. (3) The needle crosses the nearest line exactly when $X \le \frac\ell2\sin\Theta$, and $d \ge \ell$ means it cannot reach any other line. (4) $P = \int_0^{\pi/2}\int_0^{(\ell/2)\sin\theta}\frac2d \cdot \frac2\pi\,dx\,d\theta = \frac4{\pi d} \cdot \frac\ell2\int_0^{\pi/2}\sin\theta\,d\theta = \frac{2\ell}{\pi d}$. (5) Dropping many needles and counting crossings estimates $\pi$, but the error shrinks only like $\frac1{\sqrt n}$.
  *Rubric (10).* 2: the coordinates (line 1). 2: their joint distribution (line 2). 3: the crossing condition (line 3). 2: the integral (line 4). 1: the remark (line 5).
- *Example B (d5).* A stick of length 1 is broken at two independent uniform points. Find the expected length of the shortest piece.
  *Answer.* For $x < \frac13$, all three pieces exceed $x$ exactly when the two cuts leave a gap of at least $x$ at both ends and between them; this squeezes the uniform pair of cuts into a triangle scaled by $1 - 3x$, so $P(\min > x) = (1 - 3x)^2$. By the tail formula for non-negative variables (lesson 70), $E[\min] = \int_0^{1/3}(1 - 3x)^2\,dx = \frac19$. Decision point: the tail formula turns "expected minimum" into one area and avoids the joint distribution of the three pieces. (The middle piece averages $\frac5{18}$ and the longest $\frac{11}{18}$.)

**Z6 Games, stopping and betting** (`set.markov.games`). 10 items (4 d3, 4 d4, 2 d5). Long-form: the secretary problem. Code drill `drl.prob.stopping` (d3): `dice_game_value(rolls, sides=6)` by backward induction, and `secretary(n)` returning the best number to reject and its success chance. Hidden tests: `dice_game_value(1) == Fraction(7, 2)`, `dice_game_value(2) == Fraction(17, 4)`, `dice_game_value(3) == Fraction(14, 3)`; `secretary(100)` gives 37 with a chance within $10^{-12}$ of 0.371042778712643; `secretary(1)` gives `(0, 1)`; `rolls < 1` raises `ValueError`.

- *Example A (long-form, d4).* $n$ candidates arrive in random order. You reject the first $r \ge 1$ and then accept the first candidate better than all before. Show that the chance of choosing the best is $\frac rn\sum_{k=r+1}^n\frac1{k - 1}$, and that the best $r$ is near $\frac ne$ with success chance near $\frac1e$.
  *Model answer.* (1) Split on the position $k$ of the best candidate, each position having probability $\frac1n$. (2) If $k \le r$ the best is rejected. (3) If $k > r$ you take the best exactly when nobody in positions $r + 1$ to $k - 1$ is accepted, that is when the best of the first $k - 1$ candidates sits among the first $r$; by symmetry this has probability $\frac r{k - 1}$. (4) So the chance is $\sum_{k=r+1}^n\frac1n \cdot \frac r{k - 1}$. (5) For large $n$ with $r = xn$, it is about $x\int_x^1\frac{dt}t = -x\ln x$. (6) Maximising: $-\ln x - 1 = 0$ gives $x = \frac1e$, with chance $\frac1e \approx 0.368$. For $n = 100$ the best $r$ is 37, with chance about 0.371.
  *Rubric (10).* 1: conditioning on the best's position (line 1). 1: an early best (line 2). 3: the symmetry argument (line 3). 1: the sum (line 4). 2: the integral approximation (line 5). 2: the optimum (line 6).
- *Example B (d5).* An even-money bet wins with probability 0.55. Find the Kelly fraction and the growth rate per bet, and the growth rate when betting twice the Kelly fraction.
  *Answer.* Growth per bet is $g(f) = 0.55\ln(1 + f) + 0.45\ln(1 - f)$, and $g'(f) = \frac{0.55}{1 + f} - \frac{0.45}{1 - f} = 0$ gives $f^* = 0.10$. Then $g(0.1) \approx 0.00501$ per bet. At $f = 0.2$, $g \approx -0.00014$, slightly negative. Decision point: doubling the stake does not double the growth, it removes it, because the logarithm punishes a loss more than it rewards a gain of the same size.

### 3.2 The Probability Lab project (1.5, 16 hours)

A repository the learner builds on their own machine or the web app, with pytest tests and a one-page results note, as every project from 1.2 onwards (curriculum 1.2 exit; Stage 2 rules). It uses NumPy, SciPy and matplotlib, so it runs on the web app (Pyodide) or locally, not in the Artifact. Each part has a checklist with paste-verify of `pytest -q` and its figures (`setup-checklist` project mode, ASSESSMENT.md 2.9).

| Part | Hours | After level | Builds | Tests and figures |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| 1. Exact and simulated | 3.5 | N4 | An exact enumerator (sample space generator and event predicate to a `Fraction`) and a seeded simulator with standard errors; ten results from course N reproduced both ways (Galileo, de Méré, Newton-Pepys, matching, occupancy, the 100 prisoners) | Enumeration equals each closed form exactly; each simulation lies within 4 standard errors; standard error against trials on log-log axes with slope $-\frac12$ |
| 2. Bayes and classification | 4.0 | H4 | A Bayesian updater over finite hypotheses; a naive Bayes text classifier on a seeded synthetic corpus with add-one smoothing; a calibration plot (predicted against observed rates in ten bins) | Posteriors equal hand-computed values; order invariance; no zero probabilities after smoothing; calibration within the plotted bands on held-out data |
| 3. Distributions from scratch | 4.0 | V7 | Samplers by inverse transform and by rejection for six distributions; a Poisson process simulator with merging and thinning; the inspection paradox measured | Kolmogorov-Smirnov distance against `scipy.stats` below the 1% critical value for $10^5$ draws; sample moments within 4 standard errors; the measured gap length within 4 standard errors of $\frac2\lambda$ |
| 4. Chains and limits | 4.5 | Z2 | A Markov chain toolkit (stationary distribution by a linear solve and by an eigenvector, absorption, simulation); PageRank on a seeded 50-node graph; a Metropolis sampler for a Beta posterior with an effective sample size; a central limit theorem explorer comparing exact convolutions with the normal | The two stationary methods agree to $10^{-12}$; PageRank equals power iteration to $10^{-10}$; the Metropolis sample's mean within 4 effective standard errors of the exact posterior mean; the largest CDF gap shrinks roughly like $\frac1{\sqrt n}$ |

The results note answers four questions in plain words: when a simulation can and cannot replace a proof; why the naive Bayes classifier works despite its false assumption; which sampler you would use for each distribution and why; how many Metropolis draws are worth one independent draw for your target, and what made the number large or small.
